Starting with the insulator phase, after U(1)c-SW-SSB (condensing ˜ϕ), the system enters the normal fluid phase with charge density diffusion; after U(1)c-ST-SSB (condensing ϕ), the system enters the superfluid phase with Goldstone mode. The same phase diagram can also be produced starting with the superfluid phase, which is the strongly symmetry phase of U(1)v. After the U(1)v-SW-SSB (condensing ˜θ), the system enters the normal fluid, and after U(1)v-ST-SSB (condensing θ), the system enters the insulator phase of rotor.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work establishes a particle-vortex duality for 2D boson/rotor models undergoing dissipative dynamics. By analyzing how symmetries break (SW-SSB vs. WT-SSB), the authors show that the hydrodynamics of charge density and vortices are mathematically equivalent descriptions. This provides a unified framework connecting matter wave physics to magnetohydrodynamic concepts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between charge conservation symmetries ($U(1)_c$) and dual vortex symmetries ($U(1)_e$), mediated by symmetry breaking, provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding coupled quantum field theories relevant to condensed matter systems like superconductors or superfluids.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum particle-vortex duality for emergent hydrodynamics in 2D boson/rotor systems, extending known dualities from equilibrium to non-equilibrium settings.

**Main result.** A robust duality is established between the hydrodynamic consequences of different symmetry breaking mechanisms (SW-SSB vs. WT-SSB) associated with charge ($U(1)_c$) and vortex ($U(1)_v$) symmetries, bridging matter and magnetohydrodynamics.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh path integral formalism to derive effective hydrodynamic equations from Lindbladian dissipative dynamics in various quantum phases.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D quantum boson/rotor model on a lattice, governed by both a Hamiltonian (quantum coherent) and Lindblad dissipative terms. The physics is analyzed across Mott insulator, Normal fluid, and Superfluid regimes.

**Key observables.** Charge density ($\mathbf{n}$), phase field ($\phi$), gauge fields ($\mathbf{E}, \mathbf{B}$), and the resulting hydrodynamic modes (e.g., propagating Goldstone mode).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($u_n, u_\phi$), dissipation rates ($\Gamma$), and symmetry breaking types (SW-SSB, WT-SSB).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on working to leading order in small parameters and often assumes finite length scales ($\xi$) for perturbative expansions.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize the complex relationship between symmetry structure ($U(1)_c$ vs $U(1)_e$), spontaneous symmetry breaking type, and resulting hydrodynamic modes across different phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the dynamics by first establishing the quantum framework (Keldysh action), then examining specific symmetry breaking scenarios (SW-SSB, WT-SSB) in different phases (Normal/Superfluid), culminating in the general statement of the particle-vortex duality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Equipped with the recently recognized symmetry structure of mixed states of matter and "strong-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking" (SW-SSB), we develop a quantum particle-vortex duality of emergent model-F and model-A hydrodynamics of 2d boson/rotor systems. This duality relation demonstrates that classical hydrodynamics of 2d bosons can be described in terms of the charge symmetry $U(1)_c$, but also equivalently in terms of the dual (emergent) 1-form symmetry $U(1)^{(1)}_e$, as well as $U(1)_v$ associated with the conservation of vortices. This duality provides a bridge between the hydrodynamics of matter and magnetohydrodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how external parametric driving induces Faraday patterns in dipolar quantum gases across different phases. It uses the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation to model the system, revealing a distinct crossover mechanism: surface modes dominate in the superfluid phase, transitioning through hybrid excitations near the supersolid boundary, and finally settling into bulk droplet patterns characteristic of the supersolid state.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores pattern formation and dynamical instabilities in strongly interacting quantum fluids using advanced mean-field theory, bridging concepts from superfluidity, dipolar interactions, and non-equilibrium dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Faraday instabilities, which manifest as spontaneous structure formation, in dipolar superfluid and supersolid quantum gases subjected to external parametric driving.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates a clear dynamical crossover from surface collective modes (superfluids) to hybrid surface-bulk excitations near the transition, culminating in bulk density patterns characteristic of the supersolid regime.

**Method.** The dynamics are simulated using the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation (eGPE), and resonance spectra are analyzed by deriving an appropriate Mathieu equation.

**Model / system.** The system models three-dimensional trapped dipolar quantum gases, such as $^{164}	ext{Dy}$ atoms. The physics is governed by the competition between short-range interactions, long-range anisotropic dipole-dipole interactions (DDI), and external parametric driving.

**Key observables.** Faraday patterns (standing-wave density modulations), resonance frequencies, sub-harmonic response, and the characterization of surface vs. bulk excitations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strength of contact vs. dipolar interactions ($\epsilon_{dd}$), the trap aspect ratio, and the parameters governing the parametric driving protocol ($A$, $\omega_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are modeled using the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation (eGPE), which includes beyond mean-field Lee-Huang-Yang corrections in the local-density approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates distinct ground state phases (supersolid vs. superfluid) based on scattering length, while Figure 2 shows various surface density patterns ($\ell=2$ to $\ell=6$) induced by parametric resonance via scattering length modulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the system in different regimes: first addressing the superfluid phase where discrete polygonal surface modes are excited; then examining the transition region near the supersolid state, leading to hybrid excitations; and finally characterizing the bulk patterns that dominate deep within the supersolid regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate Faraday instabilities in three-dimensional parametrically driven trapped dipolar quantum gases within the framework of the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation. In the superfluid regime, periodic modulation of the short-range interactions induces the resonant excitation of discrete polygonal surface modes displaying sub-harmonic response. The resonance frequencies and the parametric windows of instability are accurately captured by an appropriate Mathieu equation, further corroborating our simulations. It is also shown that dipolar interactions in superfluids shift the resonance frequencies to lower values compared to their non-dipolar counterparts. Near the superfluid-to-supersolid transition, intrinsic density undulations associated with the softened roton mode accelerate pattern formation, yielding hybrid surface and bulk excitations. The bulk patterns prevail deeper in the supersolid regime, emerging from droplet collisions with the superfluid background. Our results reveal a crossover from surface collective modes to hybrid surface-bulk excitations and demonstrate how parametric driving dictates pattern formation in long-range interacting quantum fluids.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the chiral spin liquid phase in the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model using advanced quantum simulation methods. The authors demonstrate spin-charge separation by showing distinct real-space dynamics for spin and charge excitations. This work confirms the utility of NQS and PEPS as powerful tools for diagnosing topological order in strongly correlated electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it uses sophisticated numerical techniques (NQS/PEPS) to probe fractionalized excitations, a core topic in strongly correlated physics that often connects to emergent phenomena studied in AMO systems like Rydberg arrays or cold atom lattices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin-charge separation (fractionalization) within the context of a putative chiral spin liquid phase.

**Main result.** The authors provide direct real-space evidence for spin-charge separation by observing that spin density localizes on the edge while excess charge diffuses into the bulk during time evolution simulations.

**Method.** The research employs advanced quantum many-body techniques, specifically Variational Monte Carlo with Neural Quantum States (NQS) and Projected Entangled Pair States (PEPS), alongside real-time dynamics simulations.

**Model / system.** The model is the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model, which is relevant to moiré materials and hosts potential chiral spin liquid phases. The Hamiltonian includes hopping terms modulated by a magnetic flux ($\Phi = \pi/2$ per triangle) and an onsite Hubbard interaction ($U$).

**Key observables.** Spin Hall response ($\sigma^s_{xy}$), Charge Hall response ($\sigma_{xy}$), real-space spin density vs. charge density evolution, and two-electron binding energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the hopping amplitude ($t$), the onsite interaction strength ($U$), and the magnetic flux $\Phi$. The analysis focuses on regimes around $U \approx 11$ where a transition to CSL is expected.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that NQS and PEPS can accurately diagnose topological order, although pairing evidence diminishes rapidly with system size, limiting conclusions about superconductivity in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate gap closing/opening as a function of $U$ (Fig. 2(b)), show direct real-space separation of spin and charge densities (Fig. 2(c,d) & Fig. 4), and demonstrate quantized spin pumping response upon flux insertion (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model and methods, then analyzes gap behavior across different coupling regimes ($U$). It proceeds to provide direct evidence for SCS via real-space simulations and quantifies topological properties using charge/spin pumping measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent experiments in moiré materials have enabled the realization of a variety of exotic quantum phases. In this context, the Hofstadter-Hubbard model has been proposed as a possible setting for hosting chiral spin liquid. Concurrently, significant progress has been recently made in the computational methods for two-dimensional many-body fermion systems, which makes numerically studying this challenging model a real possibility in genuine 2D geometry. Motivated by these advances, we investigate the putative chiral spin liquid phase in the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model using variational Monte Carlo with neural quantum states (NQS) and projected entangled pair states (PEPS). We observe spin-charge separation directly in real space through numerical spin-pumping simulation and real-time spin and charge motion. In addition, in the context of anyonic superconductivity conjectured in this model, we find a positive two-electron binding energy on small systems, but it decreases below our numerical resolution as the system size increases. Our work demonstrates NQS and PEPS as powerful tools, capable of cross-checking each other, for diagnosing topological order and fractionalized excitations in strongly correlated electronic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes monitored random Clifford circuits by mapping their output states onto graph structures. Using this graph-theoretic approach, the authors derive analytical results for entanglement measures like GHZ content and identify an emergent, smaller unitary subsystem in the volume-law phase. This work successfully locates the critical point of the measurement-induced phase transition using mean-field theory on the underlying random graph ensemble.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, concrete link between random graph theory (a statistical mechanics tool) and non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, offering structural insights into how measurement processes constrain complex quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a graph-theoretic framework to directly characterize the typical output states of monitored random Clifford circuits, moving beyond traditional entanglement measures.

**Main result.** They found that in the volume-law phase, the output state is equivalent to a unitary circuit on an emergent dense subgraph ($G(N_{\text{sub}}, 1/2)$), and they analytically determined the critical point of the Measurement-Induced Phase Transition (MIPT) at $p_c = 0.1608$.

**Method.** The core method involves mapping the output stabilizer states to graph states characterized by an adjacency matrix ($\Gamma$), allowing for large-$N$ limit analysis and mean-field calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a monitored random Clifford circuit, which serves as a model for non-equilibrium quantum many-body dynamics. The state structure is analyzed using graph theory representations of stabilizer states.

**Key observables.** GHZ entanglement content ($\langle g_3 \rangle$), bipartite entanglement (entropy), and the structure/density of the emergent subgraph within the output state graph.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement probability ($p$) which controls the transition between volume-law and area-law entanglement regimes, and the system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-$N$ limit convergence to the Erdős–Rényi random graph ensemble $G(N, 1/2)$, and that local Clifford operations can be mapped onto graph complementation rules.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the circuit setup with measurements; Figure 1(b) shows the even-odd effect in GHZ content vs. measurement rate $p$; and Figure 1(c) depicts how edge correlations decay with distance on the graph.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the model and the limitations of previous entropy-based methods, then develops the graph-state framework to analyze output states, derives analytical results for GHZ content and subgraph structure in specific phases, and finally uses mean-field theory to locate the MIPT critical point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Monitored random Clifford circuit is a paradigmatic platform for exploring non-equilibrium quantum many-body dynamics using quantum-information methods. It is well-known for exhibiting a measurement-induced phase transition (MIPT) between volume-law and area-law phases of bipartite entanglement. In this Article, we develop a graph-state based framework that grants direct access to the typical output states of monitored random Clifford circuits. We first show that, in the large-$N$ limit, where $N$ denotes qubit number, the graph representations of random stabilizer states converge to the Erdős--Rényi random graph ensemble $G(N,1/2)$. This observation allows us to resolve the open problem of Greenberger--Horne--Zeilinger (GHZ) entanglement in random stabilizer states. We derive analytically the mean GHZ content, $\langle g_3\rangle=1.204$ for even $N$ and $1.325$ for odd $N$. For monitored one dimensional (1D) circuits in the volume-law phase, we uncover an emergent dense subgraph of the form $G(N_{\mathrm{sub}},1/2)$ in the output-state graphs. This implies that the output state of a monitored circuit is equivalent to an output of a unitary circuit on $N_{\mathrm{sub}}$ qubits, weakly perturbed by the remaining $N-N_{\mathrm{sub}}$ qubits carrying little entanglement. This result directly accounts for the quantum error-correcting capability of the volume-law phase. We further identify a clustering effect in the spatial distribution of the dense subgraph along the 1D qubit chain, and reproduce it with an infection-recovery toy model that exhibits a measurement-induced absorbing-state phase transition. Finally, we locate the critical point of the MIPT at $p_c = 0.1608$ through a mean-field argument on the graph, in excellent agreement with the numerical result $p_c\approx 0.16$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an advanced method for creating complex multi-qubit entangled states, such as GHZ and cluster states, using single photons on integrated optical chips. By employing high-dimensional encoding instead of direct multi-photon generation, the authors achieve high fidelity across up to 10 qubits. This work advances scalable photonic quantum information processing by demonstrating both state preparation and algorithmic execution (Grover's search).

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, scalable platform demonstration for photonic quantum computation by addressing the resource limitations inherent in multi-photon sources, which is highly relevant to experimental quantum optics and networking research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is generating high-fidelity multi-qubit graph states necessary for universal quantum computation using photonic systems, circumventing the low efficiency of traditional multi-photon sources.

**Main result.** The authors successfully generated and characterized high-fidelity GHZ states up to 10 qubits and a single-photon 4-qubit cluster state on an integrated chip. They also demonstrated Grover's search algorithm using this photonic resource.

**Method.** They propose replacing complex multi-photon preparation with resource-efficient single-photon operations involving high-dimensional encoding, routing, and layer-by-layer quantum measurement schemes.

**Model / system.** The experiment is performed on programmable photonic integrated circuits (silicon photonics) using pulsed lasers at 1550.12 nm. The system utilizes high-dimensional encoding via path degrees of freedom to encode multiple qubits within single photons.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F$), Entanglement Witness values ($\langle 	ext{W}_{Gm} angle$), and the identification probability for Grover's search.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The largest demonstrated state was a 10-qubit GHZ state, with fidelity reaching $0.961(0.003)$ for 4 qubits; cluster state fidelity reached $0.991(0.004)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes the feasibility of high-dimensional encoding and relies on quantum state tomography and entanglement witness measurements for verification.

**Figures summary.** Figures show measured fidelities for GHZ states across varying qubit numbers, entanglement witness values confirming genuine multipartite entanglement (violating classical bounds), and the identification probability achieved during a Grover search demonstration.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the conceptual framework of single-photon high-dimensional encoding, presents experimental results on generating and characterizing multi-qubit graph states (GHZ/cluster) using integrated photonics, and concludes with demonstrations of quantum algorithms like Grover's search.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic multi-qubit entanglement is key to optical quantum information processing, particularly universal quantum computing. Yet multi-photon sources suffer from low emission efficiency, making single-photon high-dimensional encoding an appealing alternative. Here we propose an explicit and resource-efficient high-dimensional encoding approach to achieve the target multi-qubit quantum state. The technically challenging preparation of multi-photon quantum states is replaced by single-photon operations involving high-dimensional expansion, routing, and multi-layered quantum measurement. Besides, each photon in the resource multi-photon quantum state can be used to encode multiple qubits in a distributed manner, and a larger entangled state will be constructed. We demonstrate this approach using programmable photonic integrated circuits, where multi-qubit graph states--including the Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger state and the cluster state--are generated and characterized. We additionally demonstrate the Grover search algorithm using the single-photon cluster state. Our findings unlock a novel route towards diverse entangled state generation with photons and advance large-scale and universal photonic quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the critical issue of signal degradation due to coupling losses when measuring quantum light states on photonic chips. The authors propose and experimentally demonstrate an 'on-chip quantum measurement' technique using cascaded silicon nitride micro-ring resonators acting as a squeezer followed by a parametric amplifier. This method successfully measures significant squeezing (4.6 dB) even with substantial external optical loss, establishing a scalable platform for integrated quantum sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial engineering solution to a major bottleneck in integrated quantum photonics—loss. By showing how amplification can circumvent loss limitations, it paves a practical path for scaling quantum sensors onto chips.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is that coupling losses between on-chip quantum optical components and off-chip detectors destroy fragile quantum correlations, limiting the observable squeezing.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a 4.6 dB quantum noise reduction despite exceeding 5 dB of chip-to-fiber coupling loss by implementing an on-chip measurement scheme.

**Method.** They utilized a two-stage system: one micro-ring resonator generates the squeezed state, and a second ring acts as a high-gain parametric amplifier (OPA) to measure this state before significant external loss occurs.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an integrated silicon nitride (SiN) photonic chip containing two cascaded micro-ring resonators. The system functions as a monolithic SU(1,1) interferometer when used for sensing.

**Key observables.** Quantum noise reduction (measured in dB), interference fringe visibility, and the measured squeezing level relative to vacuum noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chip-to-fiber coupling loss (>5 dB); observed quantum noise reduction (4.6 dB); high gain regime of the OPA ($G \gg 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurement is inherently loss-tolerant because the amplifier elevates the quantum noise floor above vacuum levels, making detection insensitive to downstream losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual schematic (Squeezer $ightarrow$ OPA), the device structure on SiN, and crucially, the measured quantum noise spectra comparing squeezed vs. vacuum states under varying pump phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of loss in chip-based quantum optics, proposes the parametric amplifier assisted measurement scheme (SU(1,1) interferometer), details the experimental implementation on SiN rings, and presents quantitative measurements demonstrating noise reduction despite high coupling losses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Integration of quantum optical technique on-chip is crucial for large scale applications of quantum technology, which were proven in a free space environment to be superior to the corresponding classical technology. Squeezed states of light can be used for enhancing the sensitivity of quantum sensors and for fault-tolerant quantum computing. Although chip-based squeezed light generation has advanced significantly, practical impact remains limited because coupling losses between the chip and off-chip detectors destroy delicate quantum correlations, restricting the amount of observed squeezing. Here, we overcome this limitation by implementing the idea of on-chip quantum measurement with the aid of a parametric amplifier and applying it to the squeezed state generated by a silicon nitride (SiN) microring resonator. In our scheme, two matched SiN micro-rings are sequentially constructed. The first ring generates a squeezed state, whereas the second ring acts as a high-gain parametric amplifier (PA) that measures the squeezed state before the light experiences significant off-chip loss. This architecture is inherently loss-tolerant: the amplifier elevates the quantum noise well above the vacuum level, making the measurement insensitive to downstream losses. We directly observe a quantum noise reduction of 4.6 dB from the first ring, despite a chip-to-fiber coupling loss exceeding 5 dB. This work also demonstrates the first monolithic SU(1,1) interferometer with an estimated 5 dB signal-to-noise enhancement compared to traditional linear interferometers, and thus establishes a practical pathway for chip-based quantum sensors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a first-principles computational framework to accurately model quantum decoherence in diamond by treating complex, heterogeneous defect environments. By combining DFT with spin-bath simulations, the authors show that individual defect types critically influence coherence times, offering a predictive tool for optimizing quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial link between atomistic material science (DFT of defect structure) and fundamental quantum dynamics (decoherence), which is vital for designing robust solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Predicting quantum decoherence in realistic solid-state materials is computationally challenging because complex defect environments are often inaccurately modeled as simple homogeneous spin baths.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that decoherence depends critically on the specific identity and composition of defects, showing that heterogeneous bath effects can significantly alter coherence times compared to predictions from simpler models.

**Method.** A predictive framework combining first-principles electronic structure calculations (DFT) with quantum many-body spin-bath simulations is developed and validated against magnetic-field-dependent Hahn-echo measurements.

**Model / system.** The system uses diamond containing Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) ensembles as the central qubit, interacting with a heterogeneous bath of multiple paramagnetic defects (e.g., N-, V-, H-related species).

**Key observables.** Quantum coherence time ($T_2^*$), decoherence decay behavior (stretched exponential), and field dependence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Defect identity, defect concentration, hyperfine interaction parameters, and magnetic field strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework moves beyond homogeneous bath approximations by incorporating defect-resolved spin Hamiltonians and heterogeneous interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the central spin model used to compute decoherence dynamics of an NV center interacting with a PM defect spin bath environment.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a computational framework, applies it to model decoherence in diamond using various defects, and validates its predictions by reproducing experimental coherence times across different magnetic fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum decoherence induced by defects remains a major limitation for solid-state quantum technologies, yet predicting decoherence in realistic materials remains computationally challenging. Complex defect populations are often approximated as homogeneous spin baths, obscuring the role of defect-specific electronic structure and spin dynamics. Here, we develop a predictive framework for decoherence in diamond by combining first-principles electronic-structure calculations, quantum many-body spin-bath simulations, and experimental validation. The framework incorporates defect-resolved spin Hamiltonians and heterogeneous spin baths containing multiple paramagnetic defect species. Using diamond nitrogen-vacancy ensembles as a model platform, we investigate mixed nitrogen-, vacancy-, and hydrogen-related defect environments. We show that decoherence depends not only on defect density but also on defect identity and bath composition, whose distinct electronic structures, hyperfine interactions, and spin dynamics produce different coherence behavior. Heterogeneous defect populations can either suppress or enhance decoherence, producing trends unexplained by homogeneous-bath models. Magnetic-field-dependent Hahn-echo measurements on samples with different defect concentrations validate the framework. The calculations reproduce the observed coherence times and stretched-exponential decay behavior across a broad magnetic-field range and identify vacancy-related defects as critical contributors beyond the conventionally assumed P1 spin bath. By linking atomistic defect properties to quantum coherence, our framework provides a predictive route for identifying hidden defect environments and optimizing decoherence in defect-based quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes an exact information-theoretic tradeoff theorem demonstrating that the emergence of redundant classical records via Quantum Darwinism directly degrades the logical fidelity protected by Quantum Error Correction. By analyzing a solvable model, the authors derive a quantitative relationship linking these two seemingly disparate phenomena and prove a general no-go bound governing this competition.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative link between two major concepts in quantum information theory (QEC and QD), suggesting that environmental interaction cannot simultaneously maximize both coherence protection and classical redundancy. This has implications for understanding decoherence mechanisms in realistic physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a quantitative, information-theoretic tradeoff between Quantum Error Correction (QEC), which preserves quantum coherence, and Quantum Darwinism (QD), which describes the emergence of objective classical information via environmental redundancy.

**Main result.** An exact, parameter-independent tradeoff theorem is derived showing that increasing redundant classical records (Darwinistic Redundancy) must come at the direct expense of the logical fidelity of the quantum state. Furthermore, a model-independent no-go theorem proves this fundamental limit.

**Method.** The authors analyze an exactly solvable block-environment model by deriving relationships between three key observables: logical fidelity ($F_L$), Holevo information ($\chi$), and decoherence factors ($\Gamma$).

**Model / system.** The system uses the logical GHZ block of the Shor code interacting with $N$ environment qubits. The dynamics are analyzed using a Hamiltonian structure, focusing on the resulting decoherence factor $\Gamma_N(t)$ that governs both quantum information loss and environmental entanglement.

**Key observables.** Logical Fidelity ($F_L^{(N)}$), Holevo Information ($\chi_m$), Darwinistic Redundancy ($R_\delta$), Decoherence Factor ($\Gamma_N(t)$), and Von Neumann Entropy ($S(ho_b)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Environment size ($N$), imperfect recovery efficiency ($\eta$), decoherence parameter ($gZ_t$), and the redundancy threshold ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analytical derivation relies on an exactly solvable limit (e.g., setting one coupling constant to zero), although numerical tests confirm robustness using the full Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures numerically demonstrate the competition between QEC and QD, showing data collapse onto the derived tradeoff equation, confirming critical scaling near thresholds, and verifying the model-independent no-go theorem by plotting $\chi$ vs $F_L$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the conflict between QEC and QD, develops the solvable block-environment model using the Shor code, derives expressions for fidelity and redundancy based on a common decoherence factor ($\Gamma$), and finally eliminates this factor to establish the exact tradeoff theorem and the general no-go bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) and Quantum Darwinism describe opposing consequences of system-environment interactions: QEC seeks to preserve logical quantum information, whereas Quantum Darwinism explains the emergence of objective classical information through its proliferation into the environment. Despite their common physical origin, no direct quantitative connection between these paradigms has previously been established. We introduce an exactly solvable block-environment model based on the logical GHZ block of the Shor [[9,1,3]] quantum error-correcting code, collectively coupled to N environment qubits. The logical fidelity, Holevo information, and Darwinistic redundancy are obtained systematically for arbitrary environment size and imperfect recovery efficiency. Eliminating the common decoherence parameter yields an exact tradeoff relating Darwinistic redundancy directly to the post-recovery logical fidelity, demonstrating that the emergence of redundant classical records occurs at the expense of logical quantum information. We further prove a model-independent no-go theorem showing that the logical fidelity exceeds a critical threshold precludes the emergence of Darwinistic redundancy, irrespective of the microscopic Hamiltonian or environment structure. The solvable model saturates this general bound, establishing the first quantitative information-theoretic connection between logical quantum information protection and the emergence of redundant classical records.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors investigate how symmetry protects quantum information encoded on the boundaries of a three-leg AKLT ladder. They derive a quantitative decay law showing that local probe operators can only access specific symmetry sectors, leading to an observable hierarchy in correlation lengths. This establishes a rigorous link between lattice geometry, underlying symmetries, and the robustness of topological quantum memory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative metrics (decay laws, selection rules) linking fundamental symmetries to concrete measures of quantum information protection in lattice models, which is highly relevant for understanding robust quantum memories and computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to establish a quantitative connection between Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) symmetry, lattice geometry, and the protection of boundary-encoded quantum information.

**Main result.** The paper shows that local operator accessibility decay is governed by selection rules derived from symmetries, leading to an observable hierarchy of correlation lengths in the three-leg AKLT ladder.

**Method.** The analysis uses exact Matrix Product State (MPS) constructions and channel decomposition techniques motivated by the Knill--Laflamme condition to quantify information leakage.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the three-leg Affleck-Kennedy-Lieb-Tasaki (AKLT) ladder, an exactly solvable SPT model with on-site symmetry SO(3) x Z2. It is analyzed using MPS representations derived from a frustration-free parent Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Logical gates ($	ext{SO}(3)$ rotations and permutation), local operator accessibility decay law $\delta_{	ext{edge}}(	ext{dist}) \propto e^{-	ext{dist}/\xi(L=\ell)}$, and the hierarchy of correlation lengths $\xi(L=1) > \xi(L=2) > \xi(L=3)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry labels (SO(3) tensor rank and $P_{tb}$ parity), the lattice geometry (three-leg ladder vs. single chain), and the correlation lengths $\xi(L)$ are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the system onto relevant sectors ($L$-sectors) and assuming that the decay law can be accurately modeled by retaining leading eigenvalues in the channel decomposition.

**Figures summary.** Table IV shows the channel-resolved edge fidelity $\delta_{	ext{edge}}$ at a finite size, demonstrating how contributions from different symmetry channels (b) and (c) add in quadrature to determine the overall decay profile.

**Paper structure.** The paper first characterizes the symmetries and logical gates using MPS. It then introduces a distinguishability measure based on channel decomposition of the transfer matrix. Finally, it derives the symmetry-resolved decay law for local accessibility, showing how different $L$-sectors contribute distinct correlation lengths.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases provide a platform for encoding quantum information in protected boundary degrees of freedom. Here we study the three-leg Affleck-Kennedy-Lieb-Tasaki (AKLT) ladder as an exactly solvable SPT system with on-site symmetry $SO(3)\times \mathbb{Z}_2$. Using an exact matrix product state construction, we characterize the symmetry action on the edge encoding space and the accessibility of this space by local operators. We find that the continuous $SO(3)$ symmetry induces boundary rotations, while the leg-exchange symmetry generates a geometry-dependent logical permutation of edge qubits. Furthermore, by introducing a distinguishability measure motivated by the Knill--Laflamme condition, we derive a symmetry-resolved decay law for local accessibility. The decay is controlled by a selection rule raised from the Wigner--Eckart theorem, whereby a rank-$\ell$ local operator couples only to the $\mathcal L=\ell$ transfer-matrix sector, with a decay length determined by the corresponding correlation length. We further identify a finite-size channel that is independent of the probe operator position. These results establish a quantitative connection between SPT symmetry, lattice geometry, and the protection of boundary-encoded quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how environmental noise degrades the performance of quantum teleportation using a three-qubit Maximally Sliced state. By deriving analytical links between fidelity, basis-independent coherence, and entanglement measures under amplitude and phase damping, it distinguishes the unique degradation pathways caused by different types of decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a unified, quantitative framework connecting fundamental concepts—multipartite entanglement ($	au$), coherence ($	ext{CBI}$), and operational capability (teleportation)—under realistic noisy conditions, which is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how environmental decoherence affects quantum teleportation fidelity by analyzing its interplay with basis-independent coherence in maximally sliced states.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes explicit analytical relationships between coherence and fidelity under amplitude damping (AD) and phase damping (PD), showing distinct noise effects: AD introduces a state-dependent threshold, while PD preserves the quantum advantage until complete dephasing.

**Method.** The authors employ the Kraus operator formalism to derive expressions for teleportation fidelity and basis-independent coherence, connecting these quantities to the Coffman-Kundu-Wootters (CKW) three-tangle.

**Model / system.** The system under study is a three-qubit Maximally Sliced (MS) state, which serves as the shared entangled resource for quantum teleportation. Decoherence is modeled using Amplitude Damping and Phase Damping channels.

**Key observables.** Teleportation fidelity ($F_{	ext{Tel}}$), basis-independent coherence ($	ext{CBI}$), and the CKW three-tangle ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The state parameter $	heta$, amplitude damping probability $p$, and phase damping rate $\lambda$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes independent application of Kraus operators for each qubit during decoherence, and that the teleportation protocol relies on the reduced bipartite density matrix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the monotonic increase of CBI with $F_{	ext{Tel}}$ for ideal states. Figure 2 illustrates the three-dimensional variation of CBI as a function of $F_{	ext{AD}}$ and $p$, showing positive correlation and degradation with increasing noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first defining the MS state and quantifying its entanglement via the three-tangle. It then derives fidelity expressions under AD and PD using Kraus operators, establishing analytical links between CBI and fidelity for both noise channels, concluding with a comparison of how each channel degrades quantum resources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The influence of environmental decoherence on quantum teleportation is investigated by considering the three-qubit Maximally Sliced (MS) state as the shared entangled resource. Using the Kraus operator formalism, analytical expressions are derived for the teleportation fidelity under amplitude damping and phase damping channels. The corresponding basis-independent coherence is obtained, establishing explicit analytical relations between coherence and teleportation fidelity under both decoherence mechanisms. The results are further expressed in terms of the Coffman-Kundu-Wootters (CKW) three-tangle, thereby connecting genuine tripartite entanglement with teleportation performance. The analysis reveals distinct effects of the two noise channels: amplitude damping introduces a state-dependent threshold for achieving quantum teleportation, whereas phase damping preserves the quantum advantage until complete dephasing. These results provide a unified analytical framework for understanding the interplay among multipartite entanglement, quantum coherence and teleportation in noisy three-qubit MS states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a closed-form theory for the intermediate scattering function of an interacting adlayer, treating it as an Ising lattice-gas system. By leveraging the ISF's role as a characteristic function, the authors successfully incorporate lateral interactions into surface diffusion dynamics. The resulting theory provides testable predictions that are validated against Kinetic Monte Carlo simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of dynamics from static structure factors using a characteristic function approach is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium many-body systems where memory effects are crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a closed-form theoretical description for the intermediate scattering function (ISF) of an interacting adlayer undergoing surface diffusion.

**Main result.** The authors derived a closed-form expression for the ISF that rigorously incorporates lateral interactions, predicting four correlated fingerprints observable in experimental measurements.

**Method.** The theory utilizes the characteristic function framework, projecting the master equation onto the density and employing techniques like Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) and Transfer Matrix methods to derive exact or controlled approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is an interacting adlayer modeled as an Ising lattice-gas on a substrate. Dynamics are governed by surface diffusion processes, analyzed via measurements like helium spin-echo yielding the ISF.

**Key observables.** Intermediate Scattering Function (ISF), $I(K, t)$; Static Structure Factor, $F(K)$; Quasi-elastic line shape; Linewidth and Arrhenius slope dependence on coverage.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\varepsilon$), temperature ($T$), adsorbate coverage ($	heta$), momentum transfer ($K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The closed form is obtained by dropping the memory kernel of the equation of motion, which provides a controlled approximation; the RPA is used for 2D treatment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the deviation parameter $\Delta(K)$ across the zone for different interaction regimes and test theoretical bounds against KMC data to validate the closed-form applicability.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the ISF as a characteristic function, develops the theory using lattice gas models (1D via Transfer Matrix, 2D via RPA), derives the closed form by controlling approximations, and validates these results through comparison with KMC simulations and specific physical applications (Na/Cu(111)).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The intermediate scattering function (ISF) measured by helium spin-echo is a characteristic function (CF): the Fourier transform of the distribution of adsorbate displacements, whose value at zero time is the static structure factor. We use this double role to bring lateral interactions of Ising lattice-gas type into surface diffusion. The ISF then follows in closed form in momentum transfer, time, coverage and temperature, and with it the whole linear-response hierarchy. A single correlation parameter fixes both the equilibrium structure and the interaction-renormalized hop rate; it is exact along a one-dimensional channel, and is carried onto the surface as the nearest-neighbour correlation of a pair approximation, the momentum dependence of the structure factor then coming from a collective (random-phase) treatment. The closed form is not postulated. Projecting the master equation onto the density gives an exact equation of motion whose amplitude is an identity and whose initial rate obeys a sum rule, so that the quasi-elastic weight is the layer's own structure factor and de Gennes narrowing becomes a theorem rather than an analogy; the closed form is what remains when the memory kernel of that equation is dropped. Kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) on the same Hamiltonian reproduces the dressed hop rate to better than about one per cent and the initial collective rate to $1$--$2\%$. Four correlated, non-critical fingerprints of the interaction follow --- in the quasi-elastic amplitude, its linewidth, the Arrhenius slope, and the dependence of the fitted linewidth on the range of times over which the decay is fitted. They invert in closed form for the attempt frequency, the static barrier and the interaction. Applications to Na/Cu(111) and H/Pt(111) are analysed and discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a breakthrough method to simulate Lattice Gauge Theories on quantum computers by dynamically generating the required gauge dynamics from a simpler starting Hamiltonian. By using single-qubit terms derived from Gauss's law, they successfully confine the evolution to the physical subspace while drastically reducing the circuit depth compared to direct simulation methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource limitations in simulating complex physical theories like LGTs by transforming a symmetry constraint into an active, efficient part of the Hamiltonian engineering process, which is highly relevant for quantum simulation algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a resource-efficient method for digital quantum simulation of Lattice Gauge Theories (LGTs) while rigorously enforcing gauge invariance.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated that using single-qubit terms proportional to the Gauss's law generator can dynamically induce the LGT dynamics from a simpler Hamiltonian, achieving a fivefold reduction in entangling-gate depth compared to direct implementations.

**Method.** They employ a novel 'dynamic induction' paradigm where gauge constraints are enforced via experimentally efficient single-qubit penalty terms ($V_{HG}$) added to a simple starting Hamiltonian ($H_{XXX}$), rather than relying on post-selection or energy penalties.

**Model / system.** The target system is the U(1) Quantum Link Model (QLM) LGT, simulated using spin-1/2 qubits. The implementation uses 101 qubits on an IBM quantum processor to simulate dynamics in $1+1$ dimensions.

**Key observables.** Average gauge violation ($\langle \epsilon_G angle$), mean electric field ($\langle E angle$), and comparison of time evolution against Matrix Product State (MPS) classical benchmarks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gauge protection strength ($V=4.5$); Trotter step size ($\Delta t=0.25$); Lattice size ($N_f=50$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is shown to work for $1+1$ dimensions, and the initial Hamiltonian used is simpler than the full target LGT.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate qubit encoding, gauge protection mechanisms separating physical/unphysical sectors, and time evolution plots comparing protected vs. unprotected dynamics of observables like $\langle \epsilon_G angle$ and $\langle E angle$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (LGT simulation), proposes the dynamic induction method using $H_{XXX}$ plus gauge terms ($V_{HG}$), details the implementation on quantum hardware, presents results showing reduced circuit depth and accurate dynamics matching classical benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge invariance is central to modern physics and underpins quantum simulations of lattice gauge theories (LGTs). Existing quantum simulation approaches employ Gauss's law either to energetically suppress gauge-violating processes in analog platforms or to detect and discard gauge-violating outcomes in digital devices. Here we introduce a third paradigm, in which Gauss's law is used to dynamically generate the gauge theory itself from a substantially simpler Hamiltonian. Starting from a readily programmable three-body XXX model, we employ experimentally efficient single-qubit U(1) gauge symmetry-generator terms that induce the dynamics of a U(1) LGT. We implement this approach using 101 qubits on a 156-qubit IBM quantum processor and observe real-time dynamics in quantitative agreement with the target LGT while reducing the entangling-gate depth per Trotter step by a factor of five compared with a direct implementation. Our results establish gauge protection as a resource for Hamiltonian engineering rather than merely symmetry preservation, opening a scalable resource-efficient route towards digital quantum simulations of increasingly complex gauge theories in higher spatial dimensions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces PaQit, a novel framework designed to co-optimize energy, runtime, and fidelity in neutral-atom quantum computers. It models the critical trade-off where increasing qubit density boosts speed but degrades computation accuracy due to Rydberg interactions. By integrating device physics into scheduling decisions, PaQit provides actionable guidelines for operating these complex quantum hardware systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering constraints necessary for realizing large-scale quantum computation with neutral atoms by providing a quantitative link between physical interactions (Rydberg blockade) and system performance metrics (Energy/Time/Fidelity).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The relationship between energy consumption, runtime, and computational fidelity in neutral-atom quantum computers is poorly understood, hindering practical scheduling decisions.

**Main result.** PaQit provides a framework that jointly optimizes these three metrics by translating target fidelity into specific qubit packing decisions based on Rydberg interaction physics.

**Method.** The authors developed a hardware-grounded analytical model and a fidelity-aware qubit packing framework to guide system scheduling.

**Model / system.** The platform is neutral-atom quantum computing using atoms trapped in optical lattices, where interactions are dominated by the $1/r_{ij}^6$ Rydberg blockade potential. The dynamics are modeled via a time-dependent Hamiltonian incorporating this interaction term.

**Key observables.** Energy consumption (P), Runtime (T), Computational Fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), and Qubit Packing Density ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Static baseline power ($P_0$), Rydberg blockade radius ($r_b$), target fidelity ($f^*$), and packing efficiency ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system operates in a static-power-dominated regime where energy depends heavily on execution time, not just instantaneous utilization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the bounds for serial versus maximally parallel execution. Figure 2 shows how PaQit determines optimal packing efficiency to minimize runtime and energy while meeting a target fidelity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical limitations (Rydberg interactions) leading to trade-offs between density/throughput and fidelity, develops analytical models for power/time scaling under serial and parallel regimes, introduces PaQit to resolve this multi-objective optimization, and validates the framework using simulations of real hardware platforms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral-atom quantum computers provide a scalable platform for large-scale quantum computation due to their all-optical control, room-temperature operation, and flexible lattice geometry. Although the favorable energy characteristics of these systems are well recognized, the relationship between system-level energy consumption, runtime, and computational fidelity remains poorly understood, limiting practical scheduling decisions. In this work, we develop a hardware-grounded analytical model that captures how energy and runtime scale with qubit utilization in neutral-atom systems.   We introduce PaQit, a fidelity-aware qubit packing framework that integrates device-level Rydberg interaction physics with system-level scheduling to jointly optimize energy, runtime, and fidelity. By translating fidelity targets into packing decisions, PaQit identifies operating regimes that maximize parallelism while respecting interaction-driven crosstalk constraints. We validate the analytical framework using simulations of QuEra's analog Aquila system and digital Gemini system, as well as real hardware executions, demonstrating close agreement between the predicted trends and observed system behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports the first direct joint spectral characterization of SPDC photon pairs near $2 \mu	ext{m}$ using integrated GaAs/AlGaAs waveguides. By combining time-of-flight spectroscopy with advanced numerical modeling, the authors validated their source's broadband emission and determined optimal device lengths for high-efficiency quantum light generation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes integrated quantum light sources into a technologically important wavelength band ($2 \mu	ext{m}$) crucial for free-space quantum communication, providing necessary characterization data for quantum network components.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the joint spectral characterization of spontaneously parametrically down-converted (SPDC) photon pairs near $2 \mu	ext{m}$ using integrated waveguides.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated broadband emission with strong spectral anti-correlations and developed a numerical model that accurately reproduces the measured Joint Spectral Intensity (JSI).

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements of JSI via time-of-flight spectroscopy with theoretical modeling using the biphoton wavefunction approach.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an integrated photon source utilizing straight GaAs- and AlGaAs-on-insulator waveguides, which provide strong second-order nonlinearity ($\chi^{(2)}$) for on-chip pair generation.

**Key observables.** Joint Spectral Intensity (JSI), heralded second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}_H(	au)$), and the SPDC conversion probability ($P_{	ext{SPDC}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The operating wavelength regime is near $2 \mu	ext{m}$, pumped from $980 	ext{ nm}$. Key parameters include effective areas ($\approx 0.75 \mu	ext{m}^2$) and optimal waveguide lengths (e.g., $\sim 13.8 	ext{ mm}$ for GaAs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The numerical model assumes both photons reach the output without scattering, and discrepancies in absolute efficiency are attributed to uncertainties in measuring on-chip pump power.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate waveguide cross-sections, field profiles, coincidence histograms showing spectral broadening for time-of-flight analysis, and comparisons between simulated and measured JSI structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of $2 \mu	ext{m}$ sources to detailing the experimental setup (JSI measurement), presenting quantitative results on pair generation rates ($P_{	ext{SPDC}}$), validating these results against a detailed numerical model, and finally determining optimal device lengths.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Integrated photon-pair sources are a core component of chip-based quantum computing, communication, and metrology. Although such sources have been demonstrated at conventional telecom wavelengths, the 2 $μ$m band remains comparatively less explored, despite offering advantages for free-space quantum communication, low-loss transmission in emerging fiber networks, and integration with silicon photonic platforms. In this paper, we demonstrate spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) in straight GaAs- and AlGaAs-on-insulator waveguides. This platform offers strong second-order nonlinearity and geometry-tunable dispersion, which are advantageous for efficient on-chip pair generation. Measurements of the joint spectral intensity and heralded second-order correlation function show broadband emission around 2 $μ$m with strong spectral anti-correlations. To our knowledge, this is the first direct joint-spectral characterization of an integrated SPDC source in this wavelength regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses neutron scattering to compare the ideal spin-ice material Ho2Ti2O7 with its disordered analog, Ho2TiO5. The key finding is that structural disorder does not eliminate the local magnetic tendency; instead, it creates a 'vacancy-disrupted' state where ice-rule physics persists on an incomplete lattice. This demonstrates the robustness of local constraints against global structural imperfections.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study of disorder effects (vacancy disruption) on emergent quantum ground states like spin ice is highly relevant to understanding frustrated magnetic systems that can be modeled using concepts applicable to correlated electron models or lattice gauge theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how local magnetic correlations persist when the ideal spin-ice material Ho2Ti2O7 is structurally disrupted by partial cation occupancy to form Ho2TiO5.

**Main result.** Ho2TiO5 retains a local Ising anisotropy and exhibits a 'vacancy-disrupted spin ice' state, where the tendency toward ice-rule configurations persists on a topologically incomplete magnetic network.

**Method.** The research combines neutron scattering measurements (total and half-polarized) with advanced analysis techniques like real-space mPDF and RMC modeling to probe local magnetic order.

**Model / system.** The system involves pyrochlore titanates, comparing the canonical spin-ice material Ho2Ti2O7 against the disordered stuffed compound Ho2TiO5. The physics is governed by strong single-ion Ising anisotropy on a tetrahedral lattice.

**Key observables.** Local magnetic correlations (mPDF), degree of adherence to the ice rule (e.g., 2-in/2-out configurations), and local site susceptibility tensors ($\chi_{\parallel}/\chi_{\perp}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature regimes ($0.3 	ext{ K}$ up to $50 	ext{ K}$), occupancy ratios ($2/3$ for disorder).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the local magnetic correlations can be extracted and modeled despite the average crystal structure retaining high symmetry ($	ext{Fd}ar{3}	ext{m}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural models comparing ideal vs. stuffed compounds, and show quantitative data on spin configurations (e.g., fraction of tetrahedra satisfying ice rules) as a function of temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the methods using PDF analysis for local structure determination, followed by polarized neutron diffraction to confirm single-ion anisotropy, and finally applying RMC/mPDF analyses to quantify the deviation from ideal spin-ice behavior in the disordered compound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how local magnetic correlations evolve when the classical pyrochlore spin-ice Ho2Ti2O7 is transformed into the partially disordered stuffed compound Ho2TiO5. Neutron scattering measurements were analyzed using real-space magnetic pair distribution function, reciprocal-space reverse Monte Carlo, and half-polarized neutron powder diffraction methods to connect the average crystal structure with local magnetic correlations. Both compounds retain long-range $Fd-3m symmetry, but in Ho2TiO5 this average structure distorts locally through Ho-O, Ti-O and O-O bond-length disorder associated with partial Ho/Ti occupancy. Despite this disorder, half-polarized neutron powder diffraction shows that the Ho moments retain local <111> rangle Ising anisotropy and form spin-in/spin-out configurations on the tetrahedral network. In Ho2Ti2O7, real- and reciprocal-space analyses reveal a nearly ideal two-in/two-out spin-ice state at 0.3K, with 95% of tetrahedra satisfying the ice rule, followed by progressive thermal disordering on warming. In Ho2TiO5, most tetrahedra are magnetically incomplete because some tetrahedral vertices are occupied by nonmagnetic Ti rather than Ho; nevertheless, the dominant incomplete configurations are 2-in/1-out and 1-in/2-out, which are locally compatible with the ice rule if the missing Ho spin is restored. The single-ion <111> Ising anisotropy is therefore retained throughout, while the collective ice ordering is strongly disrupted: Ho2TiO5 is a vacancy-disrupted spin ice in which a local ice-rule tendency persists on a topologically incomplete magnetic network.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces DAMPyF, an open-source Python tool implementing the DAMPF method to simulate open quantum dynamics. It allows for numerically exact simulation of system-environment coupling using tensor networks (MPS) applied to pseudomode representations. The tool is versatile, supporting both general energy transfer and specialized molecular spectroscopy calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses state-of-the-art simulation techniques for open quantum systems, which is a major focus area in modern AMO physics, especially concerning light-matter interactions and energy transport.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The simulation of open quantum systems where a finite system interacts with an environment exhibiting memory effects, requiring non-perturbative treatment.

**Main result.** Introduction of DAMPyF, an open-source Python implementation enabling numerically exact simulations for complex dynamics like energy transfer and molecular spectroscopy.

**Method.** The Dissipation-Assisted Matrix Product Factorization (DAMPF) method is used to represent the extended density matrix ($ho_{se}$) via a tensor network approach (MPS formalism).

**Model / system.** The system involves a finite quantum system coupled to an environment modeled by structured reservoirs, often described by generalized spin-boson models. Dynamics are governed by the GKSL master equation.

**Key observables.** Reduced system density matrix ($ho_s(t)$), linear absorption spectra ($A(\omega)$), and circular dichroism spectra ($	ext{CD}(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pseudomode bond dimension ($\chi$), time step ($\Delta t$), thermal energy ($k_BT$), and system coupling strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the pseudomode representation of structured reservoirs, which assumes Gaussianity to maintain numerically exact reduced system dynamics. Trotter decomposition introduces an error $O(\Delta t^2)$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates partitioning the environment into a non-Markovian core and Markovian remainder; Figure 2 graphically details the DAMPF decomposition of $ho_{se}$ using MPSs.

**Paper structure.** The paper describes the physical model, the DAMPF algorithm derivation, the user-facing code structure (including installation/execution), input/output formats, and provides minimal working examples for various applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

DAMPyF is an open-source Python implementation of the dissipation-assisted matrix product factorization (DAMPF) method, a tensor-network-based approach for the numerically exact simulation of finite-dimensional quantum systems coupled to bosonic environments. The method relies on a pseudomode representation of structured reservoirs and a matrix-product-state representation of the density matrix of the extended system, comprising the system and the pseudomodes. DAMPyF currently provides two workflows. First, it supports excitation energy-transfer dynamics within the single-system-excitation manifold, in which a system excitation is propagated in time. Second, it provides a high-level workflow tailored to molecular spectroscopy, in which the system levels represent electronic states and optical coherences are propagated for the subsequent computation of linear spectra, including absorption and circular dichroism. This paper describes the physical model, the DAMPF algorithm, the user-facing code structure, installation and execution, input and output formats, and minimal examples.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Sample-Half-Inserted HOM (SHOM) interferometer as a novel technique to dramatically improve measurement precision in quantum interferometry. By exploiting an asymmetric photon-sample interaction, SHOM enhances the Fisher information by five orders of magnitude over standard methods. This allows for achieving nanoscale measurements with significantly fewer photons, paving the way for practical quantum-enhanced metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally validated method to boost the sensitivity of quantum optical measurements beyond standard limits by engineering the interaction geometry, which is highly relevant for developing practical quantum sensors in AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the precision of optical path difference measurements using Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interferometry, which typically suffers from low Fisher information per measurement trial.

**Main result.** The proposed Sample-Half-Inserted HOM (SHOM) interferometer enhances the Fisher information by five orders of magnitude compared to standard HOM. Experimentally, this allowed achieving nanoscale precision ($13.63 	ext{ as}$) using significantly fewer photons ($O(10^7)$).

**Method.** The authors developed and experimentally demonstrated the SHOM configuration, which introduces an asymmetric photon-sample interaction leading to a unique dip-bump-dip interference pattern.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum interferometer utilizing two-photon quantum interference (HOM effect) enhanced by modifying the sample interaction to create the SHOM setup. Theoretical models for coincidence probabilities ($	ext{P}_{	ext{SHOM}}$) and precision estimation via Fisher Information were employed.

**Key observables.** Coincidence counts, optical path difference/time delay ($T$), interference patterns (dip-bump-dip structure), and calculated Fisher information ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Precision of $4.09 	ext{ nm}$ ($\approx 13.63 	ext{ as}$) and accuracy of $1.22 	ext{ nm}$ ($\approx 4.07 	ext{ as}$) achieved with $O(10^7)$ photons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fundamental limitation overcome is the low Fisher information inherent to standard HOM schemes; the technique assumes that the asymmetric interaction can be reliably modeled and measured.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept of SHOM setup and compares coincidence probabilities ($	ext{P}_{	ext{HOM}}$ vs $	ext{P}_{	ext{SHOM}}$). Figure 2 shows experimental results, including HOM/SHOM patterns varying with temperature, resulting sinusoidal count variations, and calculated Fisher information.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitation of standard HOM interferometry, proposes the SHOM enhancement via asymmetric interaction, derives the necessary mathematical models for coincidence probabilities and Fisher Information, presents experimental validation showing superior precision, and concludes by discussing the broad applicability of the SHOM technique to other quantum metrology tasks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum technologies have been widely recognized as unprecedented opportunities for ultra-high precision metrology. As a celebrated example in modern quantum optics, the Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interferometer is well-known for enabling temporal resolutions on the attosecond scale. However, the relatively low Fisher information per trial in ordinary HOM measurements typically necessitates tens of thousands of repetitions to achieve such precision. Here, we propose and demonstrate a sample-half-inserted HOM (SHOM) interferometer, which enhances the Fisher information by five orders of magnitude in a single interference event. By introducing an asymmetric photon-sample interaction, the SHOM configuration produces a distinctive dip-bump-dip interference structure, converting what was previously viewed as an artifact into a helpful metrological resource. Experimentally, we measured the optical path difference with an average precision of 4.09 nm (13.63 as) and an average accuracy of 1.22 nm (4.07 as) using $O(10^7)$ photons. Our results establish SHOM interferometry as an efficient phase-insensitive approach, not only paving the way toward practical quantum-enhanced thickness measurement for transparent materials, but also serving as an elegant strategy to improve the performance of various quantum devices.

</details>

They win if Bob can exclude one of the possible combinations. So he is supposed to reply either “(x1, x2) is not (3, 1)” or “(x1, x2) is not (6, 1)” or “(x1, x2) is not (6, 5)”. We show that the task can be solved perfectly with the presented qubit strategy but requires a classical message of more than two...</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the communication complexity required to simulate joint measurements on a star network of $n$ parties. It proves that achieving perfect distinguishability classically demands a message length scaling exponentially with both the number of parties and the dimension of the quantum system. This highlights a significant, quantifiable advantage of using quantum channels over classical ones for multi-partite correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous information-theoretic bounds showing that simulating multi-partite quantum correlations requires resources scaling non-trivially with system size, which is foundational for understanding the limits of classical simulation in quantum physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the quantum communication complexity of correlations arising from joint measurements on a star network, comparing the advantage of quantum versus classical message transmission.

**Main result.** It establishes that the classical communication cost required to simulate joint quantum measurements scales exponentially with the dimension ($d$) and number of parties ($n$), demonstrating a fundamental quantum advantage.

**Method.** The authors use concepts from quantum information theory, including antidistinguishability, to derive lower bounds on classical simulation costs for specific exclusion tasks in a star network setting.

**Model / system.** The model involves $n$ parties (Alices) each sending a $d$-level quantum system to a central node (Bob), who performs a joint measurement. The core task is distinguishing between potential input states based on the measurement outcome.

**Key observables.** Quantum correlation probabilities, classical communication message length ($m$), and the success probability of an exclusion task.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of senders ($n$) and dimension of the quantum system ($d$). The scaling is shown to depend on both $n$ and $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on scenarios where the task can be solved perfectly quantumly, and communication is restricted between Alices and Bob.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the star network setup for joint measurement. Figure 2 provides a concrete example demonstrating the required exclusion output based on input promises for $n=2$ and $d=6$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results by first defining the quantum communication task, then deriving lower bounds on classical simulation costs using antidistinguishability arguments (Theorem 1), followed by constructing a perfect quantum strategy (Appendix A) to confirm these bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is well established that quantum strategies outperform classical ones in several communication tasks. We study the quantum communication complexity of correlations arising from joint measurements on quantum systems distributed across a star network, where several parties each send a quantum system to a central node. We introduce an exclusion task that can be solved perfectly when each party sends a quantum $d$-level system, but would require a large classical message otherwise. In fact, the task cannot be solved with certainty if each of the $n$ parties sends a classical message with less than $n^{(d-1)}$ symbols. This implies an advantage of using quantum over classical messages in that scenario that scales with both, the dimension of the quantum system and the number of systems measured simultaneously. As an application, this shows that no finite-size classical description of a qubit suffices to reproduce the statistics of a joint measurement on sufficiently many qubits.

</details>
