**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated topological framework to measure entanglement in electronic systems by quantifying obstructions to factorizing the occupied state manifold. It shows that while local measurements might suggest independent subsystems, global invariants like mixed Chern classes reveal genuine, non-factorizable quantum correlations. This has direct implications for understanding exotic topological phases in condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools (mixed Chern classes, clutching quotients) to quantify entanglement in topological quantum matter that cannot be captured by simply summing up contributions from individual subsystems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is characterizing topological obstructions that prevent the global factorization of occupied electronic multiplets when decomposing them into independent candidate subsystems.

**Main result.** The mixed Chern class ($\chi$) successfully isolates non-factorizable topological information, and a specific criterion based on $\gcd(p,q)$ determines if global factorization is possible over $S^4$.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic topology tools, comparing transition functions via quotient structures (like the subsystem-gluing quotient) and analyzing clutching maps for bundles over $S^4$.

**Model / system.** The study concerns isolated occupied multiplets described by vector bundles ($E 	o X$) in multiband quantum systems, analyzed using Hamiltonians and spectral projectors.

**Key observables.** Mixed Chern classes ($\chi_{ab}$), total/one-label Chern numbers ($C_{tot}, C_A, C_B$), charge transfers ($\Delta Q$), and the clutching quotient $\eta_{p,q}(E)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The factor dimensions $p$ and $q$, the geometry of the underlying manifold (e.g., closed surface vs. $S^4$), and the flux pumping parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining appropriate quotient structures to filter out trivially factorizable topological data, and in some cases, assumes ideal measurement protocols for tomography.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that while total and one-label responses vanish (e.g., $\Delta Q_{tot} = \Delta Q_A = 0$), the mixed invariant ($\chi$) remains nonzero, confirming entanglement beyond simple factor contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the obstruction via transition function comparison on a general surface to analyzing the specific case of $S^4$ using clutching maps. It then applies these concepts to physical measurements like flux pumping and projector tomography protocols.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An isolated occupied multiplet may admit local tensor-product descriptions without a globally consistent subsystem structure. We characterize the obstruction by comparing the transition functions of the occupied multiplet with those generated by independent basis changes in the two candidate subsystems. When a decomposition into rank-one sectors over a closed surface is specified, the resulting quotient removes row- and column-additive Chern data and yields mixed Chern classes. Momentum-dependent mixing of the sector labels adds the Gauss--Codazzi curvature of the moving lines, while in the label-conserving limit the mixed class is measured by a crossed Thouless pump. When only the factor dimensions $p$ and $q$ are specified, the comparison is made at the level of the clutching map of a rank-$pq$ bundle over $S^4$. Product frames generate winding numbers in $q\mathbb Z+p\mathbb Z$, so global factorization is possible exactly when $C_2$ is divisible by $\gcd(p,q)$; in particular, odd $C_2$ obstructs a $2\times2$ factorization. We illustrate the two settings with finite eight-level Hamiltonians and give pumping and occupied-projector tomography protocols for their readout.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details methods to perform fault-tolerant measurements on protected $0-\pi$ superconducting qubits. By leveraging QND techniques and coupling the qubit state to auxiliary modes, the authors achieve measurement protocols for both Z and X bases. These results are crucial for realizing universal, error-corrected quantum computation in superconducting circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete theoretical blueprints for performing necessary diagnostic measurements (readout) on highly protected quantum hardware, directly addressing a major bottleneck in scaling up superconducting quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing robust methods to measure protected superconducting qubits without destroying their inherent protection mechanisms, which is essential for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully propose protocols for performing both Z and X basis measurements on the $0-\pi$ qubit that maintain its protected nature by utilizing Quantum Non-Demolition (QND) measurement principles.

**Method.** Measurement relies on encoding logical information into auxiliary modes ($\zeta$ mode for Z, $	heta$ mode parity for X) and coupling this information to ancillary qubits via controlled gates like Echoed Controlled Displacement (ECD).

**Model / system.** The system is the $0-\pi$ superconducting qubit, whose Hamiltonian involves multiple coupled Josephson junctions. The protection mechanism is linked to code structures, such as those related to rotor-GKP codes.

**Key observables.** Measurement error rates for both Z and X bases; identification of proximity to protected states ($\vertar{0}angle$ or $\vertar{1}angle$) via measurement outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Josephson coupling strengths ($E_J$), charging energies ($E_C$), ancillary qubit parameters, and control time scales (e.g., turn-off times $	au_{\downarrow}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocols assume the ability to rapidly switch off Josephson junctions or treat certain modes classically for simplification in deriving error bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Z measurement protocol using the $\zeta$ mode and ECD gates, and the X measurement by mapping information onto Cooper-pair parity of the $	heta$ mode. Later figures analyze how varying impedance affects these measured error rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general need for protected measurements to detailing specific protocols for Z and X bases on the $0-\pi$ qubit, followed by quantitative analysis showing the dependence of measurement errors on external parameters like impedance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Protected superconducting qubits such as the $0$-$π$ qubit promise to substantially suppress error rates, facilitating fault-tolerant quantum computing with fewer qubits. Measuring these qubits is challenging due to their protected nature, and thus far no concrete proposal exists for how to measure them without breaking their protection. Here we show how to perform protected measurements of the $0$-$π$ qubit in two orthogonal bases. The protection of these measurements is facilitated by their quantum non-demolition nature, allowing faults on ancillary measurement qubits to be tolerated. As experimental progress pushes protected qubits further into the low error-rate regime, our techniques will be crucial for fault-tolerant universal control.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces TNASS, a novel active space selection technique that uses tensor networks to quantify multi-orbital electronic entanglement. By encoding orbital purities as an MPS and calculating the Entanglement Feature, it identifies strongly correlated regions more robustly than traditional methods. This provides a scalable foundation for high-accuracy molecular simulations in complex chemical environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how quantum information theory can be leveraged to solve fundamental computational bottlenecks in chemistry, providing a rigorous, entanglement-based alternative to heuristic selection criteria for strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing an accurate, scalable, and automated method for selecting the active space in multi-scale molecular electronic structure calculations.

**Main result.** The proposed TNASS method, utilizing entanglement features, yields lower ground state energies and more accurate dipole moments compared to existing fully automated selection schemes like those based on single-orbital entropy or HOMO/LUMO gaps.

**Method.** It employs Tensor Network methods (specifically representing orbital purities as a Matrix Product State) to quantify multi-orbital entanglement via the Entanglement Feature (EF).

**Model / system.** The method is applied to molecular electronic structure calculations, benchmarked on diatomic molecules like BeO and BN using the 6-31G* basis set.

**Key observables.** Ground state energies, dipole moments, single-orbital entropy, and multi-orbital entanglement measures derived from the EF.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method's computational cost scales poorly with bond dimension ($\chi$), limiting initial applications to small systems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that quantifying electronic correlation via entanglement structure provides a superior guide for active space selection compared to single-orbital metrics or simple gap analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy dissociation curves and dipole moment changes across different geometries (compressed, intermediate, stretched) for BeO/BN, contrasting TNASS results against CCSD(T) benchmarks and other selection methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of active space selection, details the mathematical framework using MPS to encode entanglement features (EF), compares EF-based selection (TNASS) against single-orbital entropy and HOMO/LUMO approaches across various molecular geometries, and validates performance via energy and dipole moment calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quality of multi-scale modelling techniques in molecular electronic structure calculations, such as embedding and subspace methods, relies upon the chosen active space. The automation of active space selection is vital for ensuring the accuracy, reproducibility, and scalability in such calculations. In this work, we introduce Tensor Network Active Space Selection using the Entanglement Feature. Through the isolation of strongly correlated electrons, this method provides a scalable foundation for embedding methods in multi-scale modelling. By representing the purities of all possible orbital partitions as a Matrix Product State, our method isolates regions of strong electron correlation without requiring manual preselection of target atoms or the calculation of expensive high-order density matrices. The results demonstrate that this approach leads to lower ground state energies and more accurate dipole moments than other fully automated selection schemes such as those based solely on single-orbital entropy or the selection of spatial orbitals around the HOMO/LUMO gap.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive theory for Coulomb drag in coupled quantum wires under a magnetic field. It predicts characteristic magneto-oscillations whose contrast depends on temperature relative to the field-sharpened tunneling width. Furthermore, it rigorously analyzes nonlinear and nonreciprocal transport effects, providing diagnostic tools for characterizing device physics beyond simple linear response.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed theoretical framework for analyzing mesoscopic transport phenomena like Coulomb drag in magnetic fields is highly relevant to understanding quantum coherence and interaction effects in nanoscale electronic devices, bridging concepts from condensed matter theory and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates magneto-oscillations, nonlinearity, and nonreciprocity effects in Coulomb drag within interactively coupled quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The theory derives closed forms for the linear drag conductance and shows that nonlinear/nonreciprocal responses are governed by transconductance measurements or geometric asymmetries.

**Method.** The analysis employs Keldysh scattering-state constructions, Landauer formalism extensions, and detailed derivations in both linear (thermal) and nonlinear (shot noise) transport regimes.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two interactively coupled adiabatic constrictions (quantum point contacts or short quantum wires) embedded in a magnetic field. The coupling is modeled purely by the Coulomb interaction.

**Key observables.** Magneto-oscillations in drag current, linear drag conductance ($g_D$), particle-hole asymmetry factor ($A_i(\omega)$), and nonreciprocal components of the drag signal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$) leading to cyclotron frequency ($\omega_c$), temperature ($T$), gate voltage ($V_g$), and the ratio of thermal length to coupling range ($L_T/d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes a minimal system of two coupled constrictions, uses approximations for the interaction kernel in different limits (e.g., long thermal length), and analyzes specific transport regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate magnetoconductance staircase structures dependent on confinement ratios, show periodicity in $1/B$, map parameter space showing drag ridges tracking subband steps, and compare theoretical predictions for temperature dependence ($T^2$ vs. linear-in-$T$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the basic Hamiltonian and single-particle solutions to derive the linear drag conductance using Keldysh techniques. It then extends this analysis to the nonlinear regime (shot noise) and discusses distinct sources of nonreciprocity, concluding with open problems regarding zero-field asymmetry and low-temperature upturns.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the problem of Coulomb drag in interactively coupled quantum circuits built of adiabatic constrictions: quantum point contacts and short quantum-wire channels. The interplay of spatial confinement and magnetic field leads to a rich oscillatory response of the drag current as a function of gate voltage and magnetic field: drag peaks track the depopulation of magnetoelectric subbands, are asymptotically periodic in inverse field, and their visibility is controlled by the competition of temperature with the field-sharpened tunneling width of the constriction. We derive a closed expression for the linear drag conductance whose interaction kernel simplifies dramatically in the experimentally relevant limit of a long thermal length compared with the range of the interwire coupling, investigate the drag in the nonlinear regime, where the drag current measures the transconductance of the drive channel at any field, and discuss physically motivated models of dissipation-induced nonreciprocity of the drag signal. Extensions accounting for Zeeman splitting, interaction renormalization of the barrier transmission, backscattering at high field, and the frequency structure of the circuit coupling delineate how each mechanism imprints itself on the temperature dependence and lineshapes of the drag oscillations.

</details>

### [Separable States Violate the Complementary-Quantum Correlation Conjecture](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03828v1)

**Authors:** Jinbo Wang, Qihang Wang, Kun Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03828v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** The paper disproves the Complementary-Quantum Correlation conjecture using an explicit, non-entangled (separable) state for local dimensions $d \geq 3$. By comparing classical mutual information sums from complementary measurements against the quantum mutual information, the authors demonstrate a measurable excess. This finding is significant because it shows that correlations violating this bound do not require entanglement or quantum discord.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental theoretical challenge to existing bounds in quantum correlation theory by showing that simple separable states can violate conjectures previously thought to hold universally, impacting how we quantify non-classical correlations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to disprove the Complementary-Quantum Correlation (CQC) conjecture, which posits an upper bound on the sum of classical mutual informations from complementary measurements relative to the quantum mutual information.

**Main result.** The CQC conjecture is disproved for all local dimensions $d \geq 3$ using a specific rank-two separable state. This violation occurs even without invoking entanglement or quantum discord.

**Method.** The authors analyze the bipartite state using two complementary projective measurements (computational and Fourier-conjugate bases) and calculate classical Shannon mutual informations to show their sum exceeds the premeasurement quantum mutual information.

**Model / system.** The study considers a general bipartite quantum state $ho_{AB}$ defined for local dimension $d \geq 3$. The counterexample specifically utilizes an explicit rank-two separable state: $ho_{AB} = rac{1}{2}|uangle\langle u|_A \otimes |vangle\langle v|_B + rac{1}{2}|vangle\langle v|_A \otimes |uangle\langle u|_B$.

**Key observables.** Classical mutual informations $I(Z_A : Z_B)$ and $I(X_A : X_B)$, compared against the quantum mutual information $I(A:B)$. The excess violation is quantified for qutrits as $\approx 0.048$ bits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local dimension $d \geq 3$. The violation persists and approaches a limit as $d 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The counterexample state is separable, meaning the failure of the conjecture occurs in non-entangled regimes. The result only refutes the specific two-sided CQC inequality.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the CQC conjecture and then constructs a concrete counterexample using a separable state for $d \geq 3$. It calculates the relevant mutual information quantities to demonstrate the violation, concluding with the physical interpretation that the effect stems from complementary readouts of a shared classical variable.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Correlations measured in complementary local bases provide an experimentally accessible probe of the total correlations in a bipartite quantum state. The complementary-quantum correlation conjecture asserts that the sum of two such classical mutual informations never exceeds the premeasurement quantum mutual information. We disprove this conjecture in every local dimension $d\geq3$ with an explicit rank-two separable state. One of the two complementary measurements recovers the complete one-bit branch label, while the other retains additional classical correlation. For qutrits the excess is exactly $\frac13\log_2(3456/3125)=0.0484156759\ldots$ bits. Continuity yields full-rank separable violations. The effect therefore requires neither entanglement nor quantum discord; it arises from two complementary readouts of the same classical latent variable.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'dense coding swapping,' a mechanism allowing the transfer of quantum information advantage between different communication channels using joint unitary operations. By deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on entanglement measures like Schmidt coefficients, it provides a theoretical framework for enhancing network robustness against channel limitations or eavesdropping.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify how quantum advantages can be actively managed or transferred in complex multi-party quantum networks, which is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes 'dense coding swapping,' a novel multipartite entanglement-assisted task where quantum advantage (dense codeability) is transferred between communication channels using joint unitary operations.

**Main result.** Necessary and sufficient conditions for this swapping are derived in terms of Schmidt coefficients, showing that the process is possible while respecting constraints like the DC exclusion principle. The required resource state needs only a small amount of genuine multipartite entanglement.

**Method.** The analysis uses mathematical derivations based on conditional von Neumann entropy, Schmidt decomposition, and optimization techniques to find optimal unitary operators realizing the swap.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on $N$-party quantum states (e.g., three-qubit pure states or generalized W states) shared among legitimate parties, involving bipartite channels like $ho_{AE}$ and $ho_{AB}$.

**Key observables.** Dense Coding Capacity ($C(\cdot)$), conditional von Neumann entropy $S(A|B)$, Schmidt coefficients ($\lambda_i$), and Bloch correlation parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties ($N$), the admixture parameter for noise ($s$), and unitary transformation parameters ($J_x, J_y, J_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes pure states initially, extending later to mixed states via white or colored noise admixtures. The process relies on applying specific two-qubit unitary operations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of DC swapping in tripartite settings and map out parameter spaces showing where quantum advantage is present or lost due to noise or state constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the core concept (DC advantage/exclusion principle) to deriving necessary conditions for pure states using Schmidt coefficients, then extends this analysis to mixed states under various noise models, and finally optimizes unitary parameters for maximum capacity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a novel multipartite entanglement-assisted classical communication task, referred to as dense coding swapping, in which legitimate parties collaboratively swap the dense codeability from one communication channel to another through suitable joint unitary operations. Due to the dense coding (DC) exclusion principle, the scheme enhances the dense codeability of a target pair while simultaneously reducing it for a non-target branch in the network. This swapping capability has broader implications, as it may be viewed as a form of process swapping, distinct from resource swapping, while also providing a prevention measure when one of the receivers is compromised. We derive necessary and sufficient conditions, expressed in terms of the Schmidt coefficients, for three-qubit pure states to support DC swapping, while we obtain a sufficient criterion for mixed states using their Bloch correlation parameters. Furthermore, we identify the optimal two-qubit unitary operators capable of realizing the swapping of dense codeability between communication channels. We further examine the tolerance of these eligible states against both colored and white noise, demonstrating the resilience of the proposed task under environmental perturbations. We also show that multipartite states supporting DC swapping require only a small amount of genuine multipartite entanglement and that this requirement decreases with increasing system size.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces THQLink, an architecture designed to enable real-time Quantum Error Correction (QEC) decoding using coupled High-Performance Computing and QPU resources. By implementing a parallel window decoding strategy, the authors overcome the latency limitations of traditional decoders, enabling scalable fault tolerance for large quantum codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering bottleneck in fault-tolerant quantum computing by providing a concrete, high-throughput hardware/software framework for error correction decoding.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving real-time decoding of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes, which requires processing syndrome measurements within microsecond timescales to prevent computational backlogs.

**Main result.** The proposed THQLink architecture successfully demonstrates real-time decoding for the surface code up to distance 19 by integrating HPC resources with the QPU via a low-latency interconnect.

**Method.** The authors propose an architectural framework (THQLink) and compare different decoding strategies, notably developing a parallel-window decoder that scales better than global batch decoders in terms of latency vs. code distance.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on the surface code model for QEC, which protects logical qubits using physical qubits arranged on a 2D lattice. The implementation relies on coupling quantum processors (QPU) with general-purpose High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources.

**Key observables.** Round-trip latency ($\mu$s), decoding time per round ($1 \mu s$), logical error rate ($	ext{P}_{	ext{fail}}$), and code distance ($d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$) (e.g., 0.1%, 0.3%), Code Distance ($d$), and the required real-time constraint ($\sim 1 \mu s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The parallel window decoding strategy trades a slight increase in logical error rate for a substantial reduction in latency, which is necessary for large code distances.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the THQLink architecture and closed-loop data flow. Comparisons show that while global decoders fail to meet real-time constraints beyond moderate distances, the parallel window decoder maintains throughput by overlapping syndrome rounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the necessity of low-latency QEC decoding. It then details the THQLink architecture and its performance metrics. Finally, it compares advanced decoding algorithms (Global vs. Parallel Window) to demonstrate scalable real-time operation for large surface codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) is indispensable for building scalable fault-tolerant quantum computers. Effective QEC demands stringent real-time decoding: the decoder must process syndrome measurements and determine corrections within a time scale--typically on the order of microseconds, to avoid data backlog. Scaling to large number of logical qubits further necessitates significant computational resources. In this work, we propose an architecture, called \emph{THQLink}, for real-time decoding of quantum error correction codes using high-performance computing (HPC) resources. The network connecting the HPC and the control system of quantum processing unit (QPU) is built on TH-Express and can be adapted to different quantum technologies and their associated control stacks. We report a round-trip latency of 2.944 $μ$s on average, with an incremental overhead of 130 ns per additional hop. Using a parallel window strategy, we demonstrate real-time decoding (1 $μ$s per QEC round) of the surface code up to distance 19 using a matching-based decoder on CPUs. Our work presents a scalable framework for real-time decoding in fault-tolerant quantum computing. It can be readily applied to quantum-centric supercomputers that feature tight integration between QPU and HPC resources, thereby enabling efficient support for hybrid quantum-classical algorithms and computation-intensive workloads offloaded from the QPU.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the entanglement distillation capacity of a specific family of two-qutrit quantum states. It resolves the 1-distillability problem by proving that the states are undistillable over an open parameter interval. Furthermore, it establishes a structural obstruction concerning the necessary conditions for 2-distillability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (like analyzing principal minors and quadratic forms) to determine fundamental resource properties (entanglement distillability) in multi-partite quantum systems, which is central to quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the distillability properties (1-distillability and 2-distillability) of a specific class of symmetric NPT quantum states.

**Main result.** They prove that for a certain parameter range, these states are 1-undistillable, and they characterize a structural obstruction preventing negative expectation values required for 2-distillability.

**Method.** The analysis uses criteria based on positive semidefiniteness of projected matrices (for distillability) and reformulating the problem as finding negative eigenvalues of quadratic forms associated with Schmidt-rank-two vectors.

**Model / system.** The system involves two-qutrit quantum systems described by a family of rank-five symmetric NPT states, $ho$, parameterized by an eigenvalue $\lambda_5$.

**Key observables.** 1-distillability and 2-distillability; the expectation value $\langle\psi|\sigma|\psiangle$ for specific vectors $|\psiangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The state is parameterized by eigenvalues, particularly $\lambda_5$, which must fall within certain intervals to satisfy NPT or PPT conditions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear algebraic criteria (like positive semidefiniteness of minors) and the assumption that distillability can be determined by checking specific low-rank vectors.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail the construction of 16 large principal submatrices ($\mathbf{M}_1$ through $\mathbf{M}_{16}$) used to calculate a quadratic form expectation value.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing preliminaries on distillability criteria, then tackles 1-distillability using minor positivity checks, followed by a detailed structural analysis of the necessary conditions for 2-distillability involving quadratic forms and subspace projections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement distillation is a fundamental task in quantum information processing. In this work, we investigate the distillability properties of a class of two-qutrit symmetric NPT states of rank five. We resolve the 1-distillability problem for this class by proving that the previously open interval of the eigenvalue parameter is 1-undistillable. For the 2-distillability, we uncover a structural obstruction showing that no Schmidt-rank-two vector has a negative expectation in the relevant subspace. We also perform numerical investigations to explore the 2-distillability beyond this obstruction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel mathematical theory for quantifying 'Schmidt-gauge non-local magic,' a measure of genuine non-local correlations in quantum states. It achieves this by recasting the problem into the language of discrete harmonic analysis using the Walsh–Hadamard transform. The work establishes that this gauge provides an exact and mathematically rich characterization, particularly useful for understanding entanglement structure beyond simple spectral measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful new analytical tool (discrete harmonic analysis) to study entanglement structure in quantum states, which is highly relevant for understanding resource quantification in quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to mathematically isolate the genuinely non-local correlations within a quantum state's non-stabilizerness, distinct from local contributions.

**Main result.** The Schmidt-gauge formulation provides an exact characterization of this non-local magic, revealing an underlying discrete harmonic structure via the Walsh–Hadamard representation.

**Method.** The authors develop a mathematical theory by reformulating the optimization problem using the Walsh–Hadamard transform, connecting it to concepts from discrete harmonic analysis.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes bipartite pure quantum states $|\psiangle$, focusing on characterizing non-local correlations via entanglement spectra and stabilizer formalism.

**Key observables.** Schmidt-gauge non-local magic ($M_{	ext{Sch}}^2(\psi)$), Entanglement spectrum, Walsh–Hadamard coefficients, Second-order stabilizer Rényi entropy ($	ext{Rényi}_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bipartition structure (especially $1 	imes N$ vs. general), Local unitary transformations ($U_A, U_B$), and the associated harmonic modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial focus is on symmetric bipartitions, though exactness for $1 	imes N$ bipartitions is proven; extension to generic bipartitions remains an open problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the non-local magic via minimization over local unitaries, deriving its connection to the Schmidt spectrum, and finally establishing the rigorous mathematical framework using Walsh–Hadamard analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While the full non-stabilizerness (magic) of a quantum state contains local, basis-dependent contributions, a non-local formulation based on a minimization over local unitaries isolates the component associated with genuinely non-local correlations. Within such a framework, the Schmidt-gauge formulation of non-local magic provides a direct connection between genuinely non-local non-stabilizer correlations and the entanglement spectrum of quantum many-body states. Building on the exact Walsh--Hadamard representation introduced in our accompanying Letter, we develop the mathematical theory associated with this formulation. We extend the formalism to arbitrary bipartitions, prove the exactness of the Schmidt gauge for arbitrary $1\times N$ bipartitions, and derive general analytical properties of Schmidt-gauge non-local magic, including entanglement bounds, selection rules, and exact relations with the moments of the normalized Walsh spectrum. This representation also allows to interpret non-local magic as the inverse participation ratio of the Walsh entanglement spectrum, thus relating it to a concentration measure in Walsh space. These results demonstrate that the Walsh--Hadamard representation reveals an underlying discrete harmonic structure that is hidden in the original spectral formulation and provides considerably more than an equivalent expression for Schmidt-gauge non-local magic. Rather, it furnishes the natural mathematical framework for its analytical investigation, placing the theory within the broader context of discrete harmonic analysis.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the complex geometry of sets defining separable or positive partial transpose bipartite quantum states. It proves that the set of absolute PPT states forms a spectrahedron, while the absolutely separable states are more general semialgebraic sets. The work provides deep structural insights into these boundaries using advanced tools from convex and algebraic geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous geometric characterization of entanglement witnesses (like PPT/separability criteria) is fundamental for understanding quantum correlations and resource theories in quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the geometric structure of the spectra for bipartite quantum states that satisfy absolute separability ($	ext{ASEP}_{m,n}$) or are absolutely positive partial transpose ($	ext{APPT}_{m,n}$). It aims to characterize these sets using tools from convex geometry.

**Main result.** It proves that $	ext{APPT}_{m,n}$ is a spectrahedron with exposed faces, while $	ext{ASEP}_{m,n}$ is generally only semialgebraic. Furthermore, it provides structural characterizations of the boundaries and extreme points for both sets.

**Method.** The methodology combines convex geometry (studying absolute convex sets and their spectra) with algebraic techniques involving polynomial determinants derived from permutation group actions on matrix representations.

**Model / system.** The focus is on bipartite quantum states $ho \in M_m \otimes M_n$, specifically those satisfying the criteria of absolute separability or absolute PPT, which are constrained by global unitary transformations.

**Key observables.** Maximal attainable purity, minimal von Neumann entropy, relative spectral volume, and the geometric structure (spectrahedra/semialgebraic sets) of $	ext{APPT}_{m,n}$ and $	ext{ASEP}_{m,n}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions $m$ and $n$ of the bipartite system; the indices related to permutations in $S_{m,n}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing general geometric properties of absolute convex sets and utilizing advanced results concerning linear determinant preservers on symmetric matrices.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes general geometric properties for absolute convex matrix sets. It then applies these tools to characterize $	ext{APPT}_{m,n}$ as a spectrahedron and $	ext{ASEP}_{m,n}$ as semialgebraic. Finally, it uses permutation group theory and determinant polynomials to precisely map out the boundary structure of $	ext{APPT}_{m,n}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the geometric structure of the set of spectra of bipartite absolute separable states ($\mathrm{ASEP}_{m,n}$) and absolute positive partial transpose states ($\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$), i.e., bipartite quantum states that remain separable or PPT respectively, under all global unitary transformations. First, we establish general geometric properties of absolute convex sets of matrices, their spectra and extreme points. Regarding absolute separability, we present a permutation-symmetric reformulation of the absolute PPT criterion and use it to demonstrate that $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ is a spectrahedron for all $m\leq n$: in particular, all its faces are exposed. In contrast, while $\mathrm{ASEP}_{2,n}$ is also a spectrahedron, we prove that in general $\mathrm{ASEP}_{m,n}$ is a semialgebraic set for all $m\leq n$. Furthermore, we provide a complete characterization of the faces and extreme points of $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ and demonstrate that the dimension of a face is determined by the rank of a certain matrix, with maximal proper faces having dimension $(mn-m-1)$.   In the quantitative setting, we provide a rigorous lower bound on the maximal attainable purity of $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ via an inscribed polytope $\mathcal{P}_{m,n}$ and conjecture that the maximal purity of $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ (along with its spectra) coincides with the polytope for arbitrary dimensions except when $m=n=2$. Additionally, we also provide a rigorous upper bound on the minimal von Neumann entropy of $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ and demonstrate numerically that the minimum entropy eventually coincides with the polytope $\mathcal{P}_{m,n}$ as the local system dimension $n$ increases. Finally, we show that the relative spectral volume of $\mathrm{APPT}_{m,n}$ decays exponentially in $n$ by a constant multiplicative factor of the relative volume of the inscribed polytope $\mathcal{P}_{m,n}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a comprehensive method to characterize the fidelity of entangled single-photon resources emitted from quantum emitters. By measuring first-order coherence functions, researchers can reconstruct the full state and analytically derive Pauli error probabilities associated with subsequent fusion measurements. This provides crucial guidelines for improving photon sources for fault-tolerant quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally measurable pathway (via coherence functions) to quantify fundamental noise sources that limit fault-tolerant quantum computation in photonic platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a method to compute Pauli error rates for entangled single-photon resource states generated from quantum emitters, bridging continuous field measurements with discrete qubit error models.

**Main result.** Analytic expressions for Pauli error probabilities are derived purely as functions of experimentally measurable parameters like first-order coherence function values and transmission coefficients.

**Method.** The authors use first-order coherence measurements ($G^{(1)}$) to reconstruct the full quantum state ($\hat{ho}$) and then calculate stabilizer expectation values to determine the associated Pauli error maps for fusion operations.

**Model / system.** The system involves single-photon sources based on multi-level quantum emitters (three- or four-level), which generate entangled states used in photonic quantum computing circuits.

**Key observables.** First-order coherence function $G^{(1)}(x_1, x_2)$, Pauli error probabilities ($p_x, p_y, p_z$), and stabilizer expectation values ($\langle\hat{O}angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon noise parameters, transmission coefficient $\eta$, and coupling constants derived from emitter properties.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to measure $G^{(1)}$ to reconstruct the state; error calculations are performed up to first order in experimental parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate level schemes for three-level and four-level emitters, pulse sequences for generating entangled states (GHZ/cluster), and dual-rail fusion circuit setups.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing the emitted state using $G^{(1)}$ measurements to modeling the error process via Pauli maps applied during a fusion operation on multi-photon resource states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a scheme to compute Pauli error rates in photonics-based quantum error correction using experimental observables of single photons produced from quantum emitters. We show that first-order coherence measurements and first-order cross correlations, which can be implemented using photon counting, can extract single-photon and entangled single-photon wavefunctions in the presence of imperfections due to photon distinguishability, laser noise, and photon loss. Leveraging this, we show that the wavefunction of any entangled state of noisy photons produced from a single quantum emitter can be expressed in matrix-product-state form and can be used to analytically compute the expectation value of the stabilizer generators of the corresponding entangled state. From this we obtain analytic expressions for the Pauli error probabilities in terms of the photon noise parameters. Furthermore, we calculate Pauli error maps for entangled photons after undergoing Bell- state measurements in terms of these parameters. Our work provides a method to use experimental measurements to determine the required quality of photons produced from quantum emitters for fault-tolerant fusion-based photonics quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes rigorous lower bounds on entropy production by analyzing the asymmetry of two-time correlation functions. It develops separate forms of these bounds for steady-state and time-dependent non-equilibrium processes. The results are highly relevant for characterizing irreversibility in complex physical systems using only experimentally accessible measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful theoretical tools for quantifying irreversibility and dissipation in complex systems using only measurable correlation functions, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems where full state tomography is impossible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving rigorous lower bounds on entropy production (EPR) for systems where measurements are partial or coarse-grained.

**Main result.** Two key bounds are derived: one valid for Non-Equilibrium Steady States ($	ext{NESS}$) using arbitrary correlation lags, and another requiring the vanishing lag limit ($	au 	o 0$) for time-dependent processes.

**Method.** The analysis relies on exploiting the asymmetry of experimentally accessible two-time correlation functions between state observables ($A$ and $B$), relating this asymmetry to path weights and KL divergence bounds derived from fluctuation theorems.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to systems following Markovian dynamics or coupled overdamped Langevin equations. Examples include discrete-state Markov networks (e.g., four-state models) and continuous processes like particles on a ring potential.

**Key observables.** Entropy Production Rate ($	ext{EPR}$), two-time correlation function $C_{A,B}(t, 	au)$, and the correlation asymmetry $\chi_{A,B}(t, 	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Correlation lag ($	au$), time ($t$), system affinities, and potential amplitudes ($V_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The underlying dynamics must be Markovian on some microscopic level. For time-dependent processes, the bound derivation requires taking the limit of vanishing correlation lag ($	au 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the performance and optimization of the derived bounds (e.g., showing Quality Factors $Q_{A,B}(	au)$ vs $	au$ or affinities) for both NESS and time-dependent driving scenarios.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops two main results: a bound applicable to steady states ($\hat{\sigma}_{A,B}(	au) \le \sigma$) and a second bound derived in the vanishing lag limit ($\hat{\sigma}_{A,B}(t) \le \sigma(t)$). It then applies these bounds to various physical models (Markov networks, Langevin dynamics) to test their tightness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entropy production is a key property in stochastic thermodynamics. For partially observed and coarse-grained systems, its inference is challenging and typically rests on proven lower bounds. We derive two versions of such bounds based on the asymmetry of experimentally accessible two-time correlation functions of coarse-grained state observables. For non-equilibrium steady states, the bound is valid for arbitrary correlation lag. For time-dependent processes, it requires the limit of vanishing lag. These bounds hold true for any system that follows either a Markovian dynamics or a coupled set of overdamped Langevin equations on some underlying, unobservable level of description. We illustrate the bounds for both types of dynamics and discuss their optimization and potential tightness.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper resolves the conflict between observed particle localization in solids and the underlying translational symmetry of quantum mechanics. By quantizing the environment, the authors show that apparent symmetry breaking occurs only within localized 'decoherent branches,' while the total system state remains globally symmetric. This offers a finite-system resolution to long-standing issues in condensed matter theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum mechanical framework for understanding emergent classical-like phenomena, such as localization, using decoherence theory in a way that respects fundamental symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how localized centers of mass appear in solid materials, reconciling this observation with quantum mechanical descriptions that are fundamentally translationally symmetric.

**Main result.** Localization arises within decoherent branches when the environment is quantized, allowing the total state vector to remain globally translationally symmetric.

**Method.** The authors analyze the system-environment coupling by quantizing the environment and examining the reduced density matrix dynamics relative to the environment's position.

**Model / system.** The model involves $N$ interacting particles (the system) coupled to a harmonic oscillator environment. The Hamiltonian is analyzed in terms of center-of-mass and environmental degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Ground-state width ($\sigma_{	ext{pin}}$), reduced density matrix off-diagonal terms, and the relative state $|\psi(x)angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Environmental mass ($M_0$), coupling strength ($\mu$), and system size (finite vs. thermodynamic limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that quantizing the environment allows for a description where decoherence causes apparent symmetry breaking without violating global symmetries.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts conventional semiclassical approaches (relying on environmental potentials) with the novel approach of quantizing the environment. It develops mathematical tools to define relative states and shows how decoherence leads to localized branches while preserving global symmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solids appear to have localised centres of mass, yet many-body quantum theory describes them using translationally symmetric models that preclude localisation. Conventionally, this is resolved through spontaneous symmetry breaking by introducing an interaction with a semiclassical environment that breaks the symmetry. In the thermodynamic limit, the interaction can be removed while leaving the state localised. This, however, raises the question of how localisation arises outside the thermodynamic limit, i.e., in finite quantum systems (Wallace, 2018). Here, we show that, by quantising the environment, the localisation of finite systems occurs within decoherent branches, while the state vector of the composite system remains translationally symmetric. Our approach is analogous to the Page-Wootters construction (Page & Wootters, 1983) and quantum reference frames; moreover, we recover the semiclassical description as a limiting case while predicting experimentally distinguishable corrections away from this limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a fermionic lattice model called the Villain model that successfully incorporates exact chiral symmetries and anomalies on a lattice. By coupling Majorana fermions to bosonic fields, the authors overcome theoretical constraints and demonstrate methods like T-duality to achieve symmetric mass generation. This work provides a powerful framework for studying lattice chiral gauge theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QFT/Condensed Matter, the rigorous handling of topological invariants (anomalies) and symmetry protection in low dimensions is highly relevant for understanding superconducting phases or topological quantum materials studied using AMO techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is constructing a fermionic lattice Hamiltonian that exactly realizes chiral global symmetries and their associated anomalies while overcoming constraints like the Nielsen-Ninomiya no-go theorem.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct such a model, demonstrating anomaly cancellation and providing methods to generate mass symmetrically via T-duality mapping of six-fermion interactions.

**Method.** The analysis employs techniques from lattice field theory, including the use of Villain bosons coupled to Majorana fermions, chiral charge quantization, and fermionic T-duality transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is the 'fermionic Villain model,' which couples a Kitaev chain of Majorana fermions to bosonic Villain fields. The Hamiltonian is governed by Gauss law constraints that couple these bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Chiral charges ($Q_L, Q_R$), the $	ext{'t Hooft anomaly}$ (diagnosed via Chern-Simons terms or Schwinger terms), and the mass spectra of related models like the lattice Schwinger model.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis explores various limits: continuum limit, deep infrared limit ($k=0$), and varying coupling strengths ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the bosonic Villain fields being essential to evade no-go theorems; T-duality is used as a key mathematical tool for mapping interactions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the analysis involves deriving dispersion relations and effective Hamiltonians in various limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the model via its Hamiltonian and Gauss laws, analyzes chiral symmetries and anomalies using charge quantization, demonstrates mass generation through T-duality, and concludes with applications to specific lattice gauge theories.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a fermionic lattice Hamiltonian that exactly realizes the $\mathrm{U(1)}_\mathrm{L}\times \mathrm{U(1)}_\mathrm{R}$ global symmetry and the associated chiral anomalies of a massless Dirac fermion in 1+1 dimensions. The construction couples Villain bosons to a Kitaev chain of Majorana fermions, where the bosonic fields are essential for evading Nielsen-Ninomiya-type no-go theorems. For two copies of our model, we realize the anomaly-free $\mathrm{U(1)}_{3450}$ global symmetry and construct symmetric boundary conditions. We analytically demonstrate symmetric mass generation by mapping the symmetry-preserving six-fermion interactions to fermion bilinear terms using fermionic T-duality. Finally, by gauging general anomaly-free global symmetries, we obtain a broad class of lattice chiral gauge theories. As nontrivial applications, we compute the mass spectra of the lattice Schwinger model and the 3450 gauge theory, finding agreement with the corresponding continuum results.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to study strong optomechanical coupling at the single-molecule level using SERS enhanced by hybrid plasmonic cavities. By analyzing photon correlations, the authors show that nonclassical signatures like antibunching can be used to optically characterize anharmonic vibrational potentials in molecules. This advances the field toward quantum control of molecular vibrations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics with molecular spectroscopy by proposing a method to use single-photon statistics (antibunching) as an optical signature for anharmonicity, which is a core topic in advanced AMO physics and open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to achieve and characterize single-molecule strong optomechanical coupling within Surface-Enhanced Raman Scattering (SERS) regimes, overcoming limitations posed by weak interactions and sample damage at high intensities.

**Main result.** Strong coupling is demonstrated using detuned emitter-hybrid cavity configurations, allowing the observation of enhanced spectral splittings and nonclassical photon statistics that reveal anharmonic vibrational modifications at single-molecule levels.

**Method.** The study employs open-system quantum dynamics simulations using Lindblad master equations to model dissipation in a coupled system, supplemented by calculating second-order photon correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a hybrid plasmonic cavity coupled to fluorescent molecules. The system is modeled via an electron-vibron Hamiltonian incorporating coupling terms ($g_{om}$) and analyzed using master equations for dissipation ($\dot{\hat{ho}}$).

**Key observables.** Enhanced splittings in Stokes/anti-Stokes spectra, nonclassical photon statistics (bunching/antibunching), and violation of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong coupling regime where coherent energy exchange dominates dissipation; driving powers ($\Omega/2\pi \sim 1-10$ THz); cavity enhancement factor ($g_{hyb}/\kappa_{hyb}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Markovian description of decay into environmental baths is assumed, and the use of a detuned emitter-hybrid cavity configuration is crucial to avoid high intensities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hybrid cavity structure and energy levels; spectral maps show Stokes/anti-Stokes sidebands vs. driving strength ($\Omega$); $g^{(2)}$ maps compare resonant vs. cooperative models, highlighting nonclassical correlations for anharmonic vibrations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model (Hamiltonian/Master Equation), demonstrating how detuning enables strong coupling below damage thresholds, and finally using photon correlation measurements ($g^{(2)}$) to spectroscopically probe nonlinear vibrational potentials via antibunching signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify enhanced strong coherent Surface-Enhanced Raman Scattering (SERS) interactions facilitated by hybridized metallo-dielectric cavities with a resonant fluorescent molecule. By illuminating a detuned electronic transition near saturation via a narrowband hybrid plasmonic mode, we observe enhanced splittings in the Stokes and anti-Stokes spectra, revealing strong optomechanical coupling and nonlinear vibrational modifications at intensities far below irreversible SERS damage thresholds. We compute the second-order photon correlations that allows identifying the nonclassical character associated to these strong optomechanical interactions and SERS configurations that enables them to optically characterize the anharmonic character of the vibrational modes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an advanced experimental technique to achieve ultra-low relative intensity noise in high-power lasers using Acousto-Optic Modulators (AOMs). By integrating a sophisticated control scheme involving both feedback and feedforward paths, the system stabilizes laser intensity up to the MHz range. This capability is crucial for next-generation quantum experiments involving light ultracold atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical technical requirement for modern AMO experiments, such as optical lattice physics, where laser intensity stability is paramount for trapping and manipulating ultracold atoms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of high-power laser sources that maintain ultra-low relative intensity noise (RIN) while allowing dynamic control up to the MHz frequency range.

**Main result.** The authors achieved an ultra-low relative intensity noise level down to $-155 	ext{ dB Hz}^{-1}$ even at several hundred kHz by implementing a combined feedback and feedforward control system on an AOM platform.

**Method.** A novel control architecture combining two standard feedback loops with one feedforward branch, implemented using photodiodes for measurement and FPGA/analog electronics for signal processing.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses Acousto-Optic Modulators (AOMs) driven by RF mixers. The system incorporates a slow DC control loop for low-frequency stabilization and a fast AC loop, alongside feedforward compensation to suppress noise across a wide bandwidth.

**Key observables.** Relative Intensity Noise (RIN) spectrum, measured down to $-158 	ext{ dB Hz}^{-1}$ at the electronic limit; closed-loop bandwidth up to $1.2 	ext{ MHz}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise suppression of $>30 	ext{ dB}$; control bandwidth extending into the $	ext{MHz}$ range; AOM reaction times down to $65 	ext{ ns}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system relies on linearizing the non-linear AOM response using Gain-Scheduling and Piecewise Linear Approximation (PLA) algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, detailing beam paths for DC and AC control branches. Other figures compare RIN spectra across different control regimes (free-running vs. fully controlled).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of low-noise lasers, describes the physical AOM system, outlines the advanced signal processing combining feedback/feedforward loops, presents measurements showing noise suppression at high frequencies, and concludes with performance metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-power laser sources that exhibit low relative intensity noise and allow simultaneous dynamic control of their light level are required for a broad range of applications in various fields of physics. Acousto-optic modulators (AOMs) are widely used for active power stabilization and regulation due to their simple drive electronics requirements and high optical power handling capability in free-space. However, the rather slow propagation speed of the sound wave within the AOM crystal typically limits their control bandwidth to a few hundred kHz. In this work, we present a novel AOM-based control system that is capable of significantly suppressing intensity noise of high-power lasers up to the MHz range. By combining two standard feedback loops with one feedforward control branch and optimizing the beam path in the AOM crystal, ultra-low relative intensity noise levels down to $-155\, \text{dB}\,\text{Hz}^{-1}$ even at several hundred kHz are achieved. Our results are relevant for applications that require ultra-low intensity noise at Fourier frequencies up to the MHz range, such as optical lattice experiments with light ultracold atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details a method to generate high-quality, non-Gaussian photonic states by coupling a single quantum emitter to a waveguide. By employing squeezed vacuum inputs, the authors demonstrate the creation of bimodal states that allow for the extraction of complex resources like large squeezed cat states. This work advances quantum light sources beyond simple photon addition/subtraction protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum state engineering in photonic systems, providing concrete protocols for generating complex non-Gaussian resources (like cat states) necessary for fault-tolerant quantum computation and quantum metrology.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research focuses on deterministically and heraldedly generating non-Gaussian states of light using a single quantum emitter coupled to a chiral waveguide.

**Main result.** Using squeezed-vacuum inputs, the protocol generates bimodal non-Gaussian states from which higher-stellar-rank states, such as large squeezed cat states, can be extracted with high purity and success rates.

**Method.** The analysis employs virtual cavity formalism, linear mode rotation to analyze joint density matrices in different output bases, and utilizes fidelity witnesses combined with Wigner negativity to bound the stellar rank of generated states.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a two-level quantum emitter (TLS) coupled to a one-dimensional, ideally chiral waveguide. The interaction is modeled using an input-output relationship derived from the Hamiltonian describing the TLS and the field modes.

**Key observables.** Wigner negativity volume, Stellar rank ($r^*$), Purity of partial density matrices ($ho_{1,	heta}, ho_{2,	heta}$), and the core state $ho_{	ext{core}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling rates ($\gamma$), frequency separation ($\omega_0$), input field type (coherent vs. squeezed vacuum), and the ratio of average photon number to coupling strength ($\langle n angle\gamma	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial assumption is an ideal chiral waveguide; limitations are analyzed regarding imperfect coupling and non-chiral waveguides.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the protocol scheme using pulsed Gaussian states. Figure 2 shows properties of the reduced state $ho_1$ (Impurity, Wigner negativity volume) versus input amplitude $|\alpha|$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model and deterministic generation via coherent pulses; it then advances to utilizing squeezed-vacuum inputs to access higher-rank states; finally, it details advanced diagnostics like stellar rank bounding and discusses practical implementation considerations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the generation of deterministic and heralded non-Gaussian states of light, using a single two-level system coupled to a chiral waveguide. We study the case of a single two level system driven by pulsed coherent and squeezed drive in a chiral waveguide. For coherent input pulses, we show that the emitter can deterministically generate Wigner-negative states, albeit of limited rank. Going beyond, using squeezed-vacuum inputs, we show that the interaction produces bimodal non-Gaussian states from which higher-stellar-rank states, including large squeezed cat states, can be experimentally extracted with a substantial success rate. Motivated by experimental implementations, we further analyze the effect of imperfect coupling and of the intrinsic $50\%$ collection limit of symmetric, non-chiral waveguides. Finally, we propose a simple interferometric scheme that recovers an effectively chiral interaction in an otherwise bidirectional waveguide.

</details>

**Summary.** This work numerically analyzes chaos and diffusion in twisted bilayer graphene, demonstrating that the system exhibits quantum chaos even at large twist angles. The key finding is that border-induced scattering acts as a powerful single-particle mechanism, inducing diffusive behavior regardless of lattice commensurability. This highlights the crucial role of sample boundaries in determining electronic transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings on how structural imperfections (borders) induce universal quantum chaotic behavior in a 2D material are highly relevant to understanding open quantum systems where boundaries play a critical role.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of quantum chaos and diffusive transport mechanisms within finite-sized Twisted Bilayer Graphene (TBG) flakes.

**Main result.** Chaos is robustly induced in TBG systems regardless of lattice commensurability or rotation angle, with border scattering identified as a dominant single-particle mechanism causing diffusion.

**Method.** The analysis employs numerical diagonalization of the tight-binding Hamiltonian and uses Random Matrix Theory diagnostics (like level spacing ratios) alongside Thouless energy scaling to characterize spectral statistics.

**Model / system.** The system is Twisted Bilayer Graphene modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian. The study examines flakes with both commensurate and incommensurate twist angles, focusing on the role of boundaries.

**Key observables.** Level spacing ratios ($\langle r angle$), probability distribution $P(r)$, Thouless energy ($E_{Th}$), and dimensionless conductance ($g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), system size ($N$), and the nature of scattering (border-induced vs. intrinsic).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes random sliding between layers for comparison, and specific energy ranges are focused on for robust averaging.

**Figures summary.** Figures show level statistics ($\langle r angle$) dependence on angle/size, K-correlator plateaus confirming GOE behavior, and scaling plots demonstrating $E_{Th} \sim 1/\sqrt{N}$ consistent with border scattering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model (Hamiltonian), applying chaos diagnostics (level statistics vs. GOE/Poisson) across different angles, quantifying diffusion via Thouless energy scaling, and concluding that boundary effects dominate the chaotic behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We numerically analyze chaos and diffusion in experimentally-relevant models of twisted bilayer graphene. Our results indicate that finite systems at both commensurate and incommensurate rotation angles exhibit chaos. We compute the Thouless energy of the system at intermediate twist angles to further understand the diffusive processes associated with the chaotic nature of the spectrum. We find that the mean free path of the non-interacting electrons scales with the system size, which is consistent with diffusive processes caused by border-induced scattering. In summary, our results show the role of borders as a experimentally relevant single-particle scattering mechanism, which is much stronger than in single-layer graphene and induces chaos and diffusion regardless of the rotation angle and the lattice commensurability.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a general continuum hydrodynamic framework for complex proliferating active matter by deriving mean-field equations from a detailed lattice master equation. Using the von Mises ansatz, they successfully model collective behaviors like flocking and predict pattern formation instabilities (Turing/Hopf) that match numerical simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical active matter, the rigorous derivation of macroscopic field theories from microscopic stochastic rules, coupled with stability analysis (Turing/Hopf), mirrors techniques used in modeling non-equilibrium quantum many-body dynamics or open quantum systems far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a general continuum theory for proliferating active matter that incorporates self-propulsion, birth, death, and nonlocal competition.

**Main result.** The derived hydrodynamic framework successfully reproduces key dynamical regimes, including Turing and Hopf instabilities, confirming its applicability to diverse active systems.

**Method.** The authors derive mean-field evolution equations from a microscopic master equation by taking the continuum limit and employing a von Mises ansatz closure for angular correlations.

**Model / system.** The model describes self-propelled particles on a lattice undergoing stochastic processes (birth, death, competition) alongside directional hopping. The dynamics are mapped onto coupled hydrodynamic equations for particle density and polarization fields.

**Key observables.** Particle density ($ho$), Polarization vector field ($P$), Mean occupation numbers $\langle n_{	heta}^x angle$, and instability thresholds (Turing/Hopf).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Net growth rate ($\mu$), Péclet number ($Pe$), Diffusion coefficients ($D_T, D_R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations include the mean-field factorization of averages and the use of the von Mises ansatz to close the resulting hierarchy of moment equations.

**Figures summary.** Figure D1 shows simulation results for a quasi-one-dimensional system, demonstrating hysteretic behavior (bistability) when sweeping parameters like the growth rate ($\mu$) across instability lines for specific Péclet numbers ($Pe=5$ and $Pe=7.5$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the microscopic lattice model via a master equation to deriving mean-field continuum equations, applying the von Mises closure to obtain coupled hydrodynamic equations for density ($ho$) and polarization ($P$), and finally analyzing these equations in simplified 1D systems to study pattern formation and bistability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a continuum theory for proliferating active matter starting from a microscopic stochastic model of self-propelled particles undergoing birth, death, and nonlocal competition. Beginning from the master equation, we derive mean-field evolution equations for the particle density and polarization fields and close the resulting hierarchy through a von Mises ansatz, providing a coupled hydrodynamic description applicable to a broad class of proliferating active systems. As an application, we study the recently introduced Active Brownian Bug model, in which the form of flocking emerges despite the absence of explicit alignment interactions. Linear stability analysis predicts both Turing and Hopf instabilities, whose analytical thresholds agree with numerical simulations. The continuum model reproduces the principal dynamical regimes of the underlying particle system, including homogeneous states, stationary periodic clusters, and coherently propagating flocking states. These results establish a general continuum framework for proliferating active matter and provide a physical interpretation of collective motion driven by the interplay between activity and population dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the behavior of anomalous dimensions associated with line defects in conformal field theories when moving from Euclidean to Lorentzian spacetime. The key finding is that under certain assumptions, this dimension stabilizes at a constant value determined by endpoint operator scaling dimensions, providing new insights into non-perturbative physics beyond standard gauge theory predictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on high-energy QFT/CFT, the techniques of analyzing correlation functions near singular limits (like cusps) and deriving universal scaling laws are highly relevant to understanding critical points or boundary effects in strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to determine how the cusp anomalous dimension behaves for general conformal line defects when analytically continued from Euclidean to Lorentzian spacetime signatures.

**Main result.** For general CFTs, the large-boost limit of the Lorentzian cusp anomalous dimension is found to be fixed by the scaling dimensions of local endpoint operators, differing significantly from standard gauge theory results.

**Method.** The authors employ analytic continuation techniques, moving from Euclidean calculations involving line defects to Lorentzian configurations like the Sudakov or quench limits. They derive expressions for the anomalous dimension based on assumptions about local endpoint operators.

**Model / system.** The study is set in the context of Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) and involves analyzing cusps formed by two meeting line defects, with applications tested using models like pinning-field defects or spin impurities. The analysis draws parallels to gauge theories.

**Key observables.** Cusp anomalous dimension ($\Gamma_{ab}(	heta)$ in Euclidean space; $\Gamma^L_{ab}(y)$ in Lorentzian space), and the scaling dimensions of defect-creation operators ($\Delta_{a0}, \Delta_{b0}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The boost parameter ($y$), the coupling constants, and the scaling dimensions associated with the defects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A crucial assumption is that the line defects admit local endpoint operators connecting them to the trivial line; otherwise, the physics changes significantly (e.g., flux tube formation).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate both Euclidean and Lorentzian configurations of cusped defects, including specific setups like the Sudakov configuration.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general problem by contrasting with known gauge theory results, derives the large-boost limit based on endpoint operator assumptions, analyzes distinct analytic continuations, proposes a positivity bound, and tests these predictions in several perturbative examples.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The lightlike Wilson line cusp underlies the Sudakov double logarithm and is a central ingredient in gauge-theory factorization. We ask what replaces this behavior for general conformal line defects in Lorentzian signature. We argue that, when the defects admit local endpoint operators, the cusp anomalous dimension has a finite large-boost limit fixed by the scaling dimensions of the corresponding defect-creation operators. We further analyze the distinct analytic continuations of the cusp, elucidate their physical interpretations, and propose a positivity bound on the Lorentzian cusp anomalous dimension. We test these predictions in several perturbative examples, including pinning-field defects and spin impurities, and discuss their possible implications for gauge theories.

</details>
