**Summary.** The paper develops a theory showing how to maintain Heisenberg scaling in quantum frequency estimation despite classical directional noise. This immunity is achieved by engineering the initial quantum state such that the signal encoding direction is geometrically orthogonal to the noise diffusion direction. The results confirm this robustness, while also quantifying the performance degradation when the noise environment is only partially known.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physically realizable protocol for enhancing quantum sensing beyond the Standard Quantum Limit by actively designing input states that are robust against specific types of environmental noise, which is highly relevant to trapped-ion and cavity QED platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is achieving Heisenberg scaling ($\Delta\hat{b} \propto N^{-1}$) in quantum parameter estimation when the system is subjected to classical collective directional noise, especially when the signal and noise axes are non-parallel.

**Main result.** A geometric noise-immunity mechanism was identified: by designing an appropriate input state (One-Axis-Twisted State), the signal encoding can be made metric-orthogonal to the noise diffusion direction, preserving Heisenberg scaling regardless of noise temporal correlations.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Holstein-Primakoff approximation to map the spin system to bosonic quadratures and employs the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) derived from the Wigner function evolution under noise averaging.

**Model / system.** The model involves an ensemble of N effective two-level systems whose collective spin operators $\mathbf{J}$ are used to estimate a static frequency $b$. The noise is modeled as classical, Gaussian, and acting along a fixed direction $\hat{\mathbf{v}}$.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), estimation uncertainty ($\Delta\hat{b}$), Wigner function ($W(x, p)$), and the optimal measurement quadrature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Probe number $N$, noise correlation time $	au$ (governing Markovian/non-Markovian regimes), signal axis angle $	heta$, and noise direction $\hat{\mathbf{v}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Holstein-Primakoff approximation for tractability, assuming Gaussian statistics for the noise, and analyzing small deviations from perfect knowledge of the noise axis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates geometric protection by showing how optimal state design keeps the QCRB insensitive to noise diffusion. Figure 2 demonstrates that Heisenberg scaling is maintained for all noise correlation types when full noise knowledge is assumed, but degrades if the noise axis is imperfectly known.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of directional noise limiting metrology. It then introduces the geometric immunity mechanism using OATS states to achieve metric-orthogonality between signal and noise directions. Finally, it analyzes the breakdown of this scaling when the noise direction knowledge becomes imperfect.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-squeezed states enable entanglement-enhanced frequency estimation; however, the achievable performance is limited in practice by decoherence. We study the problem of parameter estimation under \emph{classical collective noise} that acts along a \emph{fixed} direction during signal encoding. While we restrict our analysis to Gaussian noise statistics, no assumption is made on the nature of the noise temporal correlations. Our approach captures both parallel (dephasing) and single-axis transverse noise as special cases, and covers both Markovian and non-Markovian metrological regimes. In the properly squeezed limit, where a Holstein-Primakoff description is accurate, we identify a geometric noise-immunity mechanism: for \emph{known} non-parallel signal and noise axes, an appropriate one-axis-twisted input encodes the signal in a quadrature that is metric-orthogonal to the direction of noise-induced diffusion. Irrespective of the noise temporal correlations, the resulting estimation precision exhibits Heisenberg scaling in the probe number. Optimal performance is achievable by measuring a single collective spin component. Imperfect knowledge of the noise axis produces a crossover from Heisenberg scaling at moderate probe number back to the known collective-dephasing bounds asymptotically.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates metastable helical magnetic states that emerge when a chiral magnet is interfaced with two uniaxial ferromagnets. The authors identify the stabilization mechanism as 'dynamical topological protection' and show that these states can be manipulated by applying electric currents or magnetic fields. This makes the system highly promising for advanced spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study of topologically protected non-equilibrium states and switching mechanisms using current injection shares conceptual links with controlling quantum states in engineered solid-state systems relevant to spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To elucidate the topological origin of stabilization for metastable helical states in monoaxial chiral magnets when they are placed in contact with two uniaxial ferromagnets.

**Main result.** The system exhibits metastable helical states stabilized by a mechanism termed dynamical topological protection, allowing switching between these states using magnetic fields and polarized currents.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving the derivation of energy functionals and solving the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation numerically for both static and dynamic configurations.

**Model / system.** A composite magnet consisting of a central monoaxial chiral magnet sandwiched between two identical uniaxial ferromagnets. The dynamics are governed by the LLG equation incorporating exchange, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI), and anisotropy terms.

**Key observables.** Helicity (wave number) of helical states, winding number density distribution, and stability spectrum derived from linearized LLG equations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chiral magnet parameters ($A_c, D, K_c$) and ferromagnet parameters ($\mu_u, A_u, K_u$); the helicity $p$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is assumed to have infinite dimensions in $\hat{x}$ and $\hat{y}$; stability analysis relies on linearizing the LLG equation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the composite chiral magnet structure, showing the relative placement of the chiral and ferromagnetic regions along the z-axis.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model (composite magnet), derives the relevant energy functional and dynamics (LLG equation), analyzes the topological origin of metastable states, and investigates their static/dynamic response to external fields and currents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The equilibrium state of a monoaxial chiral magnet surrounded by a non-magnetic medium (such as air or vacuum) is a helical texture characterized by a single, well-defined wave vector. No metastable states have ever been observed in such systems. Recently, however, it was demonstrated that when a chiral magnet is in close contact with two uniaxial ferromagnets, a large number of metastable helical states emerge in addition to the equilibrium state [Phys. Rev. B 109, 214424]. These helical states are distinguished by their wave number (helicity). In the present work, we elucidate the topological origin of the stabilization of these states --a mechanism we term dynamical topological protection-- and investigate their static and dynamic properties. We find that, as a consequence of this dynamical topological protection, the winding number of the helical states remains constant under the application of sufficiently weak magnetic fields and polarized electric currents. Furthermore, when a polarized current is applied to a metastable helical state, a static configuration is reached. This state retains the original winding number, but its winding number density becomes non-homogeneously distributed, concentrating near the interface with one of the ferromagnets. The dynamic response to sufficiently large magnetic fields and currents provides mechanisms to switch between different helical states. Since the magnetic properties depend on helicity, these metastable helical states are highly promising for applications in spintronics and magnonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work addresses the puzzling normal state of high-Tc superconductors by modeling it using poorly screened, non-local repulsive interactions. The authors derive a scale-covariant quasiparticle decay rate whose exponent depends continuously on doping via an interaction parameter $\alpha$. This framework successfully unifies observations across different doping regimes without requiring quantum criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on nonlocality as an alternative mechanism for anomalous transport properties is highly relevant to understanding strongly correlated electron systems where simple Fermi liquid descriptions fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a theory explaining the scale-covariant, doping-dependent normal state of high-$T_c$ strange metals without invoking quantum criticality.

**Main result.** The authors propose that poorly screened, non-local effective repulsions $V_\alpha(r) \sim 1/r^\alpha$ naturally explain the observed power-law decay rate $\Gamma(\omega, T) \propto T^\gamma$, where $\gamma = 2 - 1/\alpha$.

**Method.** The theory uses a hydrodynamic screening phenomenology and calculates the inelastic quasiparticle decay rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{inel}}$) via diagrammatic techniques within the G0W approximation.

**Model / system.** The system is high-$T_c$ superconductors exhibiting strange metal behavior. The model incorporates an interaction potential whose spatial nonlocality is parameterized by a doping-dependent exponent $\alpha$ ($1 \le \alpha \le 3$).

**Key observables.** Scale-covariant quasiparticle decay rate ($\Gamma(\omega, T)$), quasiparticle residue ($Z_k$), and the effective spatial nonlocality probed by $\alpha(p)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping level (controlling $\alpha$), temperature $T$, energy $\omega$, and the exponent $\alpha$ itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on heuristic assumptions for charge relaxation (continuity law) and approximations concerning momentum transfer ($q \ll k_F$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 proposes a phase diagram showing that optimal and overdoped regimes are captured by $1 \le \alpha \le 2$ using poorly screened repulsions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of scale-covariant self-energy in strange metals. It then develops the hydrodynamic screening phenomenology, calculates $\Gamma_{	ext{inel}}$ using linear response theory, and finally derives the scaling relation $\gamma = 2 - 1/\alpha$ to unify different doping regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Experiments in recent years on high $T_c$ superconductors find a puzzling nodal scale-covariant self-energy with an exponent varying continuously with doping. We propose a mechanism: nonlocality induced by poorly screened effective repulsions $V_α(r) \sim 1/r^α$, where a continuously doping-dependent exponent $1 \le α\le 3$ naturally interpolates between the Mott insulating and Fermi liquid limits. We develop a phenomenology of hydrodynamic screening, finding a scale-covariant quasiparticle decay rate $Γ(ω,T) \propto T^γ Φ(ω/T)$ in energy $ω$ and temperature $T$, with $γ= 2-\frac{1}α$ for nonlocal $ 1 < α< 2$. Our results naturally capture the optimally doped to overdoped regimes, whereas the underdoped regime is qualitatively distinct. In our theory, spectroscopy-fitted exponents directly probe the charged fluid's effective spatial nonlocality. Nonlocality shows that quantum criticality is not necessary to explain scale-covariant phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates emergent surface altermagnetism in KV2Se2O by reconciling conflicting experimental data. The authors predict that the unique signature of this phenomenon is a large, symmetry-protected nonlinear Edelstein response localized at the material's surface. They utilize advanced DFT and transport formalisms to calculate these spin responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the detailed calculation of transport coefficients ($\chi^{(2)}$) using advanced many-body formalisms (Boltzmann equation expansion) provides rigorous theoretical tools applicable to understanding spin-charge coupling phenomena in correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to demonstrate and reconcile the concept of emergent surface altermagnetism in KV2Se2O, explaining conflicting experimental observations regarding bulk antiferromagnetism versus d-wave spin splitting.

**Main result.** The (001) surface of antiferromagnetically ordered KV2Se2O exhibits d-wave altermagnetism, which is predicted to manifest as a large nonlinear Edelstein response localized at the surface.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations using Density Functional Theory (DFT) combined with advanced techniques like Wannier function renormalization and Boltzmann transport formalism were employed to calculate electronic structure and spin responses.

**Model / system.** The physical system is KV2Se2O, a layered material exhibiting Lieb lattice geometry. The analysis models the surface using finite slab calculations, considering different terminations (VO vs SeK).

**Key observables.** Surface spectral function difference ($\Delta G$), nonlinear Edelstein response ($\chi_{zxx}^{(2)}$), and spin-splitting in electronic band structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to surface termination, the number of layers ($N_{	ext{slab}}$), and the magnetic ordering (AM vs AFM).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include using a constant relaxation time ($	au$) for transport calculations and relying on DFT/Wannier function methods for electronic structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate surface spectral functions for different magnetic orderings, the slab model setup, and plots showing how the nonlinear Edelstein tensor $\chi^{(2)}$ varies with the number of layers ($N_{	ext{slab}}$) and termination type.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using DFT/Wannier tools to calculate surface spectral functions. It then applies the Boltzmann formalism to derive symmetry-constrained expressions for linear and nonlinear Edelstein tensors, culminating in predictions for measurable signatures like $\chi^{(2)}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate the recent concept of emergent surface altermagnetism through its unique signatures in \KVSO. We show that for bulk antiferromagnetically ordered \KVSO, the (001) surface exhibits $d$-wave altermagnetism. Our results fully explain the recent seemingly contradicgting experimental evidence, independently showing both an antiferromagnetically ordered bulk from neutron diffraction, and $d$-wave spin splitting from photoemission spectroscopy. To fully verify this conecept, we predict, as a key experimental signature, a large nonlinear Edelstein response, which is localized at the surface, and follows the $d$-wave altermagnetic symmetry. These results are not only relevant for the metallic and room-temperature magnet \KVSO, but also for several other Lieb lattice systems. Our work expands the pool of techniques that can be used to detect altermagnetism emerging at the surfaces of antiferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes two-dimensional classical lattice models at criticality when subjected to slow spatial variations. It demonstrates that these inhomogeneous systems are governed by Conformal Field Theory defined on a curved space, successfully deriving the underlying metric for both the Ising and six vertex models. This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework connecting lattice statistics, CFT, and geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between lattice statistical mechanics models (like the Ising model) and continuum field theories in curved spacetime provides a powerful tool for understanding how geometry influences critical phenomena, which is relevant to effective Hamiltonians in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how critical behavior of two-dimensional classical lattice models is modified by slow, spatially varying (inhomogeneous) deformations.

**Main result.** Such inhomogeneous systems are shown to be described by a Conformal Field Theory formulated on a curved space, allowing the identification of the underlying space metric.

**Method.** The analysis combines techniques from Conformal Field Theory (CFT), free fermion mapping via Jordan-Wigner transformation, and hydrodynamic approaches for specific cases.

**Model / system.** The primary models are 2D classical lattice systems, specifically the Ising model and the six vertex model with domain wall boundary conditions. The Hamiltonian is defined by local coupling constants that vary slowly across the lattice.

**Key observables.** Spin correlation functions, the energy gap ($\Delta E$), and the 'arctic curve' separating critical fluctuating regions from ordered phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($K, K'$), the deformation function $f(x/N)$, and the system size $N$ (in the large $N$ limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The deformations are assumed to vary slowly at lattice scales while crucially preserving the underlying critical behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram of the Ising model, show the structure of the inhomogeneous lattice couplings, and provide numerical comparisons between calculated gaps/correlations and CFT predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general framework using CFT for slow deformations to applying this formalism specifically to the 2D Ising model, deriving the curved space metric, and finally solving specific cases like the six vertex model using hydrodynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study simple inhomogeneous deformations of known two-dimensional classical lattice models described by conformal field theory (CFT) at large distances. The deformations are chosen to vary slowly at lattice scales, while preserving critical behavior. Globally, we find that such systems are described by a CFT in curved space, and identify the underlying space metric. Our two examples are the Ising model and the six vertex model with domain wall boundary conditions. In the latter boundaries are also inhomogeneous, which complicates the analysis. We nevertheless solve the free case using hydrodynamics. In the presence of interactions we determine the arctic curve which separates a critical fluctuating region from an ordered (frozen) phase.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an FNO-based framework to overcome the exponential computational cost of quantum optimal control. By using a fast surrogate model trained on molecular dynamics, they develop a planner that efficiently designs pulse sequences to purify complex molecular states in trapped ions. This significantly accelerates state preparation protocols compared to traditional optimization or reinforcement learning methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational scaling limits in quantum control theory by replacing expensive numerical solvers with data-driven neural operators, making previously intractable large-scale molecular simulations feasible for optimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is performing inverse design for quantum optimal control sequences, which is computationally prohibitive due to the exponential growth of complexity with Hilbert space dimension.

**Main result.** The proposed FNO-SPMP framework achieves state purification in a large molecular subspace ($888$-dimensional $	ext{H}_3	ext{O}^+$) significantly faster and more reliably than reinforcement learning baselines, reducing generation time from hours to minutes.

**Method.** The authors use a Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) as a fast, differentiable surrogate model to predict molecular-motional population dynamics. This is integrated into an iterative stochastic planner (FNO-SPMP) for inverse design.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the hydronium ($	ext{H}_3	ext{O}^+$) molecule coupled with a logic ion ($	ext{Ca}^+$) in a trapped-ion setup. The dynamics are modeled by tracking basis-state populations within an 888-dimensional subspace.

**Key observables.** Target-state population (aiming for $\geq 0.98$), sequence success rate, and the time required to generate the control pulse sequence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is studied at $20 	ext{ K}$, involving control parameters like laser frequency ($\omega$), pulse duration ($	au$), and polarization ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include restricting the motional mode to $
u \in \{0, 1\}$ and relying on measurement/cooling steps that reset coherences to allow a population-based description.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the full FNO-based inverse design pipeline for quantum control in $	ext{H}_3	ext{O}^+$, detailing the Hamiltonian setup, single-pulse prediction via FNO, and the iterative planning loop.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of quantum optimal control. It then introduces the FNO surrogate model to accelerate dynamics simulation. Finally, it details the FNO-SPMP protocol for inverse design, benchmarking its performance against RL methods on the $	ext{H}_3	ext{O}^+$ system.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum optimal control is a key tool for steering quantum dynamics, but its computational cost grows rapidly with the Hilbert space dimension. Here, we introduce a Fourier Neural Operator (FNO)-based framework for learning high dimensional molecular quantum dynamics and accelerating the inverse design of control protocols. Given an initial molecular population distribution, laser frequency, and polarization, the FNO predicts molecular-motional population dynamics up to $10^7$ times faster than GPU-accelerated numerical propagation with CUDA-Q Dynamics. Using this fast and differentiable surrogate, we develop the FNO stochastic pulse-measurement planner (FNO-SPMP), which constructs pulse sequences to purify an initially mixed Boltzmann distribution. We demonstrate the protocol in an 888-dimensional subspace of the hydronium molecule at 20 K, achieving a target-state population of 0.98 with a sequence success rate of up to 86.2%. In a shared discrete control space, FNO-SPMP achieves nearly twice the success rate of a reinforcement-learning baseline while using roughly half as many quantum control pulses and reducing pulse-sequence generation time from approximately 10 hours to 10-20 minutes. These results show that operator-learning surrogates can enable inverse design in quantum systems whose Hilbert spaces are too large for conventional direct optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates superconductivity in the Hofstadter-Hubbard model, focusing on how doping across a quantum critical point influences pairing. Using Neural Quantum States, the authors find that while the pairing magnitude is stable, the superfluid stiffness strongly peaks near the transition, suggesting that proximity to criticality enhances superconducting mobility more than it affects pair formation itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology—using NQS to probe quantum criticality in strongly correlated models—provides a powerful computational framework applicable to understanding exotic superconducting mechanisms driven by competing orders.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses how a parent insulating state dictates the superconductivity that emerges when the system is doped away from an insulator.

**Main result.** Superconductivity in the Hofstadter-Hubbard model is governed not by the nature of the parent state, but by proximity to the quantum critical point separating two distinct phases.

**Method.** The authors employed Neural Quantum States (NQS) trained on variational wavefunctions to simulate the system's ground state properties across various doping levels and interaction strengths.

**Model / system.** The physical model is the triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model with a $\pi/2$ flux per plaquette. At half filling, it exhibits two parent states: an integer quantum Hall insulator (IQH) and a chiral spin liquid (CSL), separated by a topological phase transition.

**Key observables.** Superfluid stiffness ($\mathcal{D}_s$), pairing order parameter ($\Delta$), $2e$ charge gap ($\Delta_{2e}$), and structure factors ($C^{\wedge}_n(k)$, $C^{\wedge}_{Sz}(k)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), doping level ($\delta$), and the proximity to the quantum critical point (QCP) separating IQH and CSL phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on NQS being a powerful tool for capturing long-range electronic correlations, and that observables like superfluid stiffness can be accurately extracted via flux threading or finite differences.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the continuous nature of the IQH–CSL transition (vanishing gap), the enhancement of superfluid stiffness near the QCP, and the distinct behavior of pairing order parameter versus stiffness across this critical region.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model by first characterizing the parent insulating phases via topological invariants (winding numbers) and then investigating superconductivity upon doping. Key diagnostics include measuring gap closing, analyzing structure factors for critical fluctuations, and comparing the response of pairing order vs. superfluid stiffness near the QCP.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how a parent insulating state shapes the superconductivity that emerges upon doping is a long-standing problem dating back to Anderson's resonating-valence-bond proposal. The triangular-lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model with $π/2$ flux per plaquette offers an ideal setting for this question: at half filling it hosts two distinct parent states---an integer quantum Hall insulator at weak coupling and a chiral spin liquid at intermediate coupling---separated by a topological phase transition. Using neural quantum states on tori of up to $432$ sites, we present strong evidence that the transition is continuous, with a vanishing $2e$ charge gap and critical charge fluctuations. Upon doping, we find a topological superconductor with off-diagonal long-range order consistent with $d+id$ pairing on either side of the transition. The two ingredients of superconductivity, pair formation and phase coherence, respond to the parent state in sharply different ways: while the pairing order parameter remains nearly unchanged across the transition, the superfluid stiffness is strongly enhanced near the critical point. The energy scale of the superconductor is therefore set not by which parent state is doped, but by proximity to the transition between them. Our results establish neural quantum states as a powerful tool for understanding the delicate interplay between long-ranged electronic correlations and superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that by exploiting the Kerr nonlinearity inherent in superconducting microwave cavities, they can passively stabilize resonance frequency fluctuations. This 'Kerr locking' technique suppresses noise by two orders of magnitude without requiring active feedback loops. The method offers a general approach for stabilizing various nonlinear resonators used across quantum sensing and computing platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates a purely nonlinear, passive method for stabilizing high-quality microwave resonators, which is crucial for building sensitive quantum sensors and improving readout fidelity in superconducting quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Resonance-frequency fluctuations limit the sensitivity of superconducting microwave cavities used for various quantum measurements.

**Main result.** The intrinsic Kerr nonlinearity provides a passive feedback mechanism that suppresses cavity frequency noise by nearly two orders of magnitude, reaching the $1/f$ noise floor without active control.

**Method.** Two-tone spectroscopy and Allan deviation analysis were used to experimentally characterize the nonlinear feedback and measure temporal stability.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting microwave cavity incorporating a flux-tunable transmon qubit, which induces a Kerr nonlinearity ($K_c$). The stabilization mechanism involves locking the noisy cavity mode to a strong pump tone.

**Key observables.** Frequency fluctuations ($\sigma_{std}$), Allan deviation ($\sigma_f(	au)$), and the transfer function $T(\omega_m)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump detuning ($\Delta_p$), Kerr nonlinearity coefficient ($K_c$), cavity linewidth ($\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stabilization relies on operating in a regime where the intrinsic nonlinear feedback dominates over external noise sources.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectroscopy response before and after locking, showing frequency stabilization; time dynamics plots compare standard deviations (MHz to kHz); and Allan deviation plots quantify the noise floor reduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of cavity noise limitation. It then details the Kerr-locking mechanism using theoretical modeling (Bogoliubov modes) and experimental characterization via two-tone spectroscopy, culminating in quantitative analysis of noise suppression using Allan deviation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Resonance-frequency fluctuations can limit the sensitivity and stability of superconducting microwave cavities used for qubit readout, optomechanical displacement sensing, and magnetic flux detection. Here, we demonstrate the suppression of resonance-frequency fluctuations by locking a noisy nonlinear superconducting microwave cavity to a strong pump tone. The Kerr nonlinearity of this system, whereby the resonance frequency depends on the intracavity field amplitude, gives rise to an intrinsic feedback mechanism that enables passive stabilization without active external feedback. Using two-tone spectroscopy, we experimentally characterize the intrinsic nonlinear feedback mechanism and investigate its temporal stability through Allan deviation analysis. The frequency fluctuations of the locked cavity mode are reduced by nearly two orders of magnitude, reaching the $1/f$ noise floor, without continuous frequency tracking or active control. Kerr locking provides a general approach for self-stabilizing nonlinear microwave resonators by suppressing low-frequency cavity noise while preserving sensitivity to signals outside the locking bandwidth. This approach may benefit a broad range of systems, including SQUID-based resonators, optomechanical devices, and parametric amplifiers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical numerical challenge of initializing non-Fourier heat conduction models like MCV. By comparing three initialization methods against an analytical solution, the authors prove that assuming zero or uniform initial derivatives introduces severe unphysical oscillations. They establish a thermodynamically consistent technique using the exact space-dependent derivative to ensure accurate simulation of transient thermal wave propagation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical heat transfer, the rigorous mathematical treatment of initial conditions and the distinction between physical dynamics and numerical artifacts is highly relevant to any field involving time evolution or non-equilibrium states, including quantum quench dynamics simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is mathematically defining the initial time derivative for non-equilibrium states when numerically solving the Maxwell--Cattaneo--Vernotte (MCV) heat conduction model.

**Main result.** Using an initialization strategy that maps the exact space-dependent derivative preserves local thermodynamic structure, yielding robust transient responses matching analytical benchmarks without introducing unphysical oscillations seen with zero or uniform assumptions.

**Method.** The authors compare three initialization strategies—zero derivative, uniform non-zero derivative, and exact space-dependent derivative—by solving the coupled MCV equations using an explicit staggered finite-difference scheme against a Galerkin analytical solution.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional transient heat conduction governed by the MCV model, which is a non-Fourier extension incorporating a memory-type constitutive relationship for heat flux. The system is analyzed under adiabatic boundary conditions with an exponentially distributed initial temperature field.

**Key observables.** Transient responses of temperature (T) and heat flux (q); quantitative error percentages ($\varepsilon_{\vartheta}, \varepsilon_{\hat{q}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time ($	au$), thermal conductivity ($\lambda$), density ($ho$), specific heat capacity ($c$); the non-dimensional relaxation parameter $\hat{	au}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes constant transport coefficients and focuses on 1D conduction; the rigorous initialization technique is shown to be computationally inexpensive relative to time-stepping.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare numerical simulations (using different initial derivative assumptions) against an analytical reference solution for various relaxation times ($\hat{	au}$), demonstrating artifact generation from incorrect initialization.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically evaluates three initialization methods, compares the resulting transient solutions to a known analytical benchmark derived via Galerkin's method, and concludes by establishing a thermodynamically consistent procedure applicable to higher-order models like Guyer–Krumhansl.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper investigates the numerical initialization of the one-dimensional Maxwell--Cattaneo--Vernotte (MCV) heat conduction model, addressing the critical mathematical challenge of defining the initial time derivative for non-equilibrium states. In modern high-frequency thermal engineering applications, traditional time-integration schemes and commercial finite-element solvers frequently introduce severe numerical artifacts when handling non-Fourier models. This study systematically evaluates three initialization strategies: a zero derivative, a spatially uniform non-zero derivative, and an exact space-dependent derivative. Using an explicit staggered finite-difference scheme, the transient responses to an exponentially distributed initial temperature field under adiabatic boundary conditions are compared against an analytical solution using the Galerkin method. The results demonstrate that assuming a zero or spatially uniform initial derivative introduces significant unphysical oscillatory deviations, leading to heat-flux prediction errors as the relaxation time increases. Conversely, mapping the exact space-dependent derivative onto the staggered grid preserves the local thermodynamic structure of the initial state, yielding robust transient responses that match the analytical benchmark without meaningful computational overhead. These findings establish a thermodynamically consistent initialization technique that can be extended to non-local models as well.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses advanced X-ray scattering techniques on $(	ext{Fe}_{0.63}	ext{Ni}_{0.3}	ext{Pd}_{0.07})_3	ext{P}$ to demonstrate precise control over magnetic stripe domains using in-plane fields. Crucially, the study reveals that cooling the material causes a temperature-dependent rotation of the effective Dzyalinskii-Moriya interaction landscape, establishing it as a model system for studying chiral spin textures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental magnetism, the precise control over emergent magnetic order parameters (like DMI orientation) using external fields and temperature provides insights into symmetry breaking and phase transitions relevant to quantum materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how applying external vector magnetic fields and varying temperature affects the complex topological spin textures, specifically stripe domains, in a noncentrosymmetric magnet.

**Main result.** Vector-field control allows continuous rotation of stripe domain orientation from chiral to achiral states at room temperature, while cooling reveals a temperature-driven evolution of anisotropic interactions, including the effective Dzyalinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI).

**Method.** The research combines resonant small-angle x-ray scattering (SAXS) and ptychography measurements performed in controlled vector magnetic fields over a wide temperature range.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the noncentrosymmetric material $(	ext{Fe}_{0.63}	ext{Ni}_{0.3}	ext{Pd}_{0.07})_3	ext{P}$, which exhibits $S_4$ symmetry and hosts various topological spin textures like stripe domains and skyrmion lattices.

**Key observables.** Magnetic modulation vector ($\mathbf{q}$), stripe domain orientation, SAXS harmonics ($1q, 3q, \dots$), and the dependence of $\mathbf{q}$ on applied field angle ($	heta$) and temperature ($T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (20 K to 300 K); weak in-plane magnetic fields ($\sim 10 	ext{ mT}$); basal plane anisotropy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the observed phenomena using combined effects of magnetocrystalline anisotropy, anisotropic exchange, and DMI within the $S4$ symmetry framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic structures, SAXS patterns under varying out-of-plane fields (showing transitions between domain types), and element-resolved SAXS maps confirming magnetic modulation in Ni moments.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the material's properties using various scattering techniques, then demonstrating deterministic vector-field control of stripe domains at room temperature, followed by a detailed analysis showing how cooling reveals complex, temperature-dependent anisotropy axes related to DMI evolution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

(Fe$_{0.63}$Ni$_{0.3}$Pd$_{0.07}$)$_3$P is a room-temperature magnet with $S_4$ symmetry that hosts a rich variety of topological spin textures. Here, we report a combined resonant small-angle x-ray scattering and ptychography study of (Fe$_{0.63}$Ni$_{0.3}$Pd$_{0.07}$)$_3$P in vector magnetic fields over a broad temperature range. We demonstrate deterministic vector-field control of magnetic stripe domains, where in-plane fields continuously rotate their orientation via a transition from a chiral stripe to an achiral fan configuration. Furthermore, at 50 K and below, the stripe orientation becomes metastably pinned and retains its field-trained direction. While the magnitude of the wavevector is nearly isotropic within the basal plane at room temperature, a pronounced temperature evolution of anisotropic interactions emerges upon cooling. In particular, non-trivial anisotropy axes develop at 20-50 K reflecting the combined effects of magnetocrystalline anisotropy, anisotropic exchange, and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI), whose effective orientation is found to rotate with temperature. These results establish (Fe$_{0.63}$Ni$_{0.3}$Pd$_{0.07}$)$_3$P as a model system for vector-field control of chiral spin textures and reveal a previously unrecognized temperature-driven evolution of the effective DMI landscape in a noncentrosymmetric magnet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory for enhancing phase estimation sensitivity in optical interferometers using multiphoton states. It derives the optimal conditions for photon distribution—favoring balance—and quantifies how measurement techniques (like number-resolving detection) maintain quantum advantages even when realistic photon loss is present. The results establish a scalable framework for quantum-enhanced distributed metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for quantum metrology in photonic systems, directly addressing how entanglement and measurement choice can overcome classical limits, which is central to quantum information science applications like quantum communication and sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how to achieve optimal phase estimation sensitivity in quantum interferometry using multiphoton states.

**Main result.** Maximum phase sensitivity is achieved when the input photons are distributed symmetrically between the phase-encoding and reference blocks, leading to a phase-independent response for $\varphi 
eq 0$.

**Method.** The authors derive the classical Fisher Information (CFI) by calculating probability amplitudes using matrix permanents and analyzing the second derivatives of the detection probabilities with respect to the unknown phase.

**Model / system.** The system is a linear-optical interferometer designed for phase estimation, utilizing $N$ separable photon inputs that evolve into number-path entangled states via unitary transformations (like QFTs) followed by photon-number-resolving detection.

**Key observables.** Classical Fisher Information (CFI), phase sensitivity, and the probability of observing specific output photon-number configurations ($\vec{n}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total photon number ($N$), imbalance parameter ($\Delta = |k - l|/N$), and photon loss parameters ($\epsilon, \eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the small-phase approximation ($\varphi \simeq 0$) for initial derivations, and later incorporates models for realistic photon loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the interferometric architecture (FIG. 1) and show how CFI depends on phase ($	ext{FIG. 2}$), while tables quantify CFI under varying conditions like imbalance or loss.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the ideal CFI for balanced partitions, to analyzing the impact of photon loss by comparing detection schemes (homodyne vs. number-resolving), and finally extending the framework to multi-mode distributed sensing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate interferometric phase-estimation using separable photon inputs that evolve into number-path entangled states through linear optical networks, followed by photon-number-resolving detection. A simple analytical expression for the classical Fisher information at zero phase is derived for arbitrary $N$-photon states distributed across 2$N$ optical modes, partitioned into phase-encoding and reference blocks. Among all possible photon distributions between these blocks, the balanced configuration maximizes the phase sensitivity for every photon number $N$ and uniquely exhibits a phase-independent response. The achievable sensitivity degrades monotonically with increasing asymmetry in the photon distribution. We further investigate the robustness of the protocol in the presence of realistic photon loss and extend the analysis to distributed architectures with multiple receivers. In the low photon-flux regime, vacuum fluctuations fundamentally limit local quadrature measurements, whereas nonlocal photon-number-resolving measurements exploit multiphoton interference to mitigate loss-induced sensitivity degradation. Together, these results establish a scalable framework for quantum-enhanced distributed multimode metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** The study investigates excitonic behavior in the layered antiferromagnetic semiconductor $	ext{CrPS}_4$ using both advanced theory and detailed optical measurements. Researchers found that temperature and magnetic fields induce measurable shifts in exciton energies, providing clear spectroscopic signatures of magnetic phase transitions. This work highlights the potential for all-optical control and sensing of magnetic order in these correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it uses optical spectroscopy to map out the complex interplay between electronic excitations (excitons) and magnetic order parameters in a solid-state material, touching upon concepts central to quantum magnetism and correlated materials physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates excitonic phenomena in $	ext{CrPS}_4$, focusing on how these optical properties are intertwined with the material's spin and lattice degrees of freedom, particularly concerning magnetic ordering.

**Main result.** The research establishes that $	ext{CrPS}_4$ is a direct-gap semiconductor whose low-energy excitonic transitions exhibit clear, measurable energy shifts corresponding to magnetic phase transitions (AFM $\leftrightarrow$ PM).

**Method.** A combination of advanced theoretical calculations (MBPT, DMFT, QSG$\hat{	ext{W}}$) and detailed experimental measurements (low-temperature PL/PLE spectroscopy under varying temperature and magnetic fields) was employed.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{CrPS}_4$, a layered antiferromagnetic van der Waals semiconductor. The theoretical framework models excitons interacting with spin and lattice degrees of freedom, while experiments probe these interactions optically.

**Key observables.** Exciton transition energies ($\sim 1.36 	ext{ eV}$), bandgap energy ($2.48 	ext{ eV}$ in AFM phase), temperature-dependent peak shifts, and exciton decay rates ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (up to $300 	ext{ K}$), magnetic field (up to $8 	ext{ T}$), and activation energies ($E_a = 80 \pm 10 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on modeling exciton population transfer between localized states ($	ext{X}1 ightarrow 	ext{X}4$) using rate equations, and assuming simplified background behavior in intensity fitting.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, calculated band/optical spectra for different magnetic phases, and temperature/field dependence of PL intensities showing characteristic energy shifts and quenching behaviors.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from theoretical predictions of electronic structure (bandgap, excitons) to experimental characterization of these features under varying external stimuli (T, B), culminating in detailed analysis of exciton population dynamics via temperature-dependent decay modeling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Layered van der Waals magnetic semiconductors provide a versatile platform for exploring excitonic phenomena intertwined with spin and lattice degrees of freedom, enabling excitons to act as sensitive probes of magnetic order. CrPS$_4$ is a layered antiferromagnetic semiconductor that hosts rich excitonic features whose microscopic origin and connection to magnetic ordering remain incompletely understood. Here, we investigate the electronic and excitonic properties of bulk CrPS$_4$ using a combination of many-body perturbation theory, dynamical mean-field theory, and photoluminescence-based experiments. Our calculations establish CrPS$_4$ as a direct-gap semiconductor with a bandgap of 2.48~eV in the antiferromagnetic phase. Several sub-bandgap excitonic transitions are predicted by theory, comprising multiple spin-allowed excitons and an additional spin-flip excitation, predominantly localized on the Cr$^{3+}$ ions. Temperature- and magnetic-field-dependent optical measurements reveal thermally driven exciton redistribution among localized states and identify characteristic energy shifts that provide clear optical signatures of magnetic phase transitions in CrPS$_4$. These results provide new insights into the excitonic transitions of antiferromagnets and suggest potential routes for all-optical sensing and light-driven control of their magnetic order.

</details>

### [Matrix Product Evolution: A Method for Simulating Quantum Circuits Using Tensor Networks](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03472v1)

**Authors:** Haruyuki Kawabe, Minoru Nagai, Tsuyoshi Okubo, Synge Todo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03472v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.03472_figures/2608.03472_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Overview of the tensor-network representation and contraction strategy underlying the MPE-based simulation for a quantum circuit. (1) An input quantum circuit consisting of an initial state and a sequence of quantum gates. (2–6) Construction of a corresponding tensor network, insertion of identity tensors, temporal compression of the tensor network, successive contraction of MPEs, and final contraction yielding the output quantum state.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.03472_figures/2608.03472_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Procedure of the temporal compression. (1) Each red box indicates a local tensor block to be processed. (2) The block is contracted with the nearest nontrivial tensors on its left (earlier time step). (3a) The result is factorized by an SVD along the spatial direction; if the spatial bond dimension λ is at most four, the merge is accepted and the all-identity columns (dotted) vacated by the merge are removed. (3b) If it exceeds four, the merge is rejected and the block is packed leftward past the identity tensors.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.03472_figures/2608.03472_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. Procedure for zipping up two MPEs. The process consists of three parts. In (1)–(7), tensors in adjacent MPEs are contracted, and the resulting lower-triangular matrix L is propagated toward the left. In (8)–(12), singular value decomposition is performed, and the singular values S together with the right unitary matrix V are propagated toward the right. In (13)–(14), an MPS representation of the final state is constructed.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">

**Summary.** This paper introduces Matrix Product Evolution (MPE), a novel tensor network method that represents quantum circuits by compressing them along the circuit's time depth. It details an efficient contraction strategy, demonstrating that MPE can achieve higher accuracy than standard MPS methods when post-selection is applied to the final state, making it valuable for simulating complex quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists studying open quantum systems or dynamics, MPE offers a powerful tool to simulate time evolution where entanglement structure is complex; the explicit handling of temporal correlations via depth compression is valuable for understanding circuit complexity beyond simple spatial bipartitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Classical simulation of quantum circuits is limited by Hilbert space size, necessitating efficient tensor network representations.

**Main result.** Matrix Product Evolution (MPE) provides a depth-oriented tensor network representation that can outperform standard MPS when post-selection is involved, particularly in reducing temporal entanglement contributions to the error.

**Method.** The authors introduce MPE, which represents circuits as 2D tensor networks compressed along the circuit depth using an exact 'temporal compression' procedure. Simulation proceeds via a specialized 'zip-up contraction' strategy.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on random quantum circuits and time evolution simulations of the quantum Ising model acting on multiple qubits (e.g., 14 qubits).

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($|\langle\varphi|\varphi_0angle|^2$), Infidelity ($1 - |\langle\varphi|\varphi_0angle|^2$), and singular value distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Maximum temporal bond dimension ($\chi$), circuit depth (number of gates/Trotter steps), and the degree of post-selection ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on controlling approximation error via truncation ($\chi$) during tensor contraction, and its advantage is highlighted when post-selection constrains temporal degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare infidelity (MPE vs MPS) for random circuits and the Ising model, showing MPE's superior performance with post-selection. They also illustrate the procedural steps for temporal compression and zipping up tensors.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces MPE as a depth-based tensor representation, details the exact temporal compression and zip-up contraction procedures, and then compares its numerical behavior against standard MPS methods using random circuits and structured Hamiltonian evolution (Ising model) with post-selection diagnostics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical simulation of quantum circuits is an essential tool in quantum information science, but its applicability is constrained by the exponential growth of the Hilbert space and the entanglement structure of quantum states. In this work, we introduce Matrix Product Evolution (MPE), a tensor-train representation of quantum circuits constructed along the circuit depth rather than along the qubit index. Within this formulation, the simulation of a quantum circuit is modeled as the contraction of multiple MPE tensors. We develop an efficient contraction strategy based on a zip-up procedure to carry out this contraction in practice. We investigate the numerical behavior of this MPE-based contraction framework through simulations of random quantum circuits and the time evolution of a quantum many-body state. Our results characterize the growth of temporal bond dimensions, clarify how post-selection modifies the contraction cost and approximation accuracy, and identify regimes in which depth-oriented tensor-network contractions provide a useful complement to standard MPS-based simulation approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This research uses surface-selective measurements on graphene proximitized by WS2 to investigate the spin structure of superconductivity. By observing how superconducting pockets appear or disappear depending on carrier polarization relative to the interface, the authors provide strong evidence supporting a spin-triplet pairing mechanism. The combination of advanced transport measurements and theoretical modeling makes this a powerful tool for studying correlated electron phenomena in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a highly tunable platform for probing exotic superconducting symmetries in 2D materials. The use of proximity coupling as a 'spin probe' is valuable because it allows researchers to selectively perturb and measure specific components of the pairing order parameter, which is crucial for understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To obtain complementary probes of the superconducting spin structure in rhombohedral graphene, as direct magnetic field probing is challenging due to multiple possible microscopic mechanisms for field-robust superconductivity.

**Main result.** The surface-selective proximity coupling to WS2 acts as a displacement-field-tunable probe, providing evidence for spin-triplet superconductivity by observing superconducting asymmetry dependent on carrier polarization relative to the interface.

**Method.** The study combines low-temperature magnetotransport measurements (measuring $R_{xx}$ and differential resistance) with theoretical self-consistent band structure calculations using a mean-field Hartree-Fock approach.

**Model / system.** The system is rhombohedral pentalayer graphene (R5G) proximitized on one side by WS2. The physics involves analyzing the interplay between superconducting pairing, induced Ising spin-orbit coupling (SOC), and carrier polarization across different electronic regimes (bulk vs. surface pockets).

**Key observables.** Superconducting pocket robustness ($	ext{SC1}, 	ext{SC2}, 	ext{SC3}$), asymmetry in superconductivity under displacement field reversal, quantum oscillation patterns, and Fermi-surface topology.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature ($T \simeq 8.5 	ext{ mK}$), zero magnetic field ($	ext{B}=0 	ext{ T}$), and the magnitude of the displacement field ($D/\epsilon_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed asymmetry is due to the surface-selective nature of the Ising SOC, which competes with different pairing symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate longitudinal resistance vs. carrier density and displacement field, quantum oscillation patterns in gate voltage planes, and schematics detailing the three distinct superconducting pockets ($	ext{SC1}-	ext{SC3}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental platform using magnetotransport measurements to map out superconducting regimes based on external fields. It then uses quantum oscillations and band structure calculations to characterize the underlying electronic topology (Fermi surface). Finally, it integrates these findings by analyzing how the SOC modifies the pairing state, leading to the conclusion about spin-triplet superconductivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene has been observed across many layer numbers, with mounting evidence pointing toward spin-triplet pairing, yet complementary probes of the superconducting spin structure remain needed. Here we use one-sided WS$_2$ proximity in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene (R5G) as a surface-selective spin-orbit probe. The induced Ising spin-orbit coupling is strongest for carriers localized near the WS$_2$ interface, allowing the displacement field to tune the overlap between superconducting carriers and the spin-orbit perturbation. We observe a strongly asymmetric superconducting landscape: two robust pockets, SC1 and SC2, survive only on mutually opposite signs of displacement field, while a third pocket, SC3, is substantially weaker. Gate-tracking features, quantum oscillations, and self-consistent band-structure calculations identify the layer polarization and Fermi-surface character of the relevant carriers. The robust superconducting states are absent or strongly weakened when the active high-DOS carriers are polarized toward the WS$_2$ interface. Since Ising spin-orbit coupling is compatible with time-reversed spin-singlet pairing but competes with same-spin intervalley triplet pairing by canting or pinning the parent spin texture, this surface-selective suppression provides additional evidence for spin-triplet superconductivity involving both hole-like and electron-like carriers. Our results establish one-sided TMD proximity as a displacement-field-tunable probe of superconducting spin structure in rhombohedral graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the synthesis and comprehensive characterization of a novel high-entropy compound, RhMnFeCoGe4, which crystallizes in a B20 structure. The material is found to be ferromagnetic with a critical temperature of 146 K. By combining magnetic measurements, NMR spectroscopy, and DFT calculations, the authors map out its electronic structure and how its magnetic properties respond to external pressure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter physics, the detailed study of magnetic phase transitions and electronic band structures in complex, disordered materials like high-entropy alloys touches upon many-body interactions relevant to correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on synthesizing and characterizing a novel high-entropy compound, RhMnFeCoGe4, which possesses a cubic non-centrosymmetric B20 structure.

**Main result.** The material exhibits ferromagnetic behavior with a critical temperature of 146 K and a spontaneous moment of 2.5 mu_B per formula unit, showing magnetic properties sensitive to external pressure.

**Method.** A combination of experimental techniques (XRD, VSM, NMR) and theoretical calculations (ab initio DFT) was employed to investigate its electronic and magnetic structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the high-entropy compound RhMnFeCoGe4, which adopts a cubic non-centrosymmetric B20 crystal structure. The research explores how magnetism and transport properties are governed by this complex composition under varying pressure conditions.

**Key observables.** Ferromagnetic ordering ($T_C=146$ K), spontaneous magnetization ($2.5 \mu_B$), critical exponents ($eta, \gamma, \delta$), magnetic moments of constituent atoms (Mn: $2.2 \mu_B$, Co: $0.5 \mu_B$), and lattice parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperature ($T_C=146$ K), applied pressures (up to 8 GPa), temperatures (2–260 K), and magnetic fields ($\le 9$ T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed resistivity anomaly is due to chemical disorder, which was not fully accounted for in the initial *ab initio* simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate XRD patterns confirming the B20 structure; magnetization vs. temperature/field curves show ferromagnetic transitions and critical behavior; DOS and band structures compare paramagnetic and ferromagnetic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard materials science investigation: synthesis confirmation (XRD) $ightarrow$ bulk magnetic characterization (VSM, critical exponents) $ightarrow$ microscopic analysis (NMR, DFT calculations of DOS/band structure) $ightarrow$ discussion of pressure effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A novel high-entropy compound, RhMnFeCoGe$_4$, with a cubic non-centrosymmetric B20 struc- ture, has been synthesized under conditions of high pressure and temperature. The electrical transport and magnetic properties of the obtained compound at both ambient and elevated pres- sures have been investigated. In addition, nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectra were obtained at 4.2 K and ab initio calculations were performed. The new material exhibits ferromag- netic behavior with a critical temperature of $T_C$ = 146 K and a spontaneous moment of 2.5 $μ_B$ per formula unit. The magnetization data obtained at the critical region yielded the critical temperature and exponents, which were found to be $T_C$ = 146(1) K, $β$ = 0.337(1), $γ$ = 1.121(1), and $δ$ = 4.326(1). The magnetic moments of Mn and Co were determined from NMR spectra to be 2.2 $μ_B$ and 0.5 $μ_B$, respectively. Ab initio calculations yielded reasonable values for the lattice parameter and the magnetic moments of all constituents. The density of states and band structure are determined for both paramagnetic and ferromagnetic states. Lattice compression results in the increase in the $T_C$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on magnetoresistance measurements in the magnetic nodal line semimetal Mn3ZnC, demonstrating a clear link between MR anomalies and the material's ferromagnetic/ferrimagnetic transitions. By analyzing how MR changes with field and temperature relative to magnetization, the authors suggest that quantum transport effects related to spin-dependent scattering govern the electronic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the strong dependence of electronic transport properties (MR) on spin polarization and topological band structure provides insights into emergent quantum phenomena relevant to correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates magnetoresistance (MR) in magnetic topological semimetals to understand how spin-dependent electron scattering mechanisms couple with magnetic ordering.

**Main result.** The MR behavior is strongly correlated with the material's magnetic state (FM vs. FIM), showing distinct field and temperature dependencies that suggest quantum transport effects dominate over classical models.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of MR versus applied magnetic field and temperature with theoretical analysis comparing observed power laws to established quantum transport theories.

**Model / system.** The system under study is Mn3ZnC, an antiperovskite nodal line semimetal exhibiting both ferromagnetic (FM) and ferrimagnetic (FIM) transitions. The analysis draws parallels with general topological magnetic materials.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance (MR), magnetization ($M$), carrier mobility ($\mu$), and the field/temperature dependence of MR power laws ($	ext{MR} \propto H^p$ or $	ext{MR} \propto M^eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic transition temperatures ($\sim 420	ext{ K}$ FM, $\sim 195	ext{ K}$ FIM), low-field critical fields ($B^*$), and high magnetic field regimes ($\geq 2	ext{ T}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis tests applicability of various transport models (PL model, Guiding Center Diffusion) and ultimately favors quantum limit theories due to low sample mobility.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate MR vs. H at varying T across magnetic transitions; contour plots show low-field MR dependence on field/temperature; magnetization curves track the FM/FIM states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes MR data, first establishing the correlation between MR and $M(H)$ in different magnetic phases (FM vs. FIM). It then compares observed field dependencies to theoretical models before concluding with interpretations based on quantum transport limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The magnetoresistance (MR) in magnetic materials reveal an intriguing spin-dependent electron scattering, highlighting the role of spin polarization on the electronic transport properties. This becomes even more interesting for the topological magnetic materials where a finite Berry curvature leads to an intrinsic scattering channel also. In this report, we investigate MR of Mn$_{3}$ZnC, an antiperovskite magnetic nodal line semimetal. Mn$_{3}$ZnC shows a ferromagnetic (FM) transition at $\sim$ 420 K, followed by an ferrimagnetic (FIM) transition at $\sim$ 195 K. We focus on the interpretation of MR data and highlight the relation between MR and its magnetization. The signature of magnetization induced MR shows correlation at low magnetic fields and displays distinct behaviors in the FIM and FM states. Similar to the isothermal magnetization M(H) curve of FM and FIM state, the MR curves exhibit a sharp increase, followed by a linear behavior at higher fields. As the magnetic field varies, the cusp-like anomaly becomes more pronounced, and vanishes at the magnetic phase transition, which then reappears as the temperature increases. The sign change in the MR curves in the FIM state is attributed to a drastic change in the carrier mobility. Interestingly, this system shows a positive MR in FM state at very low field, which is quite unusual.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically investigate exotic superconductivity in the doped Kitaev quantum spin liquid using variational Monte Carlo simulations. They find that superconducting pairing symmetries are highly dependent on doping and the sign of the Kitaev interaction, predicting robust triplet p-wave superconductivity coexisting with ferromagnetism for FM coupling. This work offers a theoretical roadmap for searching for such unconventional phases in real materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses unconventional superconductivity in a highly correlated magnetic system, providing concrete predictions—like triplet p-wave coexistence with magnetism—that guide experimental searches in candidate materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates exotic superconducting phases, particularly triplet p-wave and singlet d+id pairing, that can coexist with ferromagnetism in a doped Kitaev quantum spin liquid.

**Main result.** For ferromagnetic coupling, robust triplet superconductivity coexists with ferromagnetism at low to intermediate doping. For antiferromagnetic coupling, the superconducting symmetry changes from triplet p-wave to singlet d+id pairing as doping increases.

**Method.** The research employs the many-variable variational Monte Carlo (mVMC) method using a projected pair-product wave function representation of the Kitaev quantum spin liquid.

**Model / system.** The system is the hole-doped t-J-type Kitaev model on the honeycomb lattice, analyzed for both ferromagnetic ($K/t=-1$) and antiferromagnetic ($K/t=+1$) Kitaev interactions. The analysis covers doping regimes ($\delta$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting pairing correlations (singlet/triplet), uniform magnetization per site ($M_\alpha$), and total magnetization ($M_{all}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration ($\delta$), Kitaev interaction strength ratio ($K/t$), and lattice size ($L=8, 10, 12$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the BCS state onto a fixed particle number sector to obtain the pair-product wave function $|\phi_{	ext{pair}}(F)angle$. The fate in the thermodynamic limit remains an open question.

**Figures summary.** Figures show phase diagrams vs. doping for FM/AFM cases, distance dependence of pairing correlations ($D_{\eta}^\alpha(r)$), and doping dependence of triplet correlation ($P_{all}^{py}$) alongside total magnetization ($M_{all}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the superconducting ground state by first establishing the theoretical framework using projected wave functions (BdG approach) and then applying mVMC to calculate pairing correlations and magnetization across different doping regimes for both FM and AFM couplings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate superconductivity in a doped Kitaev quantum spin liquid by applying the many-variable variational Monte Carlo method to the hole-doped $t$-$J$-type Kitaev model. Using a projected pair-product wave function that can exactly represent the Kitaev quantum spin liquid, we examine the stability of superconducting phases on isotropic two-dimensional clusters. For the ferromagnetic Kitaev interaction, robust triplet $p$-wave superconductivity coexists with ferromagnetism in the low-to-intermediate doping regime but is suppressed as the system approaches the fully polarized ferromagnetic phase. For the antiferromagnetic Kitaev interaction, superconductivity exhibits a change in the dominant pairing symmetry from spin-dependent triplet $p$-wave at low doping to singlet $d+id$ at intermediate doping. By varying the strength of the ferromagnetic Kitaev interaction at fixed doping, we show that the triplet superconductivity increases together with the ferromagnetic moment and becomes strongest slightly below full polarization. Our results provide a theoretical basis for experimental searches for unconventional superconductivity, such as triplet superconductivity coexisting with ferromagnetism, in carrier-doped Kitaev candidate materials.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates the formation of skyrmion crystals in Wigner solids whose underlying band structure imparts Berry curvature. By modeling the competition between Heisenberg and chiral interactions, the authors show that the system's topological texture density is continuously tunable via the ratio of these coupling constants. This establishes a comprehensive phase diagram for ferromagnetic topological Wigner crystals.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects topological quantum geometry (Berry curvature) directly to emergent magnetic textures (skyrmions) in strongly correlated electron systems, providing a theoretical framework for understanding exotic electronic states beyond simple magnetism.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating how topological textures, specifically skyrmions, emerge and are stabilized in Wigner crystals when underlying electronic bands possess Berry curvature.

**Main result.** The competition between ferromagnetic exchange ($J$) and chiral interaction ($\gamma$) stabilizes a zero-field tunable skyrmion crystal whose density can be continuously tuned by the ratio $\gamma/J$.

**Method.** A combination of continuum field theory (nonlinear sigma model) applied to a discrete triangular lattice, supplemented by gradient expansion techniques up to fourth order.

**Model / system.** The system models low-density electrons forming a Wigner solid on a triangular lattice. The effective spin Hamiltonian includes Heisenberg exchange ($J$) and a chiral three-spin interaction ($\gamma$), which originates from the Berry curvature of the underlying electronic bands.

**Key observables.** Skyrmion density ($ho_{sk}$), topological charge ($N_{sk}$), magnon structure, and the Dynamical Spin Structure Factor (DSSF).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $\gamma/J$ is crucial for tuning skyrmion density; extreme coupling leads to a 24-site tetra-skyrmion crystal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a regular triangular lattice structure and relies on expanding the Hamiltonian in spatial derivatives up to fourth order in the continuum limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing transitions from ferromagnetic Wigner crystals to double-skyrmion crystals, culminating in the tetra-skyrmion crystal at large chiral coupling ($\gamma/J \geq 4$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the microscopic Hamiltonian on the lattice, deriving the continuum sigma model, analyzing energy contributions via gradient expansion (up to fourth order), and finally characterizing specific complex phases like the tetra-skyrmion crystal using real and momentum space representations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In low density systems with strong interactions electrons are expected to crystallize into a Wigner solid. Recently, advances in two-dimensional systems where electrons carry Berry curvature have added a topological dimension to Wigner crystallization. Using a model for pseudospin interactions in a topological Wigner crystal, here we show that the competition between ferromagnetic Heisenberg exchange $J$ and the chiral interaction $γ$ on the triangular lattice stabilizes a zero-field skyrmion crystal whose density is continuously tunable through the ratio $γ/J$. The chiral interaction originates from finite Berry curvature in an underlying time-reversal broken Wigner crystal. In the continuum limit, the chiral interaction acts as a chemical potential for skyrmions, while higher-order gradient terms beyond the nonlinear sigma model select the skyrmion density. At large chiral coupling, we uncover a 24-site tetra-skyrmion crystal carrying four units of topological charge per magnetic unit cell, which becomes degenerate with four-sublattice tetrahedral order as $J \to 0$. We characterize the magnon structure for these phases and discuss the transition between the tunable skyrmion crystal to these states at large $γ/J$. Our results establish the phase diagram of ferromagnetic topological Wigner crystals.

</details>
