**Summary.** The research uses helicity-resolved Raman spectroscopy to compare the electron-phonon-spin coupling in $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GeTe}_2$ and $	ext{Fe}_5	ext{GeTe}_2$. The findings reveal that this coupling is complex, stemming from spin-orbit interactions rather than just magnetic ordering. This highlights how crystal structure dictates the nature of anisotropic light-matter interaction in these layered magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental insight into how lattice vibrations couple anisotropically with spin-orbit coupled electronic states in magnetic materials, which is crucial for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to comparatively investigate anisotropic electron-phonon-spin coupling mechanisms in two-dimensional van der Waals ferromagnets, $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GeTe}_2$ and $	ext{Fe}_5	ext{GeTe}_2$.

**Main result.** The helicity-dependent Raman response is shown to arise from spin-orbit-coupled electronic interactions rather than solely from time-reversal symmetry breaking due to magnetic order. Furthermore, the crystal structure dictates distinct coupling behaviors between the two compounds.

**Method.** Helicity-resolved Raman spectroscopy was employed, utilizing left- and right-circularly polarized excitations across varying temperatures and wavelengths.

**Model / system.** The physical systems are layered metallic ferromagnets: $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GeTe}_2$ (hexagonal) and $	ext{Fe}_5	ext{GeTe}_2$ (rhombohedral). The analysis focuses on the interplay between lattice vibrations, electronic structure, and magnetic ordering in these materials.

**Key observables.** Chiral mode splitting ($\Delta f$), Fano asymmetry parameter ($1/q$), and polarization-dependent Raman intensities derived from helicity-resolved measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature dependence of coupling strengths; the difference between out-of-plane vs. in-plane magnetization effects on coupling anisotropy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed spectral features can be decomposed into contributions from electron-phonon, spin-orbit, and magnetic ordering effects using symmetry group theory for Raman tensor fitting.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate unit cells for both compounds; polarization dependence plots (e.g., in VH configuration) are used to analyze mode symmetries and coupling mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context of 2D van der Waals ferromagnets, details the comparative measurements on $	ext{F}3	ext{GT}$ and $	ext{F}5	ext{GT}$, presents spectroscopic evidence regarding chiral splitting and Fano parameters, and concludes by relating structural differences to distinct coupling dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional van der Waals ferromagnets Fe$_3$GeTe$ _2$ (F3GT) and Fe$_5$GeTe$_2$ (F5GT) exhibit pronounced magneto-optical responses, which open promising platforms for investigating the interplay among lattice, electronic, and magnetic degrees of freedom. Here, we present a comparative study of optical resonance-induced anisotropic electron-phonon coupling and its association with magnetic ordering in these systems using wavelength- and temperature-dependent helicity-resolved Raman spectroscopy. By resolving the doubly degenerate E modes under left- and right-circularly polarized excitations, we demonstrate that the temperature evolution of the chiral mode splitting ($Δf$) does not track the magnetization behavior, indicating that the helicity-dependent Raman response arises not solely from time-reversal symmetry breaking due to magnetic order, but also from spin-orbit-coupled electronic interactions. Notably, in F3GT, the out-of-plane magnetization indirectly governs the in-plane anisotropic electron-phonon coupling under optical resonance, whereas F5GT exhibits static anisotropic interactions. The Fano asymmetry parameter $1/q$ reveals mode- and temperature-dependent coupling strengths between phonons and the electronic continuum, with pronounced angular anisotropy in F3GT but isotropic behavior in F5GT--- a consequence of its multiple Fe sites and enhanced interlayer hybridization in the latter. Our results demonstrate the role of crystal structure and magnetic anisotropy in shaping the anisotropically coupled electron-phonon-spin dynamics in these layered metallic ferromagnets, and highlight Fe$_x$GeTe$_2$ as a versatile platform for microscopic insight into chiral light-matter interactions in layered metallic ferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the theoretical gap in describing gravitational redshift quantum mechanically. It proves that previous models are inconsistent and resolves this by formulating the effect as a well-defined quantum channel acting on Gaussian states of light. This allows researchers to use modern quantum information tools to analyze relativistic effects on quantum optical signals.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges general relativity with fundamental quantum information theory by providing rigorous tools (channel nonunitarity) to quantify relativistic effects on quantum light fields, which is highly relevant for quantum communication in curved spacetime.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a consistent quantum mechanical description of gravitational redshift, which has historically been modeled using flawed effective multi-mode mixers.

**Main result.** The authors successfully model the effect as a genuine quantum channel when restricted to Gaussian states, allowing characterization using standard quantum information techniques like fidelity and channel nonunitarity.

**Method.** The work involves analyzing spectral transformations derived from general relativity and correcting inconsistencies in previous models by employing field-theoretic methods and extending the description to unitary channels on larger Hilbert spaces.

**Model / system.** The system is light propagating through a stationary spacetime, where the gravitational redshift induces an operator transformation $U_\chi$ on the spectral distribution functions of the photons. The analysis focuses on characterizing this transformation as a quantum channel.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F_\chi$), Channel Nonunitarity ($\eta(\chi)$), and the overlap quantity $\Delta f(\chi) = (f, U_\chi f)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The redshift factor $\chi = \sqrt{\zeta_a/\zeta_b}$ dictates the transformation; squeezing in the initial state is crucial for observing fidelity reduction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous models incorrectly assumed that the unitary operator $U_\chi$ could be represented by a finite-dimensional matrix, an assumption proven false for general physical spectra when $\chi 
eq 1$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the violation of unitarity ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2$) in older models and show state evolution (e.g., Alice-to-Bob-to-Alice roundtrip) using fidelity measures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the classical/wave-packet transformation; it then critiques existing multi-mode mixer models, proving their inconsistency; subsequently, it introduces two consistent corrections: a field-theoretic approach and the quantum channel formalism for Gaussian states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The gravitational frequency shift of light is well understood in the theory of classical electromagnetism. Nevertheless, its description in quantum theory is not yet fully developed. Recent work pointed out inconsistencies in previously developed models aimed at describing gravitational redshift as an effective multi-mode mixer (MMM) acting on modes of light, however, a complete solution of the issues was not obtained. Here, we identify the root cause of the MMM model's inconsistency and provide two complementary approaches to correct it: a "natural" one from a field-theoretic perspective, and another adapted to the language of quantum mechanics of finite-dimensional systems. We show that the second approach allows for modeling of the redshift in a multi-mode transmission setup as a quantum channel that can be characterized using standard quantum information-theoretic techniques when restricting the input states to Gaussian states of light.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel method to derive universal features of quantum chaos and gravity without invoking ensemble averaging. By applying a 'smooth filter projection' to semiclassical formulas, the authors show that phenomena like spectral ramps emerge from single-system physics. This framework establishes a deep duality, predicting corresponding double exponential corrections in both quantum many-body systems and gravitational wormhole dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the focus on extracting macroscopic smooth behavior from underlying chaotic/erratic microscopic data mirrors techniques used in analyzing complex many-body dynamics where averaging approximations are often employed.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reinterprets universal features of quantum chaos (like spectral ramps/plateaus) and their gravitational duals, which are conventionally thought to require ensemble averaging.

**Main result.** These phenomena can be derived purely from single-system dynamics by projecting out the smooth macroscopic structure hidden within erratic microscopic data. This predicts double exponential corrections in both quantum many-body systems ($1/N^2$) and gravity ($	ext{GN}$).

**Method.** The core method involves defining a 'smooth filter projection' that isolates the smooth component of observables, bypassing explicit ensemble averaging techniques.

**Model / system.** The work applies to quantum chaotic systems modeled by the Gutzwiller trace formula and extends this framework holographically to large-N gravitational systems involving spacetime wormholes.

**Key observables.** Spectral form factor (ramp, plateau), density of states oscillations, hyper-non-perturbative objects (baby-universe condensates).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large-$N$ limit, semiclassical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$), gravitational coupling constant ($G_N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence and applicability of a 'minimal Gutzwiller-like structure' in the large-N holographic limit is postulated.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an alternative framework using smooth filtering to derive random matrix features from single systems, postulates a minimal Gutzwiller structure for holography, and then applies this dual concept to show how wormholes generate corresponding double exponential effects in gravity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universal hallmarks of quantum chaos---such as the ramp and plateau in the spectral form factor---and the ramp's gravitational duals involving wormholes are widely interpreted as consequences of spectral or ensemble averaging. In this paper, following an earlier proposal of~\cite{Liu25c}, we develop an alternative approach: these phenomena arise as macroscopic smooth structures hidden within erratic microscopic data, which can be isolated through a smooth filter projection. Using the semiclassical Gutzwiller trace formula as a paradigmatic example, we illustrate how many features characteristic of random matrix models---including the ramp, the plateau, the spectral curve, and single-eigenvalue instantons---can be derived in the semiclassical limit without invoking ensemble or explicit spectral averages.   We postulate the existence of a minimal Gutzwiller-like structure in the large-$N$ limit of holographic systems and explore its consequences. Beyond deriving the ramp and the plateau, this Gutzwiller-like structure predicts universal rapid macroscopic oscillations in the density of states and the possible existence of hyper-instantons, both of which involve double exponentials in $1/N^2$.   On the gravity side, we demonstrate how spacetime wormholes enable the construction of emergent hyper-non-perturbative objects---such as baby-universe and wormhole condensates---which yield double exponential effects in $G_N$. This mirrors the postulated boundary Gutzwiller-like structure and provides a dual gravitational derivation of the universal rapid macroscopic oscillations in the density of states and the spectral plateau.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the fluctuation of topological charges in the 2D classical Heisenberg model using both high-temperature and low-temperature expansions. The key finding is that these fluctuations follow an area law at high temperatures but switch to a perimeter law at low temperatures, suggesting a phase transition analogous to the Kosterlitz-Thouless transition.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of topological excitations and their fluctuations is central to understanding phase transitions in many quantum systems, making this relevant to theories involving emergent order parameters or critical phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how topological charge fluctuations behave in the two-dimensional classical Heisenberg model, aiming to draw parallels with the Kosterlitz-Thouless transition observed in the 2D XY model.

**Main result.** The fluctuation of topological charges exhibits an area law ($\propto L^2$) at high temperatures and transitions to a perimeter law ($\propto L$) at low temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis employs both high-temperature expansions (diagrammatic techniques) and low-temperature approximations, treating the system in the continuous limit as decoupled free scalar fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the two-dimensional classical Heisenberg model defined by the Hamiltonian $H = -J \sum_{\langle ij angle} \mathbf{S}_i \cdot \mathbf{S}_j$. At low temperatures, it is approximated in the continuous limit as a Gaussian field theory.

**Key observables.** Topological charge fluctuation ($\chi$), defined as $\langle Q^2 angle - \langle Q angle^2$, and the topological charge $Q$ itself.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$) or inverse temperature ($eta = 1/k_B T$), and the linear dimension of the region ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The high-temperature expansion is carried out up to $O(eta^4)$, and the low-temperature regime assumes spins fluctuate around the z-axis, leading to a continuum approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 defines the lattice structure and topological charge $ho_{ij}$. Figure 2 illustrates diagrams contributing to correlation functions used in the calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem by relating it to the KT transition. It then details calculations using high-temperature expansions, followed by a separate treatment using low-temperature approximations that map the system onto free scalar fields. The results are compared to show the scaling laws for $\chi$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is well known that KT transition in 2d XY model is driven by the binding and unbinding of topological defects, which can be characterized by the fluctuation of topological charges inside a region. We extend the idea into the 2d Heisenberg model and calculate the fluctuation through high-temperature and low-temperature expansion respectively. It is found that the fluctuation of topological charges is proportional to the area of the region at high temperatures while obeys the perimeter law at low temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel 'spectral route' to calculate the free energy of directed polymers in a random medium, modeling glassy line matter. By reformulating the problem as filling logarithmic eigenvalues of a transfer matrix product, the authors confirm that the resulting interaction energy scales quadratically with density ($ho^2$). This result successfully matches predictions from advanced theoretical methods like the replica Bethe ansatz.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between non-intersecting path counting (DPRMs) and random matrix theory spectra is highly relevant to understanding disordered quantum systems. The successful derivation of $ho^2$ scaling via spectral methods provides a powerful, alternative theoretical tool for analyzing glassy dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the quenched free energy and its cumulants for a finite density of mutually avoiding directed polymers (DPRMs) in a random medium, which serves as a model for glassy line matter.

**Main result.** The spectral filling approach successfully reproduces the $ho^2$ interaction energy scaling predicted by the replica Bethe ansatz at low densities. Furthermore, it establishes that the disorder-induced linear edge of the logarithmic spectrum confirms this theoretical prediction.

**Method.** The authors recast the complex many-polymer problem into an equivalent problem of filling the logarithmic eigenvalues of a single-polymer transfer matrix product, utilizing determinant identities and spectral analysis techniques.

**Model / system.** The system models mutually avoiding directed polymers in a quenched random medium. This is mathematically formulated using a time-evolution governed by a single-polymer transfer matrix product $W(t) = T(t) \cdots T(1)$, whose minors relate to multi-polymer partition functions.

**Key observables.** Free-energy density ($f(ho)$), interaction free energy ($\Delta f(ho)$), and the disorder-averaged logarithmic spectral edge $\epsilon_0(t) - \epsilon_i(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Polymer density ($ho$), time $t$, and system size $N$. Key regimes include the dilute limit ($\Delta f(ho) \sim ho^2$) and the pure case ($\Delta f(ho) \sim ho^3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the problem to a fermionic filling rule, requiring the transfer matrix product $W(t)$ to possess real positive eigenvalues, which is confirmed by combining total nonnegativity (from Boltzmann construction) and oscillatory matrix theory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates DPRMs on a space-time lattice. Figure 2 shows the cumulative logarithmic growth rate vs. density $ho$, confirming linear scaling in the dilute regime. Figure 3 tracks the second disorder cumulant, showing expected low-density scaling and convergence at full filling.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops mathematically by first establishing the spectral connection using determinant identities (Chunk 3), then applying this to calculate the free energy density via eigenvalue filling (Chunks 1 & 2). Finally, it rigorously confirms the mathematical validity of the spectral method through high-precision numerical checks on the eigenvalues themselves (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A finite density of mutually avoiding directed polymers in a quenched random medium is a minimal model of glassy line matter. The dilute theory, solved by replica Bethe ansatz, predicts an interaction free energy proportional to $ρ^2$ and disorder cumulants with distinct power-law dependences on the density $ρ$, but direct numerical tests have been hindered by the combinatorially large many-polymer transfer matrix. We recast the problem as filling logarithmic eigenvalues of a single-polymer transfer-matrix product, obtaining the quenched free energy, its cumulants, and a disorder-induced linear spectral edge consistent with the replica prediction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the energy spectrum of a $\chi^{(2)}$ Hamiltonian describing processes like second-harmonic generation at high excitation numbers. By mapping the problem to an effective Schrödinger equation, the authors derive highly accurate asymptotic formulas for the energy levels using advanced quantization techniques. These results offer significant analytical improvements over standard approximations in this quantum optics regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced analytical techniques for solving quantum systems with nonlinear interactions, particularly useful in quantum optics or open quantum system dynamics where effective potentials are derived.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to derive accurate asymptotic expressions for the energy spectrum of a degenerate $\chi^{(2)}$ Hamiltonian when the total excitation number is very large.

**Main result.** The authors derived an implicit quantization condition leading to two explicit, complementary asymptotic formulas that accurately describe the energy levels near the center of the spectrum where conventional approximations fail.

**Method.** The problem was mapped onto a one-dimensional Schrödinger equation with a double-well potential, and solutions were found using Bleher's separatrix quantization condition, supplemented by small-$t$ and large-$t$ expansions.

**Model / system.** The system models nonlinear optical processes like second-harmonic generation and parametric down-conversion, governed by a degenerate $\chi^{(2)}$ Hamiltonian. The spectral problem is simplified due to the conservation of total excitation number, leading to finite-dimensional matrix diagonalization.

**Key observables.** Energy levels ($E_n$) of the system spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large total excitation numbers ($N \gg 1$), and a dimensionless parameter $t$ used in the asymptotic analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derived formulas are valid only within specific ranges for the parameter $t$ (e.g., $t < 1/2$ for one formula), and the conventional JWKB approximation is insufficient near the separatrix energy ($E=0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate expansion coefficients, compare the accuracy of different approximations against exact matrix diagonalization at large N, and show the potential energy landscape.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the spectral problem via Hamiltonian mapping to a Schrödinger equation. It then derives an implicit quantization condition and proceeds by analyzing three complementary asymptotic regimes (small-$t$, large-$t$, and intermediate) to provide comprehensive formulas for $E_n$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the energy spectrum of the degenerate $χ^{(2)}$ Hamiltonian describing second-harmonic generation and parametric down-conversion at large total excitation numbers. Owing to the conservation of the total excitation number, the spectral problem reduces to the diagonalization of finite-dimensional tridiagonal matrices. Following the approach of Alvarez and Alvarez-Estrada, we map this problem onto an effective one-dimensional Schrödinger equation with a double-well potential. We then derive an implicit quantization condition for the energy levels near the top of the potential barrier and obtain two explicit asymptotic formulas applicable in complementary spectral regions. The ranges of applicability of the resulting formulas are established, and their accuracy is compared with exact matrix diagonalization and with the conventional JWKB approximation. The proposed approach provides an accurate analytical description of the energy levels near the center of the spectrum, where the conventional approximation loses accuracy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the NP-hard problem of optimal power system islanding by developing a novel, qubit-efficient hybrid quantum optimization approach using QAOA. By combining compact encoding techniques with Lagrangian constraint handling, the authors significantly reduce the required quantum resources and improve the scalability of the algorithm. This provides a transferable pathway for applying constrained quantum optimization to large, real-world engineering problems on near-term hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on power systems, the core methodology—developing resource-efficient quantum encodings and constraint handling for hard combinatorial problems—is highly relevant to simulating complex many-body Hamiltonians in AMO physics or optimizing quantum control sequences.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is solving controlled power system islanding, an NP-hard combinatorial optimization task, using quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors developed a qubit-efficient hybrid QAOA formulation that reduces circuit complexity from quadratic to linear scaling on sparse graphs while maintaining solution quality across multiple IEEE test systems.

**Method.** They employ a physics-informed compact encoding and a qubit-efficient Lagrangian constraint strategy within the QAOA framework to manage resource constraints imposed by near-term quantum hardware.

**Model / system.** The model is a power network represented as a weighted graph, where the goal is partitioning it into self-sustaining islands. The optimization is formulated as minimizing cut weight subject to connectivity and size constraints.

**Key observables.** Feasible, high-quality islanding solutions; linear scaling of phase-separator and per-layer gate complexity; stable solution quality under device noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (up to 89 buses); fixed number of islands ($K$); circuit depth and sampling budgets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that compact encoding and Lagrangian updates can effectively reduce the required qubit count below conventional formulations, although final feasibility requires classical power-flow analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare different Hamiltonian formulations (e.g., direct vs. Lagrangian) showing reductions in Pauli-term count and improved landscape smoothness for trainability.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, develops a compact encoding ($E_1$ to $E_2$), implements a slack-free Lagrangian treatment, analyzes complexity scaling (reducing gate complexity from quadratic to linear), validates results on multiple IEEE benchmarks, and performs noise analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlled islanding partitions a stressed power network to limit disrupted power transfer while preserving operational integrity in every island. This NP-hard partitioning problem becomes increasingly demanding as networks grow, motivating quantum optimization as a complementary approach. However, limited qubit capacity restricts the scale at which conventional QAOA can address islanding. This paper develops a qubit-efficient hybrid quantum formulation that overcomes this barrier. A physics-informed compact encoding captures essential islanding decisions while exploiting grid structure, with formal guarantees preserving the feasible solution space and optimization objective. A qubit-efficient Lagrangian strategy combines quantum optimization with classical refinement to enforce operational constraints. Complexity analysis shows that for fixed island counts on sparse graphs, the formulation reduces phase-separator and per-layer gate complexity from quadratic to linear scaling. Evaluations on eight IEEE systems (9 to 89 buses) across multiple quantum backends produce feasible, high-quality solutions under practical circuit and sampling budgets. Factorial ablation attributes resource and runtime gains to the complementary effects of compact encoding and qubit-efficient Lagrangian constraint handling. Noise analysis demonstrates stable solution quality under device noise, and landscape diagnostics reveal smoother, more consistently scaled QAOA cost surfaces. These results provide a transferable pathway for scaling constrained quantum optimization toward larger real-world applications on near-term hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates superconductivity in RP nickelate films using ARPES and thermodynamic analysis. The authors report direct evidence for a superconducting phase transition via an electronic specific heat jump, confirming a nonzero order parameter. They establish that the pairing symmetry is nodeless (s-wave) and map how the Fermi surface topology relates to this emergent superconducting state.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the clear measurement of a phase transition signature ($\Delta C/T$) and the determination of pairing symmetry provide fundamental insights into emergent quantum ground states relevant to strongly correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate high-transition-temperature superconductivity and determine the pairing symmetry in Ruddlesden-Popper nickelate films, specifically by providing direct thermodynamic evidence of the superconducting phase transition.

**Main result.** The study observed a clear electronic specific heat jump at T_C, confirming a thermodynamic phase transition, and established that the superconducting gap is nodeless, suggesting an s-wave pairing symmetry in these RP nickelates.

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe electronic structure and thermodynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelate heterostructure, $	ext{La}_2	ext{PrNi}_2	ext{O}_7/	ext{NdAlO}_3$, thin films. Measurements are performed on these epitaxial films under varying strain conditions.

**Key observables.** Superconducting gap magnitude ($\Delta \sim 16 	ext{ meV}$), electronic specific heat jump at $T_C$, and the topology of the Fermi surface pockets ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperature ($T_C$), superconducting order parameter, epitaxial strain, and orbital character (e.g., $	ext{dz}^2$ pocket).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electronic specific heat analysis relies on analogies with cuprates; DFT calculations often assume a single-layer limit to simplify the model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate ARPES results showing gap opening, temperature dependence of the gap, evidence for the specific heat jump at $T_C$, and how Fermi surface pockets evolve under varying epitaxial strain.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the electronic structure via ARPES, demonstrating the superconducting transition through thermodynamic measurements (specific heat), determining the pairing symmetry from the gap structure, and finally mapping the relationship between Fermi surface topology and superconductivity using strain dependence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelates provide an uncharted territory to explore high-transition-temperature (high-$T_C$) superconductivity and superconducting mechanism. Here, we investigate the electronic structure of a new type of high-$T_C$ superconducting RP nickelate heterostructure $\mathrm{La_2PrNi_2O_7/NdAlO_3}$ by angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy. A superconducting state is observed without a pseudogap state, enabling a direct measurement of the superconducting order parameter and a microscopic extraction of the electronic specific heat. The observed superconducting gap opens at $T_C$ with prominent coherence peaks, illustrating the emergence of nonzero order parameter upon entering the superconducting state. An electronic specific heat jump appears at $T_C$, further demonstrating a thermodynamic phase transition. The magnitude of the superconducting order parameter is quantified by the observed superconducting coherence peaks, and a nodeless behavior is unambiguously established in the absence of pseudogap. The underlying Fermi surface consists of $α$, $β$ and $γ$ pockets, exhibiting a multi-orbital nature. Strain dependent measurements further reveal the $γ$ pocket in all superconducting and non-superconducting films with different epitaxial strain. Our results establish the missing thermodynamic evidence for superconducting phase transition in nickelates. They also provide direct evidence for the symmetry of the superconducting order parameter and illustrate the relationship between Fermi surface topology and the emergence of superconductivity in RP nickelate films.

</details>

**Authors:** Sergio Davis  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03960v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

Right panels, histograms of a single velocity component against one-dimensional kappa distributions for u = 0.09262, βS = 2.48333 (first row), u = 0.19195, βS = 2.48333 (second row), and u=0.29157, βS = 2.48085 (third row).</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work shows that the kappa distribution arises naturally as an asymptotic limit when analyzing the single-particle velocity distribution of a system whose total kinetic energy follows an inverse-gamma ensemble. The derivation bypasses reliance on nonextensive statistical mechanics by using standard marginalization techniques in the thermodynamic limit, providing both theoretical insight and a practical Monte Carlo simulation method.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the rigorous derivation of non-standard distributions like Kappa from fundamental statistical mechanics principles (marginalization) is highly relevant for understanding generalized thermalization processes in complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that the Kappa distribution emerges naturally as an asymptotic marginal single-particle velocity distribution from a system governed by an inverse-gamma ensemble in the thermodynamic limit.

**Main result.** The single-particle velocity distribution converges to a kappa distribution form ($P(v|\kappa, 	heta)$) when taking $N 	o \infty$, providing a mechanism for its appearance outside nonextensive statistical mechanics.

**Method.** The derivation involves marginalizing the probability density over the velocities of $N$ particles, starting from an initial exponential family ensemble defined by total kinetic energy constraints.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as $N$ interacting particles whose dynamics are analyzed via steady-state solutions to the Liouville equation. The analysis focuses on the probability density derived from an inverse-gamma distribution of the total kinetic energy.

**Key observables.** Single-particle velocity distribution $P(v)$, total kinetic energy distribution $P(K)$, and the Kullback-Leibler distance ($	ext{D}_{	ext{KL}}$) used for convergence testing.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral index $\kappa$, ensemble parameters $\gamma$ and $K_0$, and the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on taking the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$) for the asymptotic result, and the initial state is assumed to be an energy steady state.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares simulated histograms of kinetic energy/velocity components against exact inverse-gamma distributions. Figure 2 compares the exact vs. kappa model for kinetic energy and plots $	ext{D}_{	ext{KL}}$ divergence versus $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the initial probability density based on total kinetic energy constraints, derives the marginal distribution for total energy ($P(K)$), and then performs a second marginalization over remaining particles to find the single-particle velocity distribution. The final steps involve taking the $N 	o \infty$ limit to map this result onto the known kappa distribution form.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently (Physica A 660 130370, 2025), emergence of superstatistical behavior in driven classical systems has been shown for systems with constant microcanonical heat capacity. As an application of this result, in this work we show that a system of $N$ particles with inverse gamma distribution of total kinetic energies must have kappa-distributed single-particle velocities in the limit $N \rightarrow \infty$. Our results provide insight into the nature of kappa distributions outside the theory of nonextensive statistical mechanics, while also bringing forward a practical method for the generation of kappa velocities via Monte Carlo Metropolis simulation in the inverse gamma ensemble.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously examines the hard-sphere gas model to compare the behavior of the H-function and Entropy. It demonstrates that while conventional theory relies on the H-function decreasing as a measure of irreversibility, rigorous analysis shows entropy remains constant for an isolated system. The conclusion is that entropy, not the H-function, correctly defines the arrow of time in this physical context.

**Why it may be interesting.** It challenges foundational assumptions in statistical mechanics regarding irreversible processes (H-theorem) by using a rigorous, microscopic model to show when macroscopic thermodynamic quantities fail to capture the true direction of time evolution for isolated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental discrepancy between the H-function and entropy regarding the arrow of time in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

**Main result.** For the hard-sphere gas model under rigorous derivation, the system's entropy remains constant (due to isolation), while the H-function decreases initially but exhibits complex behavior suggesting it is not the true criterion for irreversible evolution.

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing the time evolution of the H-function and Entropy using rigorous derivations of the Boltzmann equation under the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a hard-sphere gas model. The dynamics are governed by the rigorously derived Boltzmann equation, which is analyzed in the context of the Boltzmann-Grad (BG) limit.

**Key observables.** H-function ($H$) and Entropy ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Boltzmann-Grad (BG) limit; long-time limit defined by DHM paper.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis contrasts the conventional molecular chaos assumption with results from rigorous derivations for isolated systems.

**Paper structure.** The structure involves establishing the discrepancy between H and S evolution, analyzing the hard-sphere gas model under BG limits, contrasting this with standard Boltzmann theory, and concluding that entropy dictates irreversibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

By investigating the origin of the evolutionary discrepancy between the H-function and entropy, we elucidate that the H-function fails to serve as a valid arrow-of-time criterion in the rigorously derived Boltzmann equation for the hard-sphere gas model under the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves a strong impossibility result showing that perfectly complete Quantum Key Agreement (QKA) protocols cannot be built from black-box one-way functions. The authors show an eavesdropper can always recover the key efficiently by exploiting the structure imposed by perfect completeness within the QROM model. This work significantly tightens previous security bounds by removing reliance on unproven conjectures and achieving a round-independent complexity bound.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QKD/QKA security proofs rather than physical AMO systems, the underlying mathematical techniques involving polynomial methods and information-theoretic security bounds are fundamental tools in quantum information theory that inform secure communication protocols relevant to quantum networking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proves the impossibility of constructing perfectly complete quantum key agreement protocols (QKA) using only black-box access to quantumly secure one-way functions.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that an eavesdropper can recover the shared secret key with certainty using a polynomial number of classical oracle queries, specifically $O((q_A + q_B)^5)$.

**Method.** The proof utilizes the quantum polynomial method over the Boolean cube and relies on establishing that the probability distributions associated with different keys must have pairwise disjoint supports.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted in the Quantum Random Oracle Model (QROM) within a Quantum Computation with Classical Communication (QCCC) framework. Parties exchange only classical messages and query an oracle $H_z$.

**Key observables.** The shared secret key, which must be uniquely determined by the eavesdropper.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The maximum number of quantum queries allowed for Alice ($q_A$) and Bob ($q_B$), which dictate the complexity bound.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol must achieve perfect completeness ($\Pr[K_A = K_B] = 1$). The model restricts communication to classical messages, disallowing pre-shared entanglement or quantum channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the argument by first establishing polynomial representations for the protocol's probability structure (Transcript Rectangularity), then using properties of disjoint supports to limit candidate keys, and finally employing a decision tree search over these candidates to derive the query complexity bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper proves that it is impossible to construct perfectly complete quantum key agreement protocols (QKA) from quantumly secure one-way functions (OWFs) in a black-box manner.   Specifically, consider any protocol in which Alice and Bob exchange only classical messages, make at most $q_{\mathsf{A}}$ and $q_{\mathsf{B}}$ quantum queries, respectively, to a Boolean-valued random oracle, and agree on a shared key with certainty. This paper shows that there exists an eavesdropper, given the classical messages, that can recover the shared key with certainty using $O((q_{\mathsf{A}}+q_{\mathsf{B}})^5)$ classical oracle queries. The bound is independent of the number of rounds, transcript length, key length, and oracle-domain size. Previous results only applies to two-round key agreement (Li et al. CRYPTO 26) or relies on unproven conjectures (Austrin et al. CRYPTO 22).   GPT-5.6 Sol Ultra found this proof in a one-shot conversation and drafted a preliminary version of this paper. The authors are fully responsible for the correctness, writing and discussions of this paper.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Cornucopia codes, a new family of quantum error correcting codes designed specifically for implementation on neutral-atom arrays. These codes overcome traditional trade-offs by achieving high encoding rates and robust error thresholds while maintaining low physical overhead. The results demonstrate a path toward practical, large-scale fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete blueprint for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation by directly addressing hardware constraints (like connectivity and overhead) within the theoretical framework of QEC codes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge for large-scale quantum computing is suppressing errors, which requires error correction codes that balance encoding efficiency, error threshold, and hardware feasibility.

**Main result.** Cornucopia codes achieve an ultra-high encoding rate (>1/2) and a high pseudo-threshold ($\ge 0.4\%$) while maintaining low physical overhead suitable for near-term quantum processors.

**Method.** The authors introduce Cornucopia codes, co-designing the code structure, physical layout, atom rearrangement protocol, and syndrome extraction schedule to enable parallel measurements.

**Model / system.** The work is modeled using abstract qLDPC codes, specifically designed for implementation on reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays which provide long-range connectivity.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($p_L$), encoding rate ($r$), and physical overhead compared to established codes like the surface code or bivariate bicycle codes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p=0.1\%$), logical qubit count (e.g., 1426), and circuit depth (constant for syndrome extraction).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a standard circuit-level noise model and relies on the feasibility of implementing coordinate permutations via global rigid motions in neutral-atom arrays.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate code block structures, geometric actions defining couplings, and the circuit scheduling for syndrome extraction across twelve layers. Tables compare performance metrics like logical error rates across different code families.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the trade-off limitations in existing codes to introducing Cornucopia codes. It details the hardware-aware co-design, analyzes the parallel syndrome measurement circuit, and mathematically proves the structure of the resulting logical error subspaces using group algebra homology.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Suppressing errors is the central challenge for useful large-scale quantum computing. While quantum error correction promises a viable solution to this challenge, existing codes typically suffer from trade-offs among encoding efficiency, error threshold, and hardware feasibility. Here, we introduce Cornucopia codes, a family of practical, hardware-efficient quantum low-density parity-check codes that achieve an ultra-high encoding rate exceeding $1/2$ while maintaining a pseudo-threshold exceeding $0.4\%$ under the standard circuit-level noise model. Inspired by recent affine-permutation-based code constructions and the long-range connectivity available in reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays, we adopt a structured code geometry in which the code layout, atom rearrangement, and syndrome-extraction schedule are co-designed. This structure enables nonlocal syndrome measurements through simple, parallel atom rearrangements. A complete syndrome extraction cycle measures all $X$- and $Z$-type checks in parallel with $12$ entangling layers, independent of the code size. The resulting threshold is comparable to those of the surface code and bivariate bicycle codes. In particular, a single code block $[[2844,1426,18]]$ encodes $1{,}426$ distance-$18$ logical qubits, achieving an extrapolated logical error rate of $2.6\times10^{-16}$ ($1.9\times10^{-31}$) per logical qubit per cycle, assuming the physical error rate of $0.1\%$ ($0.01\%$). By comparison, a bivariate bicycle code implementation would require more than $68{,}000$ physical qubits to encode the same number of logical qubits at a comparable logical error rate. These results bring demonstrations of ultra-low overhead quantum error correction within the reach of near-term quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the six-vertex model on the Sierpinski gasket fractal to study charge confinement. It finds that while the standard ice limit shows confinement, specific parameter choices lead to a novel 'fractal deconfinement' regime. The work uses recursive calculations to determine key observables like string tension and the fractal dimension of the connecting strings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent fractional dimensions ($d_l$) governing topological excitations (strings) in a statistical system provides deep insights into how dimensionality affects long-range order, relevant to understanding critical phenomena in complex materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates fractal deconfinement and confinement phenomena by analyzing statistical mechanical models on fractal lattices.

**Main result.** The model exhibits a transition from entropic charge confinement (in the ice limit) to fractal deconfinement in specific parameter regimes, characterized by a non-trivial string dimension $d_l$.

**Method.** The authors employ recursive calculations for partition functions and correlators tailored to the hierarchical structure of the Sierpinski gasket.

**Model / system.** The system is the six-vertex model applied to the Sierpinski gasket, a four-coordinated fractal with Hausdorff dimension $d_f=\log_2 3$. The analysis compares regimes like the standard ice point to specific low-energy limits (e.g., HAFM).

**Key observables.** String tension ($	au$), correlation decay length ($\xi$), and the fractal dimension of the connecting string ($d_l$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The vertex weights (x, y, z) which define different regimes; the fractal dimension $d_f = \log_2 3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving and solving coupled recurrence relations based on the hierarchical structure of the lattice.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate recursion relation flows, phase diagrams showing parameter dependence, and geometric representations of string paths with different fractal dimensions ($d_l$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the model on the fractal, deriving recursive relations for partition functions, analyzing how correlations decay (confinement vs. deconfinement), and finally proposing experimental realizations using artificial spin ice.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the six-vertex model on the Sierpinski gasket, a four-coordinated hierarchical fractal with Hausdorff dimension $d_f=\log_23$. Given the importance of dimensionality for the long-wavelength behavior of such models, we specifically consider correlations and confinement as a function of the vertex weights, with the equal-weight point corresponding to the ice model. We calculate the partition function and correlators recursively to obtain a rich phase diagram hosting many different regimes. While the ice model shows entropic charge confinement, a particular four-vertex limit exhibits fractal deconfinement, with the string joining the deconfined charges itself a statistical fractal with fractal dimension $d_l=\log_2(5/2)\approx 1.3$. Finally, we propose a setup as an artificial spin ice to enable experimental study of the rich phenomenology of Sierpinski ice and its generalisations.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers studied the disordered perovskite $	ext{BaCu}_{1/3}	ext{Nb}_{2/3}	ext{O}_3$ using X-ray spectroscopy and low-temperature magnetometry. They found that local structural disorder suppresses direct magnetic linkages, leading to a Random Singlet (RS) ground state characterized by power-law temperature dependencies in thermodynamic measurements. Furthermore, applying high magnetic fields induces a crossover toward a distinct quantum paramagnetic phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed experimental realization of random-singlet physics in an oxide magnet, offering insights into how local structural disorder dictates macroscopic quantum magnetic behavior, which is relevant for understanding disordered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the microscopic origin of Random Singlet (RS) behavior in a disordered perovskite, $	ext{BaCu}_{1/3}	ext{Nb}_{2/3}	ext{O}_3$, and its connection to magnetic ordering.

**Main result.** The material exhibits clear signatures of a random-singlet phase via power-law scaling in specific heat and susceptibility at low temperatures, while high fields suggest a crossover to a quantum paramagnetic state.

**Method.** A combined approach using structural probes (XRD/XAFS) to determine local atomic order, coupled with detailed thermodynamic measurements ($c_p(T)$ and $\chi(T)$ under varying magnetic fields).

**Model / system.** The system is the B-site disordered spin-1/2 perovskite $	ext{BaCu}_{1/3}	ext{Nb}_{2/3}	ext{O}_3$, where disorder leads to quenched randomness in the magnetic exchange network.

**Key observables.** Specific heat ($c_p(T)$), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$), and local structural correlations derived from EXAFS.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Random-singlet exponent $\gamma \approx 0.6$; crossover to $T$-linear specific heat at high fields; low Curie-Weiss temperature ($\Theta_{CW}\approx -50$ K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic behavior can be modeled by the Random Singlet (RS) theory, and structural disorder is primarily responsible for suppressing long-range order.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail XRD patterns confirming average structure, XAFS showing local Cu/Nb correlations, and $c_p$ vs. T plots demonstrating power-law scaling at low fields and linear behavior at high fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper first uses structural methods (XRD/XAFS) to characterize the disorder; it then analyzes magnetic susceptibility and specific heat measurements across varying fields to diagnose the nature of the spin ground state, concluding with entropy calculations to estimate contributions from different spin subsystems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a combined structural and thermodynamic study of the ABO$_3$-type disordered perovskite BaCu$_{1/3}$Nb$_{2/3}$O$_3$ (BCNO), whose B site is jointly occupied by Cu and Nb in the $1:2$ ratio. Using synchrotron powder x-ray diffraction (XRD) and x-ray absorption fine structure (XAFS) spectroscopy, we investigate the microscopic nature of Cu$^{2+}$/Nb$^{5+}$ disorder on the pseudo-cubic B-sublattice and its relation to the emergent random-singlet (RS) behavior evidenced at low temperatures. While XRD reveals no long-range Cu/Nb ordering and average site occupancy consistent with stoichiometry, XAFS reveals a peculiar local chemical order characterized by preferential heteroatomic Cu$:$Nb correlations. This local arrangement strongly suppresses direct Cu$:$Cu linkages, despite the Cu concentration being close to the percolation threshold of a cubic lattice. The resulting exchange network explains the absence of spin-glass freezing or long-range magnetic order in the presence of substantial antiferromagnetic interactions, as indicated by a Curie-Weiss temperature $Θ_{CW}\approx -50$ K. Instead, the magnetic susceptibility $χ(T)$ and specific heat $c_p(T)$ exhibit power-law behavior and characteristic single-parameter $T/H$ scaling over broad temperature and magnetic-field ranges, consistent with random-singlet phenomenology. Notably, at very low temperatures, the specific heat behavior transitions from $T^{1-γ}$ ($γ\approx 0.6$ from the $T/H$ scaling) in zero-field to a T-linear dependence under high field, indicating a crossover to a distinct low-energy regime whose microscopic origin remains to be established.

</details>

The fermionic degrees of freedom are Majorana fermions γr, γ′ r. There is a fermionic Villain condition (eq. 2) imposed on all of the states in the Hilbert space. In the (equivalent) graded tensor product Hilbert space, ϕr are replaced with ˜ϕr ∈R/Z, and nr,r+1 by ˜nr,r+1 ∈Z/2, and the Villain condition maps onto the constraint that odd angular momentum states of the ˜ϕr rotor are fermion parity odd (iγrγ′ r = −1).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work constructs and solves a lattice Hamiltonian for a 1+1d Dirac fermion that possesses exact U(1) vector and axial symmetries. By mapping the complex system to an equivalent fermionic Villain model, the authors demonstrate its solvability and show it captures the physics of interacting Luttinger liquids. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for studying symmetry-protected topological phases in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous construction of an exactly solvable interacting fermionic system realizing a Dirac fermion while maintaining specific symmetries is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated electron models where symmetry protection is crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing an exactly solvable lattice Hamiltonian for a 1+1d Dirac fermion that possesses exact microscopic U(1) vector and axial symmetries, while correctly accounting for the mixed anomaly between these symmetries.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct such a model, showing it realizes the entire Luttinger liquid universality class in an exactly solvable manner through mapping to a fermionic Villain Hilbert space.

**Method.** The solvability is established by constructing two equivalent descriptions of the Hilbert space (graded tensor product and fermionic Villain) and demonstrating an isomorphism between them. Low-energy physics is analyzed via mode expansion, reducing the Hamiltonian to coupled harmonic oscillators.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1+1d lattice Dirac fermion model defined on a ring with N sites, involving bosonic rotors ($\phi_r$), discrete variables ($n_{r,r+1}$), and Majorana fermions. The Hamiltonian incorporates constraints ensuring the required symmetries are maintained.

**Key observables.** Chiral charges ($Q_L, Q_R$), energy spectrum of normal modes ($\omega_k$), and the mixing amplitude $A(t)$ used to compare field theory vs. Villain model predictions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio U0/J0 determines the fixed point; the analysis distinguishes between sectors based on axial charge ($Q_A=0$, even, or odd).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on defining and utilizing an isomorphism ('Villain disentangler') between two formal descriptions of the Hilbert space, and often involves gauge-fixing constraints to simplify the Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the degrees of freedom for the fermionic Villain model used in the construction.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the complex Hilbert space structure using two formalisms (graded tensor product vs. Villain) and establishing their equivalence. It then constructs the Hamiltonian, solves it by mapping to harmonic oscillators, analyzes low-energy excitations based on axial charge sectors, and finally compares predictions for coupling constants derived from field theory versus the lattice model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct an exactly solvable Hamiltonian lattice model realizing a 1+1d Dirac fermion, with exact microscopic vector and axial vector $U(1)$ symmetries. The mixed anomaly between these is accommodated by the not-on-site action of the symmetries. Our Hilbert space is a $Z_2$-graded tensor product of local $Z_2$-graded Hilbert spaces which include infinite dimensional rotor degrees of freedom. The Hamiltonian becomes manifestly exactly solvable after a locality-preserving unitary mapping to an equivalent fermionic Villain Hilbert space. Our construction also allows an exactly solvable realization of interacting fermionic Luttinger liquids. At the free Dirac fixed point, our Hamiltonian contains irrelevant interactions, which we compute to leading order.

</details>

**Authors:** Tsutomu Momoi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03786v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</figure>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes fundamental symmetry constraints and precise frequency-moment sum rules governing the antisymmetric part of the dynamical spin structure factor. By analyzing these constraints using magnetic space groups, the authors predict which types of magnetic excitations are allowed or forbidden in various ordered magnets like helices and antiferromagnets. This provides powerful diagnostic tools for interpreting inelastic neutron scattering data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the rigorous application of group theory symmetries to derive exact spectral properties and sum rules is a powerful general technique applicable to any quantum many-body system where symmetry dictates observable selection rules.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives symmetry constraints and exact zero-temperature sum rules for the spin-component-antisymmetric part of the dynamical spin-structure-factor tensor.

**Main result.** These symmetries dictate selection rules for magnetic excitations, allowing finite spectral weight even when zeroth or first frequency moments vanish under specific crystal/spin symmetries.

**Method.** The analysis uses group theory applied to residual unitary and antiunitary operations of magnetic space groups and spin-space groups, combined with linear spin-wave calculations.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to general magnetic systems, including collinear antiferromagnets, rotation-related altermagnets, and planar helical magnets (e.g., on a triangular lattice).

**Key observables.** Antisymmetric dynamical spin-structure-factor tensor ($C_{\alphaeta}(\mathbf{q}, \omega)$), commutator spectral function $A_{\alphaeta}^{	ext{as}}(\mathbf{q}, \omega)$, and its frequency moments ($M^0, M^1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero temperature ($T=0$), momentum ($\mathbf{q}$), and the specific symmetry operations (unitary/antiunitary).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving constraints from residual space group symmetries; some calculations use linear spin-wave theory approximations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text discusses explicit realizations of these constraints via LSWT for helical magnets and general symmetry rules for different magnetic types.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the antisymmetric DSF, then derives general symmetry constraints using space groups, establishes moment sum rules (zeroth and first moments), and finally applies this framework to specific models like collinear antiferromagnets and planar helices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Polarized inelastic neutron scattering probes the handedness of magnetic excitations through the spin-component-antisymmetric part of the dynamical spin-structure-factor tensor. We derive its symmetry constraints under unitary and antiunitary residual operations of magnetic space groups and spin-space groups. These constraints determine the allowed tensor components, their parity under momentum reversal, and symmetry-enforced nodes. We then introduce the corresponding antisymmetric commutator spectral function and derive exact zero-temperature sum rules for its frequency moments. The zeroth moment is fixed by the uniform magnetization. For Heisenberg or XXZ exchange Hamiltonians, the first moment of the $xy$ component is fixed by a momentum-weighted static vector chirality. For compensated collinear antiferromagnets, the antisymmetric dynamical structure factor is forbidden when inversion or a translation relates the opposite-spin sublattices. In rotation-related altermagnets, however, an $xy$ component that is even under momentum reversal is allowed at generic wave vectors. Finite antisymmetric spectral weight can therefore coexist with vanishing zeroth and first moments of the commutator spectral function. In a planar helix, by contrast, a residual antiunitary spin-space-group symmetry makes the allowed $xy$ component odd under momentum reversal. Its first moment is generally nonzero and is fixed by a momentum-weighted static vector chirality through the first-moment sum rule. Linear spin-wave calculations for representative altermagnetic and helical models explicitly realize these symmetry and sum-rule constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an analytical framework using Typical Medium Theory (TMT) to study Anderson localization on the Bethe lattice with Cauchy disorder. The authors derive key results showing that the typical density of states vanishes linearly at a critical disorder strength, signaling the continuous collapse of extended electronic states. This work provides quantitative insights into the metal-insulator transition in highly disordered quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of TMT to characterize localization transitions provides powerful analytical tools applicable to understanding spectral properties in disordered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the Anderson localization transition (metal-insulator transition) in a disordered quantum system.

**Main result.** The typical density of states at the band center decreases linearly with disorder strength and vanishes at a critical disorder $W_c = D/2$, indicating continuous collapse of extended states.

**Method.** The authors employ Typical Medium Theory (TMT), which uses the geometric average of local quantities, to analyze the Anderson Hamiltonian on an infinite-connectivity Bethe lattice.

**Model / system.** The system is non-interacting spin-less fermions hopping on a Bethe lattice subjected to random on-site energies drawn from a Cauchy distribution. The model is described by an Anderson Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Typical Density of States ($ho_{W}^{	ext{typ}}(\omega)$), Mobility Edge ($\omega_c$), Number of Extended/Localized States ($N_{W}^{	ext{ext}}, N_{W}^{	ext{loc}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($W$), Disorder bandwidth parameter ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the use of Typical Medium Theory (TMT) over the conventional CPA, and analytical derivations rely on specific algebraic simplifications afforded by the Cauchy distribution.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 numerically shows the typical density of states ($ho_{W}^{	ext{typ}}$) for varying disorder strengths, illustrating its suppression and the shrinking energy interval.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the TMT self-consistency loop for this specific system. It derives analytical expressions for key observables like $ho_{W}^{	ext{typ}}(0)$ and $\omega_c$, analyzing the continuous transition from extended to localized states, and deriving approximate forms for the imaginary part of the self-energy ($\Sigma_I$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a combined numerical and analytically tractable realization of the typical medium theory (TMT) for Anderson localization on the infinite-connectivity Bethe lattice with Cauchy-distributed on-site disorder. Exploiting the special properties of the Cauchy distribution and the Bethe lattice, we derive an analytical expression for the typical density of states (TDOS) and obtain a simplified TMT self-consistency scheme. We show that the TDOS at the band center decreases linearly with disorder strength and vanishes at the critical disorder $W_c=D/2$. The resulting $ω$--$W$ phase diagram reveals the continuous collapse of the mobility edge and the disappearance of extended states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies an extension of the Fibonacci number system, called the fractional Fibonacci number system, to analyze localization in the Aubry-André model. It establishes that the nature of the particle density dictates whether the system undergoes a metal-insulator transition at $W=2t$ or remains localized even for small potentials. The work provides a mathematical framework for classifying critical behavior in quasiperiodic quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges deep mathematical concepts (number systems like Fibonacci bases) directly into condensed matter physics to predict critical behavior (localization transitions), offering a novel theoretical tool for analyzing quasiperiodic systems beyond standard band theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how number theoretic properties, specifically using Fibonacci number systems, can be employed to interpret the localization phase diagram of the many-body Aubry-André model.

**Main result.** The localization behavior depends critically on whether the particle density is rational or irrational; certain irrational densities exhibit different transition points ($W=0$ vs $W=2t$) compared to others.

**Method.** The authors introduce and utilize a 'fractional Fibonacci number system' to represent particle densities ($ho = N/L$), allowing for a systematic classification of localization regimes in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the many-body non-interacting Aubry-André model, characterized by a Hamiltonian involving hopping strength ($t$) and a quasiperiodic potential strength ($W$). The analysis is performed across varying particle densities ($ho = N/L$).

**Key observables.** Localization criterion (change in density representation as $L 	o \infty$), variance of polarization ($M_2$), and the transition point between metallic and insulating phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potential strength ($W$) relative to hopping strength ($t$); particle density ($ho$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Periodic boundary conditions are assumed; the classification based on base-$\varphi$ is noted to lose strict meaning in the true thermodynamic limit, depending on the approach path.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows numerical variance results for three different densities (rational, golden ratio irrational, other irrational) demonstrating size dependence changes at $W/t=2$. Figure 2 compares polarization variance for $\gamma^+$ and $\gamma^-$ approaching a limit, showing a transition at $W=2t$ for finite systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the fractional Fibonacci number system to analyze the Aubry-André model. It derives localization criteria based on density representation (Zeckendorf/base-$\varphi$) and compares results for different types of densities, concluding with a discussion on the limitations in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an extension of the Fibonacci number system, we call the fractional Fibonacci number system, which interpolates between the Fibonacci number system (used for natural numbers) and the irrational base-$φ$ number system, which can be used to represent real numbers. The number system finds its use in interpreting the localization phase diagram of the many-body non-interacting Aubry-André model. For finite system sizes a localization criterion can be obtained if the particle density is written in the fractional Fibonacci number system. In the thermodynamic limit, the criterion remains, but in this case the particle density is expressed in base-$φ$. The nature of the thermodynamic limit is also discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the complexity of writing large-scale quantum programs by proposing a high-level structured programming paradigm. It models computation not as gate sequences on individual qubits, but as transformations acting upon abstract 'quantum registers.' By formalizing this structure using an algebraic approach, it enables the design of tools like quantum SMT solvers that operate at a much higher level of abstraction.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides theoretical tools for abstracting quantum computation beyond simple circuit diagrams, which is crucial for designing scalable algorithms and verifying complex quantum protocols relevant to AMO physics simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Current quantum programming is too low-level, requiring manual management of individual qubits and gates for complex problems, leading to high cognitive load and errors.

**Main result.** The paper proposes a conceptual framework for high-level quantum structured programming by treating computation as semantically defined transformations acting on entire quantum registers.

**Method.** It develops an algebraic formalism that bridges high-level syntax with low-level semantics, allowing the design of a quantum satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) solver based purely on structural operations.

**Model / system.** The model views computation as operating on abstract 'quantum registers' ($\mathbf{q}$), which are treated as undivided computational entities rather than sequences of individual qubits. The formalism extends concepts like Hoare's laws to this quantum domain.

**Key observables.** The focus shifts from gate sequences to the transformation and composition rules governing entire quantum registers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** None explicitly stated beyond the abstract structure of the quantum registers themselves.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that computation can be successfully abstracted by defining transformations on register compositions, independent of underlying qubit details. The formalism's completeness regarding all possible quantum circuit laws is also noted as a limitation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the problem (low-level gate programming) to proposing a conceptual framework based on register composition, formalizing this with an algebraic formalism that defines recursive circuits and composition laws, culminating in the ability to use this structure for SMT solving.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Current quantum programs are mainly designed at the level of quantum gates acting on individual qubits; on a large scale and for complex problems this may involve a high cognitive load on the programmer, making the program specification nontrivial and error-prone. In this context, providing quantum programming with higher abstraction mechanisms will assist in making this task more manageable and robust against design errors. In this work, a conceptual framework is addressed following the notion of the whole quantum computation as a structure composed of quantum registers representing each an undivided entity. Thus, computation progresses through semantically well-defined transformations that act on, or entangle, quantum registers, thereby modifying the global state. Ultimately, the program reaches the desired state by following a specific composition strategy. With this in mind, high-level syntax is presented through an algebraic formalism that bridges them with their low-level semantics. Proposed syntax is based on certain well-know operations used on quantum algorithms that apply phase shifts upon logical condition satisfaction or leverage on parallel evaluation. Based solely on the formalized operations, a quantum satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) solver can be designed. At its core, this work contributes to establishing some methodological principles towards realizing a high-level quantum structured programming.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose a secure quantum communication protocol using mesoscopic optical states encoded via the first and second moments of photon number distributions. Security is guaranteed by utilizing the nonclassical correlations present in an accompanying twin-beam state resource against common eavesdropping attacks. The numerical simulations confirm that this approach maintains key generation rates, suggesting feasibility for future experimental realization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum key distribution in a high-loss regime by leveraging classical state properties combined with nonclassical entanglement resources, which is highly relevant to practical quantum networking research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To propose a quantum communication protocol that enhances robustness against photon loss by encoding information in mesoscopic optical states.

**Main result.** The protocol is shown to be secure against intercept-resend and beam-splitting attacks, yielding non-zero key generation rates under numerical simulation.

**Method.** Information is encoded using the first and second moments of photon-number distributions ($\langle n angle$ and $	ext{Var}(n)$) of classical states superimposed on a twin-beam state.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes mesoscopic optical states, specifically multi-mode twin-beam (TWB) states generated via spontaneous parametric down-conversion, requiring Photon-Number-Resolving (PNR) detectors for implementation.

**Key observables.** Photon-number moments ($\langle n angle$, $	ext{Var}(n)$), the noise reduction factor ($R$), and the Key Generation Rate (KGR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mesoscopic intensity regime, detection efficiency ($\eta=0.4$), TWB mean photon number ($\langle m_{Alice} angle$ in $[3.0, 10.0]$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of PNR detectors and that security relies on exploiting nonclassical correlations inherent to the twin-beam resource.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates photon number distributions and variances for various pseudo-thermal states versus $\langle n angle$. Figure 2 sketches the proposed communication scheme alongside models for intercept-resend and beam-splitting attacks.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by proposing an encoding based on moments, derives security witnesses using TWB correlations ($R<1$), analyzes performance metrics (TPR, KGR) against specific eavesdropping attacks, and concludes with feasibility statements for experimental implementation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mesoscopic optical states are a promising resource for quantum communication, combining robustness against losses with the preservation of genuine quantum features. Here, we propose a quantum communication protocol in which information is encoded in the first and second moments of the photon-number distributions of classical optical states, and then decoded by photon-number-resolving detectors. Security relies on the nonclassical photon-number correlations of a twin-beam state transmitted alongside the signal in the quantum channel, providing an experimentally accessible security witness against both intercept-resend and beam-splitter attacks investigated in this work. Numerical simulations performed in experimentally accessible parameter regimes support the feasibility and security of the proposed communication protocol, yielding nonzero key generation rates under the considered eavesdropping attacks, and motivating its future experimental implementation.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03755v1>
