**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel group-theoretic approach using multi-copy symmetries to diagnose non-Gaussianity in bosonic quantum states. By analyzing how copy-mixing transformations interact with Gaussian unitaries, the authors derive necessary symmetry identities that all Gaussian states must obey. Violation of these identities provides a concrete and experimentally testable witness for non-Gaussian character.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous tool rooted in symmetry theory (group theory) to detect non-Gaussianity in continuous variable systems, which is highly relevant for quantum optics and quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a robust method to characterize non-Gaussianity in continuous-variable quantum states, as traditional witnesses struggle due to the non-convex nature of Gaussian sets.

**Main result.** The authors developed a group-theoretic, multi-copy symmetry framework where violating specific identities derived from copy-mixing transformations certifies that a state is non-Gaussian.

**Method.** The method analyzes how passive linear optical transformations mixing multiple copies of a quantum state commute with Gaussian unitaries applied to each copy, leading to symmetry constraints violated by non-Gaussian states.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on bosonic systems (single-mode and multi-mode) described by continuous variables, utilizing concepts from Lie groups like the Heisenberg group and symplectic transformations.

**Key observables.** Non-Gaussianity witnesses derived from symmetry constraints; specific multi-copy expectation values $	ext{Tr}(ho^{\otimes k}\Gamma_d(O))$ compared against Gaussian reference values $W_G(O)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of copies ($k$), the thermal reference parameter (fixed via purity), and parameters defining the covariance matrix ($\sigma$) are crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the relevant symmetry structure can be captured by analyzing commutators involving copy-mixing transformations and Gaussian unitaries, which is extended to multi-mode systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the difficulty of non-Gaussianity witnesses to developing a group-theoretic framework based on copy-mixing symmetries. It derives specific commutation relations for Gaussian states and then proposes using violations of these identities as quantitative detectors, culminating in an experimental protocol.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gaussian states are fundamental in continuous-variable quantum information, yet characterizing non-Gaussianity remains challenging due to the non-convexity of the Gaussian set. Existing witnesses typically rely on Wigner negativity or other information-theoretic quantities. In this work, we develop a group-theoretic, multi-copy approach to detect non-Gaussianity in bosonic systems. We study passive linear optical transformations that mix copies of a quantum state and analyze their commutation with identical Gaussian unitaries applied to each copy. Orthogonal copy-mixing transformations commute with the symplectic part of the Gaussian action, while the displacement part restricts the symmetry to the stabilizer of the collective mode. This structure yields a family of witnesses satisfied by all single-mode Gaussian states. Fixing the thermal reference parameter via the purity, violation of these identities certifies non-Gaussianity. We illustrate the method with several single-mode examples and present an experimental protocol based on passive interferometry and photon-number-resolved detection, showing that the relevant multi-copy expectation values can be estimated from bounded phase observables. Finally, we extend the construction to multi-mode systems and discuss how the same symmetry framework may lead to quantitative measures of non-Gaussianity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work significantly advances the study of correlated electrons on the kagome lattice by developing an FFT-accelerated Quantum Monte Carlo method. The research confirms superfluid quantum criticality at specific fillings while demonstrating that proposed CDW orders are likely artifacts of finite system size. This computational breakthrough allows for precise determination of critical exponents.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of an $N^{2.49}$ computational scaling for a complex QMC simulation is highly relevant, providing access to larger systems than previously possible for studying quantum critical points in lattice models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates interaction-driven correlated states, specifically the competition between superfluid (SF) and charge-density-wave (CDW) orders on the kagome lattice.

**Main result.** Large-scale simulations reveal zero-temperature superfluid quantum criticality at the Dirac filling, while evidence suggests that previously proposed CDW orders are likely finite-size effects.

**Method.** The authors developed and applied an accelerated Determinant Quantum Monte Carlo (DQMC) scheme combining FFT techniques with delay-update algorithms to overcome previous computational limitations.

**Model / system.** The system is the attractive Hubbard model defined on the kagome lattice, characterized by a Hamiltonian involving nearest-neighbor hopping ($t$) and an attractive interaction ($U < 0$).

**Key observables.** Superfluid order parameter (P), correlation ratio ($R_{	ext{Pair}}$), and momentum-dependent CDW order parameter ($D(\vec{Q})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dirac filling ($ho = 2/3$), critical interaction $|U|_c \approx 4.79$, and system sizes up to $L=24$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the accelerated DQMC scheme accurately models the physics, allowing for reliable finite-size scaling analyses.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare computational time scaling (FFT vs. DGEMM) and show data collapse for SF order parameters at $ho=2/3$. Other figures illustrate finite-size extrapolations of CDW order parameters at wave vectors $\Gamma$ and K.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the model setup, introduces the FFT-accelerated DQMC methodology (combining FFT with delay updates), presents results on SF criticality via finite-size scaling, and concludes by analyzing the stability of various CDW states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent discovery of several families of kagome materials and experimental realization of optical kagome lattices have stimulated growing numerical studies of interaction-driven correlated states on the kagome lattice. Among the available numerical approaches, determinant quantum Monte Carlo (DQMC) is a powerful method for investigating such strongly correlated states. However, the accessible system sizes of existing DQMC simulations remain limited, preventing reliable finite-size scaling analyses. Here we develop a general acceleration scheme based on fast Fourier transform (FFT) for propagator multiplications on composite lattices and combine it with the delay-update algorithm, enabling simulations on system sizes twice as large as those of previous DQMC studies, allowing reliable finite-size scaling analyses of the attractive kagome-lattice Hubbard model. Our large-scale simulations reveal the interaction-driven zero-temperature superfluid quantum criticality at the Dirac filling and provide reliable estimates of the associated critical exponents. Besides, we find no evidence that the previously proposed triangle-rule charge-density-wave order survives in the thermodynamic limit, suggesting that it is likely a finite-size effect. Moreover, for system sizes accessible in current two-dimensional optical lattice experiments, the combined FFT and delay-update scheme exhibits an effective computational cost scaling as $N^{2.49}$, substantially below the $\mathcal{O}(N^3)$ computational cost of conventional DQMC simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel framework to calculate robust margins for quantum gate fidelity when system parameters are uncertain. By deriving structure-specific Lipschitz bounds, it certifies the maximum allowed perturbation size while guaranteeing the final gate fidelity stays above a set threshold. This moves beyond simple local sensitivity analysis by providing actionable safety boundaries for quantum control protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, quantifiable guarantees on the stability of quantum gates under realistic parameter uncertainties, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum hardware and understanding experimental limitations in quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a method to quantify the robustness margin for finite-time quantum control when the system Hamiltonian is subject to structured, small perturbations.

**Main result.** A structure-specific fidelity-threshold robustness margin is derived, providing a certified minimum perturbation radius ensuring gate fidelity remains above a specified threshold $F_T$.

**Method.** The analysis uses differential sensitivity bounds and establishes a Lipschitz constant for the fidelity function with respect to the perturbing parameter. An iterative algorithm refines this margin by taking safe steps toward the first boundary.

**Model / system.** The system is a finite-dimensional closed quantum system evolving under piecewise-constant coherent control, governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian that includes structured affine perturbations from a single scalar parameter $\mu$.

**Key observables.** Normalized trace-amplitude gate fidelity ($F(t_f)$), the sensitivity of fidelity to perturbation ($\zeta_\mu^0$), and the certified robust margin ($M$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control time horizon $t_f$, the target fidelity threshold $F_T$, and the magnitude of the structured Hamiltonian perturbations $\mu$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to scalar, one-parameter structured Hamiltonian perturbations; it does not cover simultaneous multi-parameter uncertainty or open-system dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate fidelity error trajectories under various perturbations ($H_0, H_1, H_2$) and compare the calculated robustness margins ($M$) against the nominal differential sensitivity magnitudes ($|\zeta_j|$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using fidelity derivatives to derive a finite margin certificate. It then applies this method to specific three-qubit gate control examples, comparing the resulting structure-dependent margins across different perturbation types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a structure-specific fidelity-threshold robustness margin for finite-dimensional closed quantum systems under piecewise-constant coherent control. A scalar physical parameter may perturb the drift, a control Hamiltonian, or another declared Hamiltonian component across the control horizon. A differential sensitivity bound for trace-amplitude gate fidelity yields a threshold-dependent Lipschitz constant on the connected safe parameter component and hence a certified finite perturbation radius. Recentering this certificate produces an iterative one-dimensional method that takes certified safe steps toward the first fidelity-threshold boundary in either parameter direction. A three-qubit gate-control example shows that these finite margins vary by up to a factor of three across controllers of comparable nominal fidelity and contain structure-dependent information not captured by nominal differential sensitivity alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper significantly improves theoretical bounds for determining the required polynomial degree ($d$) needed to simulate quantum time evolution using Quantum Signal Processing. By refining the mathematical bounding of the error tail in the Jacobi-Anger series, the authors reduce the estimated overhead for Hamiltonian simulation tasks by a factor of $e/2$. This is a crucial algorithmic refinement for quantum computing resource estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, quantitative improvement in the resource estimation for quantum simulation algorithms, directly impacting the feasibility estimates for implementing complex dynamics on near-term quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the constant multiplicative factor determining the required polynomial degree ($d$) needed for approximating quantum time evolution operators using Quantum Signal Processing (QSP). This addresses a gap between current theoretical bounds and the optimal asymptotic constant.

**Main result.** The authors derive rigorous, tighter upper bounds on $d$ by carefully analyzing the Bessel tail in the Jacobi-Anger series. This improvement reduces overhead estimates for Hamiltonian simulation tasks by a factor of approximately $e/2$.

**Method.** The work combines quantum signal processing theory with advanced polynomial approximation techniques. Specifically, it employs Kapteyn's and Watson's inequalities to bound the truncation error arising from approximating the time evolution operator $e^{-i	au H}$.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Hamiltonian simulation, which approximates the time-evolution operator $e^{-i	au H}$ by finding a polynomial approximation of $e^{-i	au x}$ over $[-1, 1]$. The underlying mathematical structure involves the Jacobi-Anger expansion and Bessel functions.

**Key observables.** The required polynomial truncation degree ($d$), the approximation error ($\epsilon$), and the time evolution parameter ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The asymptotic regime $	au \gg 1$ is crucial, as the leading constant in the bound approaches the optimal value of 1.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing rigorous bounds for the Bessel tail using specific mathematical inequalities (Kapteyn's and Watson's).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the derived degree bounds against the exact Bessel tail, demonstrating convergence towards the optimal result as $	au$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the connection between Hamiltonian simulation and polynomial approximation via QSP. It then develops increasingly tight mathematical bounds on $d$ using successive applications of inequalities (Kapteyn's followed by Watson's) to rigorously bound the truncation error term.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum signal processing (QSP) serves as the asymptotically optimal technique for Hamiltonian simulation on a quantum computer. By approximating the time evolution operator via the Jacobi-Anger expansion, the Hamiltonian simulation problem reduces to a problem in polynomial approximation theory: find a sufficient degree-$d$ polynomial series to approximate $e^{-iτx}$ on $[-1,1]$ within error $ε$. While $d\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(τ)$ is known to be asymptotically optimal, there exists a gap between state-of-the-art bounds and the optimal constant multiplicative factor, which is approximately equal to 1. Here, we close this gap almost entirely, to the point where possible future improvements will not be of practical significance. Our improvement resides in a careful treatment of the Bessel tail in the Jacobi-Anger series using Kapteyn's and Watson's inequalities, thereby reducing the overhead estimates for all Hamiltonian simulation tasks on quantum computers by a factor of $\approx e/2$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QuScope, an open-source Python framework that models the entire process of Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) image formation using quantum circuits. It maps electron wavefunction propagation onto quantum gates, allowing simulations of techniques like CTEM and STEM. The work validates its accuracy by matching classical results to unit fidelity while also providing crucial resource estimates for future quantum hardware implementation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, physics-driven application demonstrating how complex wave phenomena can be formulated as quantum circuits, offering valuable benchmarks for quantum simulation algorithms beyond standard chemistry or materials Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is simulating the complex, coherent evolution of an electron wavefunction through a specimen in Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM).

**Main result.** The authors developed QuScope, an open-source framework that successfully maps the entire TEM imaging pipeline onto quantum circuits, achieving unit fidelity with classical simulations.

**Method.** The methodology involves amplitude encoding the $N 	imes N$ wavefunction into qubits and implementing physical operations (like Fresnel propagation or phase transmission) using diagonal unitaries conjugated by Quantum Fourier Transforms (QFTs).

**Model / system.** The system is TEM, simulating electron wave propagation through specimens like $	ext{SrTiO}_3$. The physics maps to a quantum circuit model where optical elements are unitary gates.

**Key observables.** Image intensity ($|\psi_{	ext{image}}(\mathbf{r})|^2$), state fidelity ($\langle\psi_q|\psi_cangle|^2$), and resource estimates (gate counts, depth).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Grid size ($N 	imes N$), scattering strength ($\sigma V$), and error rates for noise analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on the assumption that quantum simulation can accurately model wave propagation; limitations include the exponential cost of synthesizing arbitrary diagonal unitaries and decoherence effects in deep circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate transfer characteristics, probe intensity channeling through crystalline materials ($	ext{SrTiO}_3$), and comparisons between quantum and classical multislice results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem mapping to quantum circuits, details the implementation of core components (QFTs, diagonal unitaries), validates the framework against classical simulations across various TEM techniques (CTEM, STEM), and concludes with resource estimation for near-term hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Image formation in transmission electron microscopy (TEM) is governed by the coherent evolution of the electron wavefunction through the specimen and the objective lens. This physics maps naturally onto the gate model of quantum computation. We present QuScope, an open-source Python framework that expresses the complete TEM image-formation pipeline as quantum circuits. The $N\times N$ electron wavefunction is amplitude-encoded in $2\log_2 N$ qubits, and every optical element, including phase-grating transmission, Fresnel propagation between specimen slices, and the aberrated objective lens, is implemented as a diagonal unitary conjugated by quantum Fourier transforms. On this foundation, QuScope v0.2.0 implements validated imaging pipelines, covering conventional TEM under the phase-object approximation, full multislice CTEM and STEM for thick specimens. All quantum results reported here come from exact, noise-free statevector simulation of the circuits on classical hardware, and every result is validated against a classical twin implementation. Quantum and classical multislice exit waves agree to unit fidelity, and all physical constants are verified against standard references. We provide transpiled quantum-resource estimates for each algorithm, an analysis of the diagonal-synthesis bottleneck that governs near-term hardware execution, and a fully tested, documented, and pip-installable package. QuScope v0.2.0 is available at https://github.com/QuScope/QuScope.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of running complex quantum circuit simulations on hardware accelerators limited to real arithmetic. The authors propose a 'realification rewrite' that mathematically transforms any complex tensor network into an equivalent real one. This method successfully demonstrates superior performance over standard complex-handling baselines when executed on real-only matrix engines.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete computational pathway for simulating complex quantum dynamics (which often involve complex amplitudes) using commodity real-valued AI accelerators, making advanced quantum simulation more accessible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating quantum circuits via tensor networks on modern matrix accelerators (NPUs, TPUs) that are restricted to real-valued matrix multiplications (GEMM).

**Main result.** The authors introduce a 'realification rewrite' that maps complex tensor networks to equivalent real ones, achieving performance competitive with existing methods.

**Method.** They develop a mathematical rewrite that replaces complex tensor contractions with sequences of real-valued tensor operations using an algebra structure tensor (M), allowing computation on real-only hardware.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum circuit simulation represented by tensor network contraction over complex numbers. The physical system involves general quantum circuits, including random, Clifford+T, QAOA, and VQE instances.

**Key observables.** Arithmetic overhead in real multiplications ($1 + 2m + r$), time complexity ($	ext{tcR}$), space complexity ($	ext{scR}$), and read–write cost ($	ext{rwcR}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cost law shows an overhead never exceeding $3	imes$ relative to real contraction; the performance is shown on an Ascend 910 NPU.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that existing software stacks optimized for real GEMMs can be utilized, and it relies on a tree-dependent structure choice during rewriting.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process from quantum circuit to complex tensor network to final real-valued rewrite. Figure 2 details how the multiplication tensor M is simplified to C by absorbing a phase factor Z. Table 5 reports optimized complexities for various circuits.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the hardware mismatch (complex vs. real GEMM). It then introduces the 'realification rewrite' and proves a tight cost law based on volume fractions ($m, r$). The core of the work involves applying this method to benchmark multiple circuit types across different optimization strategies, concluding with performance results on specific hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tensor-network contraction simulates quantum circuits, but modern matrix accelerators (NPUs, TPUs) expose only real GEMM pipelines, so the complex networks of quantum simulation must be reconstructed in software. We resolve the mismatch by a realification rewrite that maps any complex tensor network to a real one. At each merge of two complex tensors, a rank-3 structure tensor realizes Gauss's three-multiplication (3M) formula; contractions with one or no complex operand need only two or one real products. We prove a tight cost law: overhead $1 + 2m + r$ in real multiplications, where $m$ and $r$ are the volume fractions of two- and one-complex-operand contractions, never exceeding $3\times$ relative to real contraction, with every intermediate at most doubled in size. On 67 circuits (random, Clifford+$T$, QAOA, VQE), the law holds across the real-to-complex range and complex-gate placement, not count, governs cost. Contraction orders transfer from the complex network with a relative arithmetic-cost gap below $5\times 10^{-4}$ on 66 of 67 circuits; the exception closes under a few steps of low-temperature simulated annealing. On an Ascend 910 NPU the rewrite beat both the four-real-GEMM baseline and a per-GEMM Gauss lowering on all twelve random circuits and on 52 of 55 structured cells (three cells slower by at most 12\%); the four-GEMM baseline was slower by a median $1.7\times$ (random) and $1.4\times$ (structured). Realification makes complex tensor-network contraction native to real-only matrix engines.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated mathematical machinery using a non-commutative Laplace transform to relate path space observables (quantum path signatures) to algebraic equations from random matrix theory. It successfully derives the full genus expansion of these loop equations, providing deep structural insights into quantum field theories governed by such spectral relations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques developed—relating geometric/path integrals (signatures) to formal power series and spectral curves—are powerful tools for analyzing quantum field theories in dimensions where standard methods fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a rigorous mathematical framework connecting path space analysis (quantum path signatures) to algebraic structures derived from random matrix theory via a non-commutative Laplace transform.

**Main result.** They successfully derive the genus-$g$ loop equations for the quantum path signature, confirming consistency across multiple computational methods up to genus one and providing a systematic expansion in $1/N$.

**Method.** The core method involves introducing and utilizing a non-commutative Laplace transform that maps convolution products on path space functionals (signatures) to algebraic products of formal power series.

**Model / system.** The analysis centers on the quantum path signature, derived from random unitary matrix-valued paths $U(\gamma)$, which is related to Wilson loops in gauge theory and planar limits of matrix models.

**Key observables.** Quantum Path Signature ($S$), Planar Wilson Line Observable ($W$), Genus-$g$ corrections ($W_g$), and the non-commutative resolvent ($R_\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large-$N$ limit (planar limit), genus $g$, and path geometry $\gamma(t)$ are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes factorial decay of the signature moments for the existence of the planar limit, and it notes that the full solution theory for interacting models remains open due to non-closure.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the structure implies showing recursive relations (e.g., Eq. 66) and comparing results from different computational approaches (discretized loops vs. combinatorial forms).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the non-commutative Laplace transform to map path convolution to algebraic products; it then applies this to derive the planar loop equation; subsequently, it extends this framework to systematically calculate genus-$g$ corrections ($W_g$) via integral equations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a non-commutative Laplace transform between functionals on path space and formal series in a tensor algebra, under which a natural convolution of path functionals becomes an algebraic product of series. Applying it to a random unitary matrix-valued path development - the quantum path signature - we show that the governing planar loop equations take a non-commutative spectral form. We then extend the loop equations to a $1/N$ genus expansion, organised by topological recursion, and obtain a hierarchy of integral equations on path space for the corrections.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that calculating the minimum number of CNOT gates required to implement a given linear transformation (CNOT-Distance) is an NP-complete problem. The authors achieve this by constructing specific matrices whose gate count exactly matches the size of the minimum vertex cover of a graph. This result establishes a strong theoretical lower bound on the complexity of quantum circuit synthesis.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental computational hardness results for quantum circuit synthesis, which is crucial for understanding the limits of implementing desired unitary operations using physical hardware constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The problem addressed is determining if a given invertible linear transformation $A \in GL(N, 2)$ can be implemented using at most $K$ CNOT gates ($	ext{CNOT-Distance}$).

**Main result.** The authors prove that the $	ext{CNOT-Distance}$ problem is NP-complete under all-to-all connectivity.

**Method.** The proof relies on a reduction from Minimum Vertex Cover (an NP-complete problem) and involves analyzing the structure of CNOT circuits by mapping them to XOR directed acyclic graphs.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum circuit synthesis using only CNOT gates acting on fixed labeled wires, represented mathematically by linear transformations over $\mathbb{F}_2$.

**Key observables.** $\ell_{	ext{CNOT}}(A)$ (minimum gate count), $	au(G)$ (minimum vertex cover size).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $N$ (number of wires/qubits), $K$ (gate budget).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes all-to-all connectivity between any pair of distinct wires.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the NP-completeness by constructing a specific matrix $A_G$ derived from an arbitrary graph $G$, showing that $\ell_{	ext{CNOT}}(A_G)$ exactly encodes the minimum vertex cover size, thus linking circuit complexity to graph theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Given $A\in\operatorname{GL}(N,2)$ and an integer $K$, we ask whether $A$ can be implemented by at most $K$ CNOT gates on fixed labelled wires with all-to-all connectivity. We prove that this problem is NP-complete. From a finite simple graph $G=(V,E)$, we construct an upper-unitriangular matrix $A_G\in\operatorname{GL}(2|V|+|E|+1,2)$ satisfying $\ell_{\mathrm{CNOT}}(A_G)=2|V|+2|E|+τ(G)$, where $τ(G)$ is the minimum vertex-cover size. Each target matrix has $O(N)$ nonzero entries and row Hamming weight at most four. The lower bound unfolds an arbitrary CNOT circuit into an XOR directed acyclic graph and applies projection--contraction operations, allowing cancellation and unrestricted reuse of intermediate parities. For this family, the optimum is unchanged by any finite number of clean or borrowed ancillary wires that must be restored. A polynomial-time decoder further yields NP-hardness of approximation within every fixed additive constant and, through an L-reduction from Minimum Vertex Cover on cubic graphs, APX-hardness of the associated CNOT-circuit optimisation problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents novel algorithms that factor complex logic expressions (ESOP) by mapping them onto 2D matrices and using rectangle covering techniques. This factorization allows for the creation of equivalent, but significantly simpler, quantum circuits. The resulting cost reductions in both classical gate counts and quantum circuit metrics are substantial, making it a key tool for optimizing quantum computation resource estimates.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on circuit minimization rather than physical Hamiltonian simulation, the techniques developed for efficient logical decomposition and resource estimation are fundamental to designing scalable quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is optimizing quantum circuit costs associated with implementing complex logic functions represented as Exclusive-Sum-Of-Products (ESOP) expressions.

**Main result.** The proposed factorization algorithms significantly reduce both the classical literal count and the quantum Maslov cost of realizing these circuits, achieving reductions between 20% and 95%.

**Method.** The method involves representing the ESOP expression as a 2D matrix and applying specialized 'Rectangle Covering methods' (Disjoint and Even-Odd) to find an equivalent, factored form.

**Model / system.** The model is abstract quantum circuit realization for AND/EXOR logic gates. Costs are measured using the Maslov cost for both quantum oracle and automaton circuits, which are built upon generalized n-bit Toffoli gates.

**Key observables.** Literal count (L(E)), Maslov cost for quantum automata (A(E)), and Maslov cost for quantum oracles (O(E)).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity of the ESOP expression, which must be FPRM with cube degrees at most 2 for direct matrix representation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For quantum oracle circuits, intermediate variables in factored forms require 'restoring' via mirroring gates to maintain equivalence with the original circuit structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate gate realizations (CNOT, Toffoli), comparisons of costs before and after factorization using matrix representations, and demonstrations of the disjoint rectangle covering process on these matrices.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of ESOP cost optimization, details the matrix representation and the two rectangle covering algorithms (Disjoint and Even-Odd), presents comparative results across multiple benchmarks for literal count, automata cost, and oracle cost, and concludes with an analysis comparing algorithmic efficiency.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The implementation of quantum circuits is currently very expensive, especially due to the usage of large Toffoli gates. Therefore, it is critical to optimize circuit costs by factoring expressions as they become more complex. In the proposed algorithms to factor ESOP expressions, each product term is converted into a cell in a 2D matrix, and optimal factored AND/EXOR solutions are determined using Disjoint and Even-Odd Rectangle covering methods. Two Python programs implementing these algorithms were tested and evaluated using well-known benchmarks. The results showed that both the literal counts used in classical logic circuits as well as the Maslov cost used in quantum circuits was reduced by 20%-95% depending on expression size.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Harvest, a novel compiler designed for fault-tolerant quantum computation utilizing lattice surgery. It solves the critical problem where scheduling, placement, and magic-state resource management cannot be optimized in isolation due to shared physical constraints. By co-optimizing these factors, Harvest achieves substantial speedups and dramatically improves hardware resource utilization.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on compilation rather than fundamental physics, the resource management challenges—optimizing spatial layout alongside temporal execution constraints—are highly relevant to designing physical implementations of quantum circuits using superconducting or trapped-ion architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a quantum compiler that can co-optimize placement, routing, scheduling, and magic-state supply for lattice-surgery operations in fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Main result.** Harvest achieves significant performance gains on benchmarks, showing an average speedup of 4.83x (up to 17.8x) over sequential execution while reclaiming substantial unused physical resources.

**Method.** The authors propose HARVEST, a resource-aware compilation approach that uses a patch-level intermediate representation (H-IR) to jointly optimize scheduling with circuit-aware placement and congestion-aware routing.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a fault-tolerant quantum processor using topological codes, where operations are executed via 'lattice surgery' on a 2D grid of physical patches that can hold data, magic states, or act as routes.

**Key observables.** Average speedup (e.g., 4.83x), schedule length improvement (up to 1.35x), and the percentage of reclaimed unused resources (e.g., up to 72.0% magic-state patches).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization must jointly consider data dependencies, physical resource conflicts (ports/routes), and consumable non-Clifford resources (magic states).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Independent optimization of placement, routing, scheduling, or magic-state supply is insufficient because operations contend for shared physical resources like ports and routes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the patch-level execution model showing data, routing, and magic-state patches; comparisons show speedup gains over naive serial execution by integrating resource awareness into scheduling.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of existing compilers, proposes the HARVEST framework with its H-IR, details the co-optimization pipeline (placement $ightarrow$ scheduling $ightarrow$ pruning), and validates it against standard quantum benchmark suites.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum processors based on topological codes execute programs through lattice surgery, where operations must be mapped, routed, and supplied with magic states across a 2D grid of physical patches. Non-Clifford operations require these magic states, produced either by distillation factories or by cultivation, each trading footprint against preparation latency, and delivering a magic state to the data patches that consume it requires routing through the same shared layout as every other operation. Yet placement, routing, scheduling, and magic-state supply cannot be optimized in isolation: two operations with no circuit-level dependency can still contend for the same ports, routes, or magic-state terminals once placed, so a compiler that decouples instruction scheduling from magic-state generation, or hard-codes a single generation protocol, is forced to trade execution time against layout footprint instead of co-optimizing both across protocols.   We present Harvest, a resource-aware compilation approach for lattice-surgery that co-optimizes magic-state consumption with circuit-aware placement and congestion-aware routing under a protocol-agnostic resource model, then reclaims unused layout footprint after scheduling. Across standard benchmark suites (QAOA, QFT, QASMBench), Harvest achieves an average speedup of $4.83\times$ (up to $17.8\times$) over sequential execution, improves schedule length by up to $1.35\times$ through circuit-aware placement, and reclaims up to $72.0\%$ of unused magic-state patches and $33.9\%$ of unused routing patches.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces improved quantum algorithms designed to solve for optimal policies in Reinforcement Learning within Markov Decision Processes. By combining quantum subroutines like quantum mean estimation and maximum finding, the authors significantly reduce the required query complexity compared to prior classical and quantum methods. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum computation's role in solving complex sequential decision-making problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is ML/AI, the core methodology relies entirely on developing and analyzing complex quantum algorithms (quantum mean estimation, amplitude amplification techniques) applied to optimization problems, making it highly relevant to quantum computation theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing quantum algorithms to compute approximate optimal policies for Reinforcement Learning (RL) in Markov Decision Processes (MDPs). This aims to overcome the computational challenges posed by large state/action spaces.

**Main result.** The authors propose new quantum algorithms that improve query complexities compared to previous works, achieving bounds approaching established quantum lower limits for both finite- and infinite-horizon MDPs.

**Method.** The approach combines standard value iteration techniques with advanced quantum subroutines, specifically quantum mean estimation and quantum maximum finding. These are integrated using total-variance reduction techniques.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time Markov Decision Process (MDP), studied in finite-horizon or infinite-horizon discounted settings. The process relies on estimating optimal policies ($\pi^*$) and value functions ($V^*$).

**Key observables.** Query complexity, $\epsilon$-optimality for policies and value functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** State space size (S), action space size (A), finite horizon length (H), discount factor ($\gamma$), and the desired error tolerance ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes access to transition probabilities via an oracle, which is quantum accessible-environment ($Q_p$) in the quantum setting.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; references suggest comparison of classical vs. quantum query bounds in a table format.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon foundational work in MDPs and RL theory, proposing novel quantum algorithms that combine quantum mean estimation and maximum finding to improve sample complexity bounds for solving optimal policies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reinforcement learning is a subfield of machine learning that studies how an agent interacts with an environment in order to extract as large a reward as possible. A standard approach to study such interaction is through Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) and the task of choosing an optimal policy --- a function that tells the agent which action to take. In this work, we study two types of MDPs --- finite-horizon and infinite-horizon discounted --- and propose new quantum algorithms for computing approximate optimal policies. Our quantum algorithms are based on a new combination of standard value iteration and quantum subroutines like quantum mean estimation and quantum maximum finding, overall enhanced with techniques from sample-optimal classical algorithms. Our resulting query complexities improve upon previous works, thus approaching already established quantum lower bounds.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.03656v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops and applies an adaptation of the Iterative Linear Quadratic Regulator (iLQR) tailored specifically to the Lie-group structure of SU(N). By incorporating this geometric constraint into the optimization process, the authors demonstrate improved convergence speed for quantum gate synthesis compared to standard methods. The findings highlight the value of group theory in enhancing optimal control algorithms for multi-qubit systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for optimizing quantum controls by respecting the underlying group structure (SU(N)), which is crucial for developing scalable, physically constrained gate synthesis protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is designing complex multi-qubit quantum gates using Quantum Optimal Control (QOC) theory.

**Main result.** Incorporating the Lie-group geometry into the iLQR formulation accelerates convergence compared to Euclidean methods in unconstrained settings, though it increases sensitivity to initialization under constraints.

**Method.** The authors adapt the Iterative Linear Quadratic Regulator (iLQR) by formulating updates and cost functions directly on the Special Unitary group SU(N), comparing this Lie-group approach against standard Euclidean iLQR.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum dynamics evolving on the special unitary group SU(N), where control is achieved by applying time-dependent Hamiltonian terms (drive fields) to synthesize target gates.

**Key observables.** Convergence speed, gate fidelity, and the resulting infidelities of synthesized gates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison hinges on two regimes: an idealized unconstrained setting versus a constrained setting limited to 2-local Hamiltonian terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies heavily on Taylor expansions and linearizing the system dynamics around the current trajectory, which introduces approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show infidelity results for various multi-qubit gates (e.g., three- and five-qubit gates) comparing performance across different optimization score functions and iteration steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds mathematically by deriving necessary Taylor expansions on Lie groups, detailing the iLQR update equations for SU(N), and then presenting numerical results comparing the convergence of the Lie-group vs. Euclidean formulations under varying constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum optimal control theory, gradient-based trajectory optimization techniques have proven versatile in designing multi-qubit quantum gates. Furthermore, incorporating the underlying Lie-group structure can accelerate the optimization process. In this work, we adapt the Lie-group formulation of the iterative linear quadratic regulator (iLQR) to the special unitary group SU(N) and apply it to quantum gate synthesis, systematically comparing it against the standard Euclidean iLQR formulation across multiple two- to five-qubit gates. We find that in the idealized, unconstrained setting, where all Lie-algebra basis elements are available as drive Hamiltonian terms, the Lie-group formulation converges faster than the Euclidean iLQR formulation. If drive terms are constrained to 2-local Hamiltonian terms, the Lie-group variant converges faster in early optimization iterations, but exhibits greater sensitivity to initialization and a stronger tendency towards local minima. These results demonstrate that incorporating Lie-group geometry into iLQR substantially improves convergence and highlight important next steps for improvements in constrained control settings.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the problem of achieving optimal and deterministic quantum search on the weighted simplex of complete graphs. The authors provide novel analytical proofs demonstrating that specific bridge weight conditions ($w=M$) guarantee both asymptotic optimality ($\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{N})$) and determinism for the quantum walk algorithm. It is significant because it rigorously characterizes the relationship between graph structure, edge weights, and search complexity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous theoretical bounds on quantum search complexity ($\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{N})$) for a complex network structure, offering deep insights into how local structural properties (like weights) dictate global algorithmic efficiency in quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to analytically prove optimal and deterministic quantum search algorithms for a specific graph structure: the simplex of complete graphs.

**Main result.** The authors provide the first analytical proof that optimal quantum search occurs when the bridge weight equals $M$, and they establish conditions ($\sqrt{M} \leq w \leq M$) under which the search is both asymptotically optimal and deterministic.

**Method.** The research combines analytical proofs using continuous-time quantum walk Hamiltonians with numerical demonstrations across various parameter regimes (bridge weights).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a weighted simplex of complete graphs, containing $N = M(M+1)$ vertices. The search dynamics are governed by a time evolution operator derived from a specific Hamiltonian involving the adjacency matrix and bridge weights ($w$).

**Key observables.** Search runtime (compared to $\sqrt{N}$), success probability, and the probability distribution across basis states $|aangle$ (marked vertex) and neighboring vertices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The weight of the bridges ($w$) relative to $M$, specifically comparing regimes like $w=M$, $w=\sqrt{M}$, and $\sqrt{M} \ll w \ll M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the single-marked vertex case, and it explicitly shows that graph connectivity measures (like algebraic connectivity) are unreliable indicators of fast search performance in this family.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize search results across different weights $w$, showing runtime vs. weight scaling; Figure 3 details probability overlaps and runtime for specific weight scalings ($w=M^\alpha$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model, analyzing the quantum walk Hamiltonian in reduced subspaces using perturbation theory, deriving optimal rates for various weight regimes, and finally comparing search performance against graph connectivity metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The simplex of complete graphs, also known as the first-order truncated simplex lattice, is a network of $M+1$ identical complete graphs, each with $M$ vertices, such that each clique contains an edge or bridge to every other clique. It contains $N = M(M+1)$ vertices, and previous asymptotic results using a continuous-time quantum walk to search this graph for a single marked vertex have either numerically demonstrated an optimal runtime of $O(\sqrt{N})$, or analytically proved a deterministic success probability of 1, but not both, even when the bridges are weighted. In this paper, we give the first analytical proof of optimal quantum search on this graph, proving that it occurs when the weight of the bridges equals $M$. In addition, we numerically show that the optimal runtime is achieved more broadly whenever the weight is at least $\sqrt{M}$. Furthermore, the algorithm is also deterministic when the weight scales between $\sqrt{M}$ and $M$, and this is the first example of quantum search on the simplex of complete graphs that is both asymptotically optimal and deterministic. In addition, for weights where the algorithm is nondeterministic, we give a way to find the marked vertex by inspecting neighboring vertices. Finally, while it is known that connectivity is not a reliable indicator of fast quantum search when comparing different graph families, we show that it is also unreliable within the graph family of weighted simplex of complete graphs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Physics-Informed Quantum Machine Learning (PIQML) framework designed to solve nonlinear ODEs using near-term quantum hardware. It achieves this by embedding initial conditions as hard constraints and formulating the loss function around satisfying the differential equation residual itself. The method demonstrates high accuracy on complex dynamics, positioning it as a viable tool for quantum simulation in the NISQ era.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on numerical methods, the reliance on quantum circuits for derivative calculation (parameter-shift rule) and the integration of physical laws into the training objective makes it relevant to developing quantum algorithms for simulating complex physical dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is solving nonlinear first-order ordinary differential equations (ODEs) using quantum machine learning techniques suitable for near-term quantum devices.

**Main result.** The proposed PIQML framework successfully learns solutions to challenging ODEs, including highly oscillatory ones, showing close agreement with high-precision classical numerical benchmarks.

**Method.** It employs a Physics-Informed Quantum Machine Learning (PIQML) framework that uses hard constraint embedding to analytically enforce initial conditions and focuses the loss function on satisfying the differential equation residual.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) as variational ansatzes, encoding input variables via Fourier or Chebyshev feature maps. The solution is constructed by explicitly enforcing initial conditions through a function mapper.

**Key observables.** Differential equation residual ($\mathcal{L}_{	ext{res}}$), reference data fitting loss ($\mathcal{L}_{	ext{data}}$), and the comparison of quantum-derived solutions against high-precision classical numerical benchmarks (e.g., RK45).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method is specifically designed for the NISQ era, utilizing techniques like the parameter-shift rule for gradient evaluation to avoid classical discretization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that quantum circuits can effectively approximate the required solution manifold and that hard constraint embedding overcomes convergence issues associated with soft penalty terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the PIQML model's ability to accurately capture oscillatory and decaying behavior for damped oscillators, showing close alignment of function values and derivatives compared to analytical solutions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the motivation (DE solving challenges vs. NISQ limits), details the methodology covering feature maps, ansatz structure, hard constraint embedding, parameter-shift rule gradient computation, and the composite loss formulation, concluding with validation on various ODEs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum algorithms based on linear-system approaches for solving differential equations demand qubit and precision resources beyond near-term capabilities. To address these challenges, this work proposes a physics-informed quantum machine learning (PIQML) framework with hard constraint embedding, specifically designed for NISQ era. Within this framework, parameterized quantum circuits serve as machine learning models, where the input variable is encoded into a high-dimensional feature space via a Fourier feature map. Subsequently, to eliminate approximation errors in critical physical conditions, the solution is constructed through a rigorously designed function mapper that analytically enforces initial conditions as hard constraints. Crucially, we compute derivatives with respect to the input variable using the parameter-shift rule---a quantum native gradient evaluation technique that avoids classical discretization. Unlike generic loss functions that target abstract data patterns, our loss function focuses on the differential equation residual and reference data. This design ensures that the trained model not only approximates the data but also intrinsically satisfies the physical constraint expressed by the DE itself. Our method is validated on several differential equations, including highly oscillatory ones, demonstrating its capability to tackle challenging nonlinear dynamics. Results demonstrate that our quantum model successfully learns the solution, showing close agreement with a high-precision classical numerical benchmark.

</details>

**Authors:** Srinivasan Arunachalam, Arkopal Dutt, Hari Krovi, Rik Sengupta

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that low-depth quantum circuits possess computational capabilities fundamentally beyond those achievable by modern large language models (Transformers and DLMs). By showing separations in both the ability to sample certain distributions and the complexity of computing specific functions, it initiates a formal study of quantum advantage within the AI domain.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum advantage in the context of state-of-the-art AI models, providing theoretical benchmarks for when quantum computation might outperform classical deep learning methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish unconditional separations between low-depth quantum computation ($	ext{QNC}_0$) and classical Large Language Model (LLM) architectures like Transformers and Diffusion Language Models (DLMs).

**Main result.** The authors prove both distributional separation (a specific distribution sampleable by $	ext{QNC}_0$ but not by constant-round DLMs) and functional separation (a function computable by shallow quantum circuits requiring a prohibitively large width in any constant-depth transformer).

**Method.** The work employs complexity theory, constructing specific computational tasks (distribution sampling and function computation) to compare the inherent power of bounded-depth quantum gates versus the resource scaling (width $H_{dp}$) of modern LLMs.

**Model / system.** The models compared are low-depth quantum circuits ($	ext{QNC}_0$) and classical neural network architectures, specifically decoder-only Transformers and Diffusion Language Models. The analysis focuses on their computational capacity under shallow depth constraints.

**Key observables.** Distributional distance (for sampling), required width ($H_{dp}$) for functional computation, and circuit depth ($	ext{QNC}_0$ depth).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Constant depth, $\mathcal{O}(\log \log n)$ depth, input length $n$, and the resource metric $H_{dp}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The separations are based on specific complexity-theoretic tasks designed to highlight structural differences between quantum computation and LLM mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops arguments by first establishing foundational separations between $	ext{QNC}_0$ and classical circuits, then extending this comparison specifically to the operational constraints of modern DLMs (including revision/remasking steps) for both distributional and functional tasks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Modern large language models - transformers and diffusion language models - are built around two canonical algorithmic tasks: prediction and generation. We prove unconditional separations between low-depth quantum computation and the corresponding bounded-resource classical language-model architectures in both regimes. Concretely, we exhibit the following:   1. Distributional separation. We give a distribution that is sampleable by $\textsf{QNC}^0$ circuits (i.e., a family of constant-depth quantum circuits consisting of bounded fan-in gates) that no constant-round diffusion language model ($\textsf{DLM}$) with shallow scheduling and denoising can sample within constant distance, even when allowed sublinear chain-of-thought and output-token revision/remasking events, the very features modern $\textsf{DLM}$s rely on.   2. Functional separation. We exhibit a function computable in $\land \circ \textsf{QNC}^0[\log\log n]$ (i.e., a family of O$(\log\log n)$-depth $\textsf{QNC}^0$ circuits, where $n$ is the input length, followed by a single classical $\mathsf{AND}$ gate) such that any constant-depth decoder-only transformer computing the function must be large: it would have to have width $n^{Ω(1)}$.   Together, our work initiates the study of quantum advantage in the era of large language models.

</details>

### [Unweighted Gapped Clique Homology is $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-complete](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02726v1)

**Authors:** Ryu Hayakawa  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.02726v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

A weighted base complex is replaced by an unweighted expanded complex in which vertex weights are represented by block sizes. For simplicity, only the expanded vertices and incomplete edges are drawn. Symmetric averages over one-copy-per- block choices reconstruct the weighted Hodge metric. The illustration uses block sizes 6 and 3, exhibiting an integral ratio of 2.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.02726_figures/2608.02726_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: The qubit-register blow-up. A one-qubit bowtie has two harmonic cycles representing |0⟩and |1⟩. In the expanded register, each vertex is replaced by a complete graph and each bowtie edge by the corresponding complete bipartite connection. The n-qubit register is the join product of these one-qubit expanded graphs. Each register block has size M = ρ; the displayed copy counts are schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.02726_figures/2608.02726_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Figure 3: A schematic single-projector filling gadget. The boundary cycle is filled by an interior membrane, so the selected encoded cycle becomes null-homologous while the orthogonal cycles are preserved by the selectivity argument. The drawing suppresses the cut-and-reglue triangu- lation and clique-closure data used in that argument.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper proves that deciding the homology of an unweighted clique complex, given a spectral gap promise on its Laplacian, is $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-complete. It achieves this by showing that the complexity originates from the gap condition itself, not from vertex weights, using sophisticated blow-up constructions to map quantum Hamiltonian gaps onto topological invariants.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects deep concepts from algebraic topology (homology), spectral theory (Laplacians/gaps), and quantum complexity theory ($\mathsf{QMA}$), providing a powerful tool for classifying computational problems arising from physical structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the computational complexity of determining if a graph's clique complex has nontrivial homology, especially when spectral gap promises are involved.

**Main result.** It proves that Unweighted Gapped Clique Homology is $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-complete, demonstrating that the difficulty stems from the spectral gap promise rather than vertex weighting.

**Method.** The proof uses a 'blow-up' construction to transform weighted homology problems into unweighted ones, relying on detailed spectral analysis of the Hodge Laplacian.

**Model / system.** The model involves clique complexes derived from graphs ($Cl(G)$) and their associated combinatorial Hodge Laplacians ($\Delta_k$). The complexity is linked to quantum computation via Hamiltonian gap properties.

**Key observables.** Homology group $H_k(X; \mathbb{R})$ (non-trivial vs. trivial), minimum eigenvalue of the Laplacian $\lambda_{\min}(\Delta_k)$, and spectral gaps ($p(N)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vertex weights, gap size promise ($1/	ext{poly}(N)$), and the universal gate set $g_2$ for $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a source Hamiltonian whose spectral properties can be mapped to the homology gaps via the blow-up construction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 schematically illustrates a single-projector filling gadget, showing how an interior membrane nullifies a boundary cycle while preserving orthogonal cycles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing hardness for weighted complexes, then developing the 'blow-up' construction to eliminate weights, and finally proving both $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-hardness (via gap preservation) and containment in $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Deciding whether the clique complex of a given graph has nontrivial homology in a given dimension, under vertex-product weighting and an inverse-polynomial spectral gap promise on the combinatorial Hodge Laplacian, is known to be $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-hard and contained in $\mathsf{QMA}$ by King and Kohler (FOCS 2024). The vertex weights are essential in the known proof, where they provide the scale separation needed for the spectral gap analysis. We prove that, when every vertex has weight one, the problem remains $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$-hard and is contained in $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$, where $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$ is $\mathsf{QMA}_1$ with the universal gate set $g_2=\{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{CX},\mathsf{CCX},H\otimes H\}$. The construction replaces weight by expansion: each vertex of the weighted complex is blown up into a clique whose size encodes its weight. The block sizes are chosen so that symmetric averages reproduce the weighted Hodge metric. The symmetric sector therefore carries the weighted Laplacian up to a common scalar factor, while a local averaging argument gives a uniform lower bound on the orthogonal complement to the symmetric sector. Hence the weighted gap analysis transfers to an unweighted clique complex without introducing either additional low-energy states or spurious homology. Containment in $\mathsf{QMA}_1^{g_2}$ follows from Rudolph's exact linear combination of unitaries simulation of sparse integer clique Laplacians. The result shows that the gap promise, rather than vertex weighting, is the source of the complexity of gapped clique homology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes the topological phases of a triangular Hofstadter model used to simulate twisted bilayer TMDs. By studying how staggered flux and dimerization break symmetries, the authors map out complex phase diagrams characterized by quantized Chern numbers. The findings provide testable predictions for both solid-state materials and controllable cold atom systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the connection to moiré superlattices and tunable parameters (like electric displacement field) makes it highly relevant for AMO physicists working on engineered quantum systems using optical lattices or photonic crystals.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the topological properties and phase diagram of the triangular Hofstadter model incorporating staggered flux.

**Main result.** The system exhibits rich topological phases dependent on whether the magnetic flux parameter q is even or odd, with distinct Chern number characteristics in different gapped regimes.

**Method.** The analysis involves mapping the Hamiltonian to momentum space and calculating topological invariants (like the Chern number) by analyzing the energy spectrum's dependence on external fluxes and dimerization strength.

**Model / system.** The model is the triangular Hofstadter lattice with staggered flux, relevant for describing twisted bilayer transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs). The Hamiltonian incorporates both a uniform magnetic flux ($\Phi_B = 2\pi p/q$) and an additional staggered flux term.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($C$), Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), longitudinal conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}$), and the energy spectrum $E(\mathbf{k})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rational external magnetic flux $\Phi_B = 2\pi p/q$, staggered flux parameter $\phi$, and dimerization strength ($\delta J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a tight-binding description for the low energy physics, and specific symmetries (like P symmetry) are analyzed at certain flux values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Hofstadter spectrum for various fluxes and depict the phase diagram behavior based on the rational flux ratio $p/q$ and dimerization strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the Hamiltonian, then analyzes the effect of staggered flux symmetry. It proceeds to study the dimerized model's phase diagram separately for even and odd values of q, concluding with implications for experimental realization in TMDs or cold atoms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the topological properties and phase diagram of the triangular Hofstadter model with staggered flux in adjacent triangles in this work. This lattice can be used to describe the low energy physics of the twisted bilayer transition metal dichalcogenides (TMD) in a certain range of the electric displacement field between the two layers. We show that the Hofstadter spectrum of this model is generally asymmetric except at specific staggered flux $3φ= π/2 \mod π$ due to an additional P symmetry at such $φ$. Breaking the translation symmetry by dimerization lifts the P symmetry and results in rich topological phases in the system. The dimerized model with different rational external magnetic flux $Φ_B = 2πp/q$ has phase diagram with the following common features. For even q, the dimerized model generally has three gapped regimes. The one with small dimerization has finite Chern number and the other two have zero Chern number. For odd q, the model is gapped with zero Chern number at any finite dimerization. For both q even and odd, the two regimes with zero Chern number can be further characterized by the inversion symmetry of the parametrized one-dimensional chains of the system at φ= 0 \mod π/3$, and one regime is topologically non-trivial and the other is trivial. Our results may be tested in twisted bilayer TMD with weak interaction or cold atom systems in optical lattice or photonic crystals achieved in recent experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to understand how the energy structure and polarization of Fermi polarons are controlled in 2D semiconductor monolayers using combined magnetic fields and mechanical strain. By calculating optical spectra via Green's functions, it demonstrates that these external parameters offer powerful, complementary tools for manipulating many-body excitonic quasiparticles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it treats open quantum systems dynamics (excitons coupled to a Fermi sea) under external fields, providing detailed predictions for magneto-optical responses that can be probed experimentally.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The theory develops the fine structure and polarization of Fermi polarons in doped two-dimensional semiconductors under the combined influence of external magnetic fields and uniaxial strain.

**Main result.** The study shows that controlling strain, magnetic field, and electron density allows continuous tuning of energy splitting and eigenstate polarization. Real and strain-induced pseudomagnetic fields provide complementary tools for controlling optical response.

**Method.** The research employs the Green's function approach to calculate energy levels and Stokes parameters, supplemented by a simplified effective two-level model for analytical study.

**Model / system.** The system involves excitons interacting with a Fermi sea of resident charge carriers in TMDC monolayers. The Hamiltonian incorporates terms for external magnetic fields (Zeeman shifts) and strain ($\hat{H}_s$).

**Key observables.** Energy levels, Stokes parameters, absorption/reflection spectra, circular and linear dichroism, and the polaron g factor renormalization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field ($B$), uniaxial strain, electron density, and critical magnetic fields leading to full valley polarization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Orbital effects of the magnetic field are neglected due to a small scattering time ($	au_e$). The analysis assumes $\mathbf{B}$ does not affect interaction parameters $V_1$ and $V_2$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the intervalley Fermi polaron in W-based TMDC monolayers, showing Zeeman shifts both without and with an external magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The theory builds from the non-interacting Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_0$), incorporates magnetic field effects ($\hat{H}_B$) via the full Hamiltonian $\hat{H}$, and finally analyzes optical properties using Green's functions, culminating in spectral calculations for various regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The theory of attractive Fermi polaron energy spectrum fine structure and polarization in doped two-dimensional semiconductors in external magnetic field is developed. Fermi polaron $g$ factor renormalization due to correlations with the valley-polarized Fermi sea of resident charge carriers is calculated. We study the competition between Zeeman and strain-induced splittings in monolayers under uniaxial strain. The control of strain, magnetic field and electron density allows continuous tuning of energy splitting and eigenstate polarization. Linearly polarized strain-induced split doublet exhibits quadratic Zeeman shift changing to linear Zeeman splitting with elliptical polarization with the increase of magnetic field. We identify a critical magnetic field above which resident charge carriers become fully valley polarized and only one circularly polarized Fermi polaron state remains. Within the Green's function approach we calculate energy levels and Stokes parameters of attractive Fermi polaron states and introduce a simplified effective two-level model allowing us to study analytically the interplay of Zeeman and pseudo-Zeeman splitting. We calculate absorption and reflection spectra, including circular and linear dichroism in the trion spectral range. These results show that real and strain-induced pseudomagnetic fields provide complementary tools for controlling the optical response of many-body excitonic quasiparticles in two-dimensional semiconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents the first demonstration of spatiotemporal chaos arising from multiple colliding pulse sequences in nonlinear waveguide arrays. By deriving generalized Lotka-Volterra models via perturbation theory applied to coupled-NLS equations, the authors show that energy dynamics exhibit chaotic behavior. The key finding is the remarkable agreement between these simplified LV models and complex numerical simulations, even when strong pattern distortions occur.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear wave physics (NLS) with classical dynamical systems (LV models), providing a robust theoretical framework for understanding complex energy dissipation in optical systems, which has implications for quantum optics and nonlinear signal processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study demonstrates the transition to spatiotemporal chaos when multiple colliding pulse sequences propagate in nonlinear waveguide arrays.

**Main result.** Excellent agreement is found between generalized Lotka-Volterra (LV) models and coupled-NLS simulations for energy dynamics, confirming chaotic behavior even under strong distortions.

**Method.** The authors used perturbation theory to derive LV models from the perturbed cubic Nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS), followed by extensive numerical simulations.

**Model / system.** The system involves multiple sequences of optical pulses propagating in nonlinear waveguide arrays governed by perturbed coupled-NLS equations with cubic gain/loss. The dynamics are modeled using generalized Lotka-Volterra systems for energy evolution.

**Key observables.** Pulse energy dynamics ($U_j(z)$) and the spatial pattern evolution ($\vert\psi_j(t, z)\vert$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameter $m$ is varied to explore different regimes; the cubic nonlinearity coefficient $\epsilon_3$ governs dissipation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial derivations relied on weak perturbation assumptions and periodicity of soliton sequences, which were later overcome by introducing a shifting transformation ($U' = U+a$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate pulse patterns ($\vert\psi_j(t, z)\vert$) for both unshifted and shifted models in chaotic regimes, alongside plots comparing energy evolution from coupled-NLS simulations against LV model predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing single collision effects using perturbation theory to derive LV models, then applying these models to multisequence systems, and finally confirming the transition to chaos through numerical comparison between the full coupled-NLS equations and the derived LV dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the first demonstration of transition to spatiotemporal chaos with multiple colliding pulse sequences in systems described by perturbed cubic nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equations. For this purpose, we consider propagation of multiple sequences of optical pulses in two distinct types of nonlinear waveguide arrays with cubic gain and loss. By employing a perturbation theory for NLS solitons, we show that the dynamics of pulse energies in the waveguide array systems is described by generalized Lotka-Volterra (LV) models, which exhibit dissipative chaos in a wide region in parameter space. We test the LV models' predictions for chaotic dynamics of pulse energies by extensive numerical simulations with perturbed systems of coupled-NLS equations. We find excellent agreement between the results of the LV and coupled-NLS models for energy dynamics in both types of waveguide array systems, despite the strong pulse pattern distortions and the strongly nonlinear nature of the dynamics.

</details>
